## 0.1 Pre-pregnancy counselling

**Immunisation**: rubella.

**Smoking cessation**
- ↓ovulation, abnormal sperm production
- 2x miscarriage risk
- Associated with preterm labour, fetal growth restriction, placenta praevia, abruption

**Weight**: aim BMI 18.5–<30; avoid contact sports.

**Supplementation — Folic acid**
- Essential for DNA/RNA synthesis
- Causes of deficiency: phenytoin, methotrexate, pregnancy, alcohol excess
- Consequences of deficiency: megaloblastic anaemia, neural tube defects

> [!info] Folic acid dosing
> - **0.4 mg/day** from >1 month preconception until ≥13 weeks (standard)
> - **5 mg/day** if: past neural tube defect, on antiepileptics, diabetic, obese, HIV+ve on co-trimoxazole prophylaxis, or sickle cell disease

**Supplementation — Vitamin D**: 10 mcg/day for all women, especially those with darker or covered skin.

**Alcohol**
- High levels → fetal alcohol syndrome, ↑miscarriage risk
- Minimal drinking (1–2 units/week) not shown to adversely affect fetus, but crosses placenta and may affect fetal brain

> [!info] Verified against the current NHMRC Australian Guidelines to Reduce Health Risks from Drinking Alcohol — **the Australian position is genuinely stricter than the UK's, not just differently worded**: NHMRC advises that women who are pregnant or planning a pregnancy should **not drink alcohol at all**, stating explicitly that no safe level of alcohol consumption during pregnancy has been identified — there is no Australian equivalent of the UK's "<1 unit/24h is low risk" framing, and the file's original UK threshold should not be used to reassure a patient that a small amount is fine. Even one or two drinks a week is described as potentially harmful to the developing fetus. This complete-abstinence message is a genuinely important, unambiguous point to convey clearly, not hedge.

**Recreational drugs**: associated with miscarriage, preterm birth, poor fetal development, intrauterine death.

**Pre-existing medical disorders**
- Poorly controlled → same or worse in pregnancy
- Well controlled → same or better
- Refer for specialist help ASAP

> [!danger] Medication changes prior to conception
> Change to reduce teratogenesis risk: **antiepileptics, ACE inhibitors, immune-modulators**. Avoid OTC medications as much as possible.

**Miscarriage**: spontaneous miscarriage occurs in 15–20% of all pregnancies (↑ with extremes of maternal age). Recurrent miscarriage = ≥3 consecutive miscarriages.

---

## 0.2 Physiological changes in pregnancy

**Hormonal**
- Progesterone: ↓muscle excitability, ↑body temperature
- Oestrogens (90% oestriol): ↑breast/nipple growth, water retention, protein synthesis
- Thyroid: ↑ in size
- Prolactin: ↑ throughout pregnancy
- Cortisol: ↑ output, but unbound levels remain constant

**Genital**
- Uterus: 100 g → 1.1 kg (hypertrophy ≤20w, then stretching)
- Vaginal discharge ↑ due to cervical ectopy, cell desquamation, ↑mucus production from vasocongested vagina

**Haemodynamic**

| System | Change |
|---|---|
| Plasma volume | Rises from 8–32w (50% above non-pregnant) |
| RBC | ↑30% if supplements taken; Hb falls due to dilution |
| WCC, platelets, ESR, cholesterol, β-globulin, fibrinogen | ↑ |
| Albumin, GGT | ↓ |
| Urea, creatinine | ↓ |
| Cardiac output | ↑ (SV ↑10%, HR ↑~15/min) |
| Peripheral resistance | ↓ |
| BP (esp. DBP) | ↓ in 2nd trimester, rises to non-pregnant levels by term |

- Varicose veins may form
- IVC compression from 20w → ↓CO by 30–40% (supine hypotension) → relieve with left lateral position or ~15° left tilt

**Other systems**
- Ventilation ↑40% (progesterone-mediated), O₂ uptake ↑20% only → SOB common
- Gut motility ↓ → constipation, delayed gastric emptying, lax oesophageal sphincter, heartburn
- Renal size ↑1cm length, GFR ↑60%, ↑pressure on bladder → ↑micturition
- Skin: linea nigra, nipple pigmentation, chloasma, palmar erythema, spider naevi, striae
- Hair shedding in puerperium
- Pregnancy tests may be +ve from 9 days post-conception; false +ve rate low

---

## 0.3 Antenatal care

### 0.3.1 1st antenatal (booking) visit

**History**
- FHx: DM, HTN, fetal abnormality, inheritable disease, twins
- Concurrent illness, FGM, VTE risk (± LMWH)
- GDM risk assessment → 75 g OGTT at 28w if BMI >30, previous macrosomia, 1st-degree relative with DM, or ethnic risk factors
  - If previous GDM: screen at 16w **and** again at 28w
- Past mental illness: if serious (schizophrenia, bipolar, self-harm) or past postnatal depression → antenatal assessment by perinatal mental health team
- Women born outside UK: ↑Hb problems, blood-borne viruses, pre-existing cardiac disease
- Ask about domestic violence and substance abuse in unsupported women

**Examination**: heart, lungs, BP, weight & BMI, abdomen, varicose veins, ± pap smear, FGM check.

**Tests**: Hb, blood group, infectious disease screen, sickle cell/thalassaemia screen; offer chromosomal/structural abnormality screening; 12w scan confirms EDD.

**Advice**: seatbelt use (above/below bump), antenatal classes, maternity benefits information.

> [!info] Verified — flight timing restrictions (commonly up to 36 weeks for a singleton pregnancy, earlier for multiples) and "fit to fly" letter requirements are generally set by individual airlines rather than being a UK-specific national rule, so this guidance is broadly applicable in Australia too — check the specific airline's policy for the current threshold given these vary between carriers and are reviewed periodically. Malaria-endemic area avoidance and the general travel precautions remain valid regardless of jurisdiction.

### 0.3.2 Later visits
- Discuss screening results, treat anaemia and UTI
- At each visit: check protein, BP, fundal height

> [!info] NICE visit schedule — **UK schedule, retained for reference only.** Australian antenatal visit scheduling follows RANZCOG and state maternity guidance and differs; **not verified against an Australian source** (see `PENDING_GUIDELINE_CHECKS.md` B32).
> Visits at <12, 16, 25, 28, 31, 34, 36, 38, 40, 41 weeks (25/31/40/41 only if primip). Verify against Australian shared antenatal care schedules.

- 28w: Hb, Rh antibodies — give anti-D if needed
- Most screening occurs in low-risk uncomplicated pregnancies
- Higher risk women: previously affected child, pre-existing DM, epilepsy

> [!info] Verified — that all antenatal screening is offered rather than mandatory, and can be declined by the pregnant person, is a universal principle of informed consent rather than a UK-specific policy, and applies identically in Australia — this doesn't need correcting for jurisdiction.

---

## 0.4 Structural abnormality screening

**Ultrasound**: detects pregnancy from ~5w gestation; used to date pregnancy.

**Nuchal translucency (NT) — 11–13w**
- Determines viability, dates pregnancy, diagnoses multiple pregnancy and chorionicity
- Screens for chromosomal abnormality via nuchal fold measurement + bloods
- ↑fetal NT may reflect fetal heart failure or serious cardiac/great vessel abnormality
- If bloods + NT give risk <1:150, invasive testing is offered

**Anomaly scan**
- Detailed US at 18–22w to detect structural malformations
- ~30 min; sensitivity varies

> [!info] Lethal anomalies detectable on anomaly scan
> Anencephaly, bilateral renal agenesis, some major cardiac abnormalities, trisomies 13 and 18

**Fetal echocardiography**: offered to those at high risk of fetal cardiac abnormality — FHx/personal Hx, NT >3.5mm, drugs in pregnancy (lithium), DM, monochorionic twins.

**Other scans**: soft markers (slightly more common in chromosomally abnormal fetuses), fetal growth scans, Doppler US (blood flow, useful in high-risk pregnancies).

### 0.4.1 Work and lifestyle
- Usually safe to continue working unless occupational hazards
- Air travel: avoid after 37w (singletons) / 32w (multiples); associated with ↑VTE risk — wear compression stockings

**Supplementation**
- PO iron: only if anaemic (<105 g/L in 1st trimester or <115 g/L thereafter)
- Vitamin A: high doses (>700 mcg) are teratogenic — avoid eating liver

**Diet advice**: avoid liver and vitamin A; limit caffeine to 200 mg/day (~2 cups tea/coffee); cook meat thoroughly; avoid soft/unpasteurised cheese, shellfish, raw fish.

---

## 0.5 Aneuploidy & screening

### 0.5.1 Trisomy 21 (Down syndrome)
- 10% die before age 5; life expectancy ~55y
- ↑rate of spontaneous miscarriage
- ↑maternal age → ↑risk (age <25: 1:1500 vs age 45: 1:30)
- Congenital cardiac malformations common (esp. VSD/ASD), duodenal atresia

### 0.5.2 Trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome)
- Most die soon after birth; survival >1y rare
- Small chin, low-set ears, rocker-bottom feet, VSD

### 0.5.3 Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome)
- Most die soon after birth
- Microcephaly, holoprosencephaly, exomphalos, cleft lip/palate

### 0.5.4 Screening with combined test
- NT + free hCG + PAPP-A + maternal age
- Performed 11w to 13+6w
- Detects 90% of aneuploidies
- Results: "lower" or "higher chance" — higher chance (>1:150) → counselling

### 0.5.5 Screening with quadruple test
- Used if booking later (15–20w)
- AFP, unconjugated oestriol (UcO), hCG, inhibin A

| Condition | AFP | UcO | hCG | Inhibin A |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Down syndrome | ↑ | ↓ | ↑ | ↑ |
| Edwards/Patau | ↓ | ↓ | ↓ | ~ |
| Neural tube defects | ↑ | ~ | ~ | ~ |

### 0.5.6 Non-invasive prenatal testing (NIPT)
- Analyses cell-free fetal DNA fragments in maternal blood
- >99% sensitivity/specificity for chromosomal abnormalities, especially Down syndrome
- Private companies offer from 10w gestation

---

## 0.6 Invasive testing

### 0.6.1 Chorionic villus sampling
- Performed 10–13w; allows earlier termination than amniocentesis
- Placental sampling via transabdominal or transcervical approach
- Risks: ↑miscarriage by 1–2%, ↑BBV transmission, maternal cell contamination, false +ve/-ve
- Not recommended for dichorionic multiple pregnancy

### 0.6.2 Amniocentesis
- Performed from 16w onwards
- Aspiration of fluid containing shed fetal skin/gut cells via transabdominal needle
- Fetal loss rate ~1% at 16w
- Anti-D needed in all Rh-negative women
- Can diagnose fetal infections (e.g. CMV); reduces excess miscarriage rate compared to CVS

---

## 0.7 Use of anti-D

> [!info] Verified against the National Blood Authority (NBA) Australia's "Guideline for the prophylactic use of Rh D immunoglobulin in pregnancy care" (2024), Aug 2026 — **the dose amounts below needed a genuine correction, not just a units-terminology swap**: Australian dosing is **250 IU for first-trimester events (before 13 weeks)** and **625 IU for events from 13 weeks onward** — the file's original "500 units if ≥20w" understates the correct Australian second/third-trimester dose. The routine antenatal prophylaxis timing (28 and 34 weeks) is genuinely consistent with Australian practice — that part didn't need correcting. Where a larger-volume fetomaternal haemorrhage (FMH) is identified (≥6mL fetal red cells) after 20 weeks, additional dosing is calculated as 100 IU per mL of fetal red cells beyond this threshold, guided by specialist input — a more precise, FMH-volume-based top-up principle than a single flat dose, worth knowing given the original note doesn't capture this. Two RhD immunoglobulin products are available in Australia (CSL Rh(D) Immunoglobulin-VF as 250 IU/625 IU vials, and Rhophylac 1500 IU) — the higher-volume Rhophylac formulation is used where a larger dose is required intravenously.

**Dosing**
- 250 IU for sensitising events before 13 weeks' gestation
- 625 IU for sensitising events from 13 weeks' gestation onward, and for routine antenatal prophylaxis at 28 and 34 weeks
- Give IM in deltoid (IV or SC if bleeding disorder)
- From 20w, perform Kleihauer test (measures bleed volume via maternal FBC) — do **not** give anti-D if already sensitised

**Postnatal use**: 500 units standard dose.
- Give to all Rh-negative women where baby's blood group cannot be determined, or is unknown 72h post-delivery
- Do Kleihauer test on all eligible for anti-D
- Any mother receiving antenatal anti-D should also receive it postnatally, unless she delivers an Rh-negative baby

**Anti-D in miscarriage (Rh-negative mothers)**
- Give to all having surgical or medical terminations, evacuation of hydatidiform mole, and ectopic pregnancies (unless known anti-D antibodies)
- Give where spontaneous miscarriage requires termination
- Give where spontaneous **complete** miscarriage occurs after 12w
- Threatened miscarriage ≥12w: give if bleeding continues intermittently, then q6w until delivery
- Routine anti-D **not** recommended for threatened miscarriage before 12w

**Anti-D in pregnancy (Rh-negative mothers)**
- Blood sample before 28w to check for antibodies
- **Give 625 IU at 28w and 34w** for routine antenatal prophylaxis
- For a sensitising event: **250 IU before 13 weeks; 625 IU from 13 weeks onward** — from 20w, quantify the fetomaternal haemorrhage with a Kleihauer test, as a large bleed needs additional dosing

> [!danger] **Corrected 2026-08-29 — this block contradicted the verified Dosing block ~18 lines above it, inside the same entry.** It read **"give 500 units at 28w and 34w"** and **"250 units before 20w, 500 units after 20w"** — the second of which is the *exact* figure the verification box at the top of this entry says "understates the correct Australian second/third-trimester dose". **The box announced the correction; the correction reached only the Dosing block and not this one.**
>
> The gestational boundary also differed: this block used **20 weeks**, the NBA-verified figures use **13 weeks**. A reader working from here gave 500 units where the Australian dose is 625 IU, and used the wrong threshold to choose between doses.
>
> **Seventh instance of the partial-verification-box pattern, and a new variant: the un-propagated correction happening *inside the entry the correction was made in*.** Every previous instance was a box silent about content beneath it; this is a box that spoke, and was not listened to eighteen lines later. See `PENDING_GUIDELINE_CHECKS.md` **B61**, which also flags the postnatal dose.

## 0.8 Antenatal care timetable

> [!info] Verified against RANZCOG's "Routine antenatal assessment in the absence of pregnancy complications" statement and the Australian Government's Pregnancy Care Guidelines, Aug 2026 — **Australia does not use the UK's rigid "10 visits primip / 7 visits multip" NICE framing**; RANZCOG and the Australian Pregnancy Care Guidelines instead recommend an **individualised visit schedule** based on the woman's specific risk profile, explicitly citing evidence that a reduced-visit schedule (as few as 5 visits) does not worsen maternal/perinatal outcomes and is more cost-effective than a higher fixed count — so the specific "10 vs 7" numbers below should not be treated as the Australian standard. What **is** consistent with Australian practice: the first antenatal visit should occur by around 10 weeks' gestation, and the general content/purpose of each visit stage below (booking bloods, anomaly screening, growth/BP/urine monitoring) broadly matches Australian first-visit content (FBC, blood group and antibody screen, rubella/syphilis/HIV/hepatitis serology, MSU, cervical screening where due). Two genuinely useful Australian-specific additions: **influenza and pertussis vaccination are recommended at 20–32 weeks** (not a UK-specific detail, but worth stating explicitly as it's a core part of the Australian schedule), and sleeping on the side is recommended from 28 weeks given the association between supine sleep position and stillbirth risk in later pregnancy.

**NICE recommendations**
- 10 visits in 1st pregnancy if uncomplicated
- 7 visits in subsequent pregnancies if uncomplicated
- No need to be seen by consultant if uncomplicated

| Gestation | Content |
|---|---|
| 8–12w (ideally <10w) | Booking visit; booking bloods + urine culture (asymptomatic bacteriuria) |
| 10–13+6w | Early scan to confirm dates, exclude multiple pregnancy |
| 11–13+6w | Down's syndrome screening incl. nuchal scan |
| 16w | Discuss anomaly/blood results; if Hb <110g/L, consider iron; BP + urine dipstick |
| 18–20+6w | Anomaly scan |
| 25w (primip) | BP, urine dipstick, symphysis-fundal height |
| 28w | 2nd anaemia/atypical red cell antibody screen (iron if Hb <105g/L); 1st anti-D dose to Rh-ve women |
| 31w (primip) | Routine care |
| 34w | 2nd anti-D dose; info on labour/birth plan |
| 36w | Check presentation, offer ECV if indicated; breastfeeding/vitamin K/baby blues info |
| 38w | Routine care |
| 40w (primip) | Discuss options for prolonged pregnancy |
| 41w | As per 40w visit |

**Offer screening for**: anaemia, bacteriuria, blood group/Rhesus/anti-RBC antibodies, Down syndrome, fetal anomalies, hepatitis B, HIV, neural tube defects, pre-eclampsia risk factors, syphilis.

**Screen depending on history**: placenta praevia, psychiatric illness, sickle cell disease, Tay-Sachs disease, thalassaemia.

**Do NOT offer routinely**: bacterial vaginosis, chlamydia, CMV, fragile X, hepatitis C, toxoplasmosis.

> [!danger] **Group B Strep was in the "do not offer routinely" list above and has been removed — that is the UK position, not the Australian one.**
>
> Australian guidance (RANZCOG and the Australian Government Pregnancy Care Guidelines) **explicitly permits either** universal culture-based screening at 35–37 weeks **or** a risk-factor-based approach, with the choice left to the individual maternity service. Listing GBS as "not routinely offered" states one of the two permitted strategies as though it were the national position, and it is the strategy that misses the colonised woman with no risk factors.
>
> **The same defect was corrected in [[08_01-03_Infectious_Disease_-_Bacterial_Infections]] Group B Strep (*Streptococcus agalactiae*) earlier this session and did not propagate here** — found by the dedicated GBS consistency check run at Phase 4 close (2026-08-29), which existed precisely because two independent GBS defects had already appeared in two different files. That entry owns the screening-strategy question; [[16_06-07_Ante-Perinatal_Infections]] Group B Streptococcus (GBS) owns the intrapartum antibiotic regimen.

## 0.9 Minor symptoms in pregnancy

| Symptom | Mechanism | Management |
|---|---|---|
| Headache, palpitations, fainting | Dilated peripheral circulation, ↑sweating | ↑fluid intake, stand slowly |
| Urinary frequency | Fetal head pressure on bladder, ↑GFR | Exclude UTI |
| Breathlessness | Physiological | Rule out VTE |
| Constipation | ↓gut motility | ↑fluids, high-fibre diet; avoid stimulant laxatives |
| GORD | Progesterone-mediated pyloric relaxation + fetal pressure | Avoid triggers, small meals, antacids/H2RA/PPI, prop up with pillows |
| Symphysis pubis dysfunction | Pelvic ligament/muscle relaxation | Analgesia, physiotherapy |
| Carpal tunnel syndrome | Fluid retention | Wrist splints |
| Itch/itchy rash (up to 25%) | Common pruritic eruption of pregnancy | Emollients, weak topical steroids; delivery is curative |
| Ankle oedema | — | Measure BP, urine protein, check for DVT; rest, leg elevation |
| Leg cramps (33%) | — | ↑fluids, bananas, tonic water; can be difficult to treat |
| Chloasma | Pigmentation | — |
| Nausea (80%) / vomiting (50%) | Can start at 4w, ↓over following weeks | — |

> [!info] Verified — pemphigoid gestationis and its vesicular presentation, cardiac/heat-loss risk, and recurrence pattern are universal clinical facts, not UK-specific guidance; this doesn't need jurisdiction correction.

## 0.10 Hyperemesis gravidarum

**D**: persistent N/V in pregnancy causing weight loss >5%, dehydration, and electrolyte imbalance.

**R**: ↑hCG (multiple pregnancy, trophoblastic disease), nulliparity, obesity, FHx/PMH of HG. Smoking **↓**risk of HG.

**Criteria for diagnosis**
- ≥5% pre-pregnancy weight loss
- Dehydration
- Electrolyte imbalance

> [!info] PUQE score
> Pregnancy-Unique Quantification of Emesis (PUQE) score used to classify severity of HG.

**S/smx for admission/referral**
- Continued N/V, unable to keep down liquids or oral antiemetics
- Continued N/V with ketonuria and/or weight loss >5% despite oral antiemetics
- Confirmed/suspected comorbidity (e.g. unable to tolerate oral abx for a UTI)

**Mx**
- Simple measures: rest, avoid triggers, bland foods, ginger, wrist acupuncture

> [!info] Verified against Australian Prescriber's "Treatment of nausea and vomiting in pregnancy" and RANZCOG prescribing survey data, Aug 2026 — **two genuinely important, non-obvious Australian-specific points, not just drug-name swaps**: first, the fixed-dose doxylamine/pyridoxine *combination* product (Debendox) was actually **voluntarily withdrawn in Australia in 1983** after birth defect claims (later shown unfounded) — the opposite of the original note's framing, which implied it was an available Australian option "not available in UK." Doxylamine and pyridoxine remain usable in Australia, just as **separate products** (doxylamine as Restavit; pyridoxine separately) rather than a single combination tablet. Second, **real-world Australian prescribing practice diverges from most international guidelines**: RANZCOG survey data shows Australian clinicians overwhelmingly favour **metoclopramide as first-line** for both morning sickness and hyperemesis (70–89% of respondents), with ondansetron commonly used second — a genuinely different practice pattern from UK/US/Canadian guidelines, which place metoclopramide later in the sequence. Ondansetron's small first-trimester cleft lip/palate signal, and the >5-day extrapyramidal-risk caution for metoclopramide/domperidone, both remain accurate and relevant in the Australian context.
> - 1st line (per actual Australian prescribing patterns): metoclopramide, or pyridoxine ± separately-dosed doxylamine (not as a combination product); prochlorperazine or chlorpromazine also used
> - 2nd line: ondansetron (1st trimester use associated with a small increased risk of cleft lip/palate) — commonly used in Australian practice for hyperemesis despite not being formally first-line in most guidelines; metoclopramide or domperidone (**do not use >5 days** — extrapyramidal side effects)

**Ix**: urine dip for ketones/UTI; FBC, U&Es, LFTs; if refractory — TFTs, LFTs, calcium, phosphate, amylase (r/o pancreatitis), ABG; US to diagnose multiple pregnancy and rule out trophoblastic disease.

**Admission Mx**
- IVF: normal saline + K or Hartmann's for rehydration

> [!danger] Do NOT give glucose — can precipitate Wernicke's encephalopathy.

- If vomiting intractable, consider steroids (prednisolone 40–50mg OD or hydrocortisone 100mg/12h IV)
- Prescribe high-dose folic acid 5mg/day, thiamine to prevent Wernicke's
- In hospital: enoxaparin 40mg OD + anti-VTE stockings (dehydration ↑VTE risk)

**Complications**: AKI, Wernicke's encephalopathy, oesophagitis/Mallory-Weiss tear, VTE. Fetal: small ↑ in preterm birth and low birthweight.

## 0.11 Thromboprophylaxis & VTE

*See also →01.07 for general VTE.*

**E**: 0.1–0.2% of all pregnancies. DVT 3x more common than PE. Untreated DVT → PE in 16%. DVT more common postnatally.

**General advice**: avoid immobility and dehydration. Risk ↑ from early 1st trimester to 6w postpartum.

### 0.11.1 Risk stratification

> [!info] VTE risk factors
> **High risk → antenatal LMWH**
> - Hx of ≥2 VTE
> - Unprovoked or oestrogen-related VTE
> - Single provoked VTE + thrombophilia or FHx
> - Antithrombin III deficiency (30% risk)
>
> **Intermediate risk → consider LMWH**
> - Thrombophilia but no VTE
> - Single provoked VTE
> - Medical comorbidities (cancer, inflammatory conditions, significant cardiac/respiratory disease, SLE, sickle cell disease, nephrotic syndrome, IVDU, any antenatal surgery)
>
> **Other risk factors (if ≥3, consider LMWH)**
> - Age >35, obesity, parity ≥3, smoker, large varicose veins, current infection, pre-eclampsia, immobility, dehydration, multiple pregnancy, assisted reproduction
>
> **Postpartum-specific risk factors**
> - Mid-cavity or rotational instrumental delivery
> - PPH + blood transfusion

**Indications**: as per risk factors above. If antenatal LMWH given, continue until 6w postpartum. Emergency LSCS: 7 days postpartum.

> [!info] Verified against the Queensland Clinical Guideline "VTE prophylaxis in pregnancy and the puerperium" (2025), Aug 2026 — **the weight-banded enoxaparin doses below already match current Australian standard prophylactic dosing exactly** (40mg for 50–90kg, 60mg for 91–130kg, 80mg for 131–170kg) — no correction needed to these bands; Australian guidance additionally specifies 20mg daily for <50kg and 0.5mg/kg/day for ≥171kg, extending the range at both ends. **The neuraxial timing needed more precision than a single "4h" figure**: the correct Australian intervals are **12 hours from the last prophylactic LMWH dose to neuraxial block**, **6 hours from neuraxial block back to the next LMWH dose**, and **4 hours from epidural catheter removal to the next dose** — three genuinely different intervals depending on the specific step, not one blanket figure.
> - Enoxaparin: 20mg SC OD if <50kg; 40mg if 50–90kg; 60mg if 91–130kg; 80mg if 131–170kg; 0.5mg/kg if ≥171kg
> - 12h from last LMWH dose to neuraxial block; 6h from neuraxial block to next dose; 4h from catheter removal to next dose
> - **Do not give if PPH ongoing**
> - Otherwise can give immediately

**Ix**: FBC, U&Es, LFTs, clotting. If PE suspected: ABG, ECG, CXR. D-dimer only useful if negative (to rule out) — don't routinely order.

**Imaging**
- DVT: compression or duplex US of deep veins
- High clinical suspicion: continue treatment dose LMWH + repeat imaging in 1w
- PE: CXR → (if normal) US of deep veins → if still negative, V/Q scan ± spiral CT/MRI

**Mx — start ASAP if suspected**

> [!danger] Massive PE
> Immediate senior help. Thrombolysis or percutaneous catheter thrombus fragmentation. Consider UFH. Take blood for APTT 6h post-loading dose; aim for 1.5–2.5x lab control value.

- Generally: LMWH (BD dosing per local formulary), refer to haematology for follow-up, consider switching to warfarin post-delivery. Continue for ≥6w postpartum.

**During labour**
- Stop LMWH; keep well hydrated
- Avoid regional anaesthesia until ≥12h after last prophylactic LMWH dose, or ≥24h after treatment dose
- Wait >4h until epidural catheter removed before next dose
- Do not remove catheter until >12h after last dose
- High risk patients stopping anticoagulation: consider UFH ± IVC filter

## 0.12 Pre-existing problems in pregnancy

### 0.12.1 Anaemia in pregnancy

> [!warning] FLAG 2026-09-01 — `§0.12.1`–`§0.12.13` are thirteen one-paragraph versions of diseases owned elsewhere
> Anaemia · diabetes · hypertension · hyperthyroidism · jaundice · malaria · renal disease · psychiatric
> drugs · epilepsy · connective tissue disease · HIV · rubella · measles.
> `O2 §0.6 Medical Problems and Infection in Pregnancy` is the Corpus B version of the same list.
> ⚠️ **DO NOT DISPERSE THESE. The pregnancy-specific modification is the content** — the whole point is
> what changes in pregnancy. Each system file also carries its own "in pregnancy" note
> (`Endocrine I1 §0.6`, `I2 §0.7`, `Cardio B5 §0.3`, `Neuro D6 §0.3` on valproate).
> Flagged as a set for cross-checking, not for splitting.

- Defined as Hb <105 g/L
- Antenatal screening at booking + 28w

> [!info] Verified against the Australian Government Pregnancy Care Guideline — **the thresholds below already match current Australian practice exactly**: Hb <110g/L before 20 weeks, <105g/L at or after 20 weeks — no correction needed. (The postpartum <100g/L threshold reflects a separate, additional convention some sources use and remains reasonable, though it's worth noting the core Australian antenatal guideline defines the antenatal thresholds specifically by the 20-week mark rather than by trimester as originally framed here.)
> - Before 20 weeks: <110 g/L
> - At or after 20 weeks: <105 g/L
> - Postpartum: <100 g/L

- Offer PO ferrous sulfate 200mg BD with orange juice; if intolerant, alternate days or 2x/week. Avoid with tea, calcium, metal-containing supplements. Continue for 3 months after correction to replenish stores.
- Thalassaemia: refer to specialists
- Sickle cell: aim for diagnosis at birth (cord blood) for penicillin pneumococcal prophylaxis

### 0.12.2 Diabetes in pregnancy

DM complicates 5% of pregnancies: 87.5% GDM, 7.5% T1DM, 5% T2DM.

**R**: BMI >30, previous macrosomia (≥4.5kg), previous GDM, 1st-degree FHx, ethnicity.

**Screening (OGTT)**
- Previous GDM: OGTT ASAP after booking, and at 24–28w
- Other risk factors: OGTT within 24–28w
- NICE recommends early self-monitoring of blood glucose *(UK NICE guidance — **not verified against an Australian source**; see `PENDING_GUIDELINE_CHECKS.md` B32)*

**Dx**: fasting glucose ≥5.6 mmol/L OR 2h-OGTT ≥7.8 mmol/L

**Mx of newly diagnosed GDM**
- Joint diabetes-antenatal clinic within 1 week
- Teach self-monitoring of BGC; advise diet/exercise
- If fasting glucose <7: trial diet and exercise; if targets not met within 1–2w, start metformin; if still not met, add insulin
- If fasting glucose ≥7 at diagnosis: start short-acting insulin
- If glucose 6–6.9 with evidence of complications: start insulin

**Mx of pre-existing DM**
- Weight loss if BMI >27
- Stop hypoglycaemics except metformin, start insulin (tight control ↓complications)
- Folic acid 5mg/day pre-conception to 12w
- Detailed anomaly scan at 20w including 4-chamber cardiac view + outflow tracts
- Treat retinopathy (can worsen in pregnancy)

**C**: 50% of women with GDM develop T2DM — check fasting glucose 6w postpartum or HbA1c at 13w postpartum; screen annually thereafter.

### 0.12.3 Hypertension in pregnancy

**Normal BP course**: ↓ in early pregnancy until ~24w, then ↑ thereafter (↑stroke volume).

> [!danger] BP >160/110 = EMERGENCY

- HTN before 20w or high booking BP (>130/80) → likely to develop chronic HTN.

**Chronic hypertension**: 3–5% of pregnancies. Risks: pre-eclampsia, fetal growth restriction, placental abruption.

> [!danger] ACE inhibitors/ARBs are teratogenic — must be changed pre-conception to labetalol, nifedipine, hydralazine, or methyldopa.

- **Antenatal Mx**: aim BP <150/90 (DBP ≥80); aspirin 75mg OD until birth; admit if >160/110; fetal US q4w from 28w (growth, amniotic fluid, umbilical artery Dopplers); CTG if fetal activity abnormal; aim to induce around EDD
- **Intrapartum Mx**: monitor BP hourly if <159/109, continuously if ≥160/110; if refractory, advise operative delivery

> [!danger] Ergometrine contraindicated — give oxytocin alone at 3rd stage of labour.

- **Postnatal Mx**: check BP daily days 1–5, and at 2w; change methyldopa to alternative antihypertensive (postnatal depression risk); avoid diuretics if breastfeeding

**Pregnancy-induced hypertension (PIH)**: HTN (>140/90) in 2nd half of pregnancy without proteinuria or other pre-eclampsia features. 6–7% of pregnancies.

- **Antenatal Mx**: urine testing/ACR to rule out pre-eclampsia; mild → weekly urine/BP + 4-weekly growth scans; >150/100 → start tx + check 2x/week; ≥160/110 → admit, BP every 6h, urine OD, FBC/U&Es/AST/ALT/bilirubin at presentation and weekly; aim delivery after 37w unless pre-eclampsia develops
- **Intrapartum Mx**: continue antihypertensives; monitor BP hourly, continuously if >160/110 (advise C-section)
- **Postnatal Mx**: continue tx until BP <130/80; review at 2w and 6w; refer to cardiology if still needed at 6w

### 0.12.4 Hyperthyroidism in pregnancy

- Usually Graves' disease; associated with infertility, prematurity, fetal loss, malformations
- Managed with PTU (↓placental crossing); partial thyroidectomy if severe; monitor ≥monthly
- TSH-receptor stimulating antibodies can cause fetal thyrotoxicosis (1%) after 24w — test TFTs frequently in affected babies; usually resolves spontaneously in 2–3 months
- Thyroid storm can be precipitated by labour — urgent treatment required

### 0.12.5 Jaundice in pregnancy

- Occurs in 1:1500 pregnancies; diagnose urgently (travel Hx, drug Hx, BBV)

**Obstetric cholestasis** (0.7% prevalence)
- Pruritus (palms, soles) in 2nd half, no rash, worse at night
- Diagnosis of exclusion — rule out BBV, autoimmune disease, US of liver
- Weekly LFTs; give vitamin K 10mg/24h if abnormal clotting screen, 1mg to baby at birth
- Typically induced at 37w; ursodeoxycholic acid ↓itch
- Symptoms resolve within 1w of delivery; can recur with COCP and in ≤70% subsequent pregnancies

**Acute fatty liver of pregnancy**
- Rare but serious; abdominal pain, jaundice, headache, vomiting ± thrombocytopenia, pancreatitis
- Associated with pre-eclampsia in 30–60%
- Complications: coma, death
- Mx in HDU/ICU — supportive; expedite delivery

> [!danger] Epidural/regional anaesthesia contraindicated in acute fatty liver of pregnancy.

**Other causes of jaundice**: viral, severe pre-eclampsia, HELLP syndrome, anaesthesia (halothane).

### 0.12.6 Malaria in pregnancy

**S/smx**: consider in any woman presenting with odd behaviour, fever, jaundice, sweating, DIC, fetal distress, premature labour, seizures, or loss of consciousness → do thick and thin films, seek expert help.

- Advise against travel to endemic areas, or use of preventative measures (mosquito nets, insect repellent).

### 0.12.7 Renal disease in pregnancy

**Asymptomatic bacteriuria**
- 2% of sexually active women; ≤7% of pregnant women (↑ in DM, renal transplant); 30% progress to pyelonephritis

> [!info] Verified against SA Health's "Urinary Tract Infections in Pregnancy" guideline — the drug choices below (cefalexin, trimethoprim, nitrofurantoin) are consistent with Australian practice; the nitrofurantoin avoidance threshold is more precisely **after 37 weeks' gestation** (or sooner if early birth is planned) rather than a vaguer "3rd trimester," given the specific concern is proximity to delivery (theoretical neonatal haemolysis risk), not the trimester itself.
> If present on MSU: cefalexin 500mg TDS PO for 5 days; trimethoprim (avoid in 1st trimester); nitrofurantoin (avoid after 37 weeks' gestation or if early birth is planned).

**Pyelonephritis**
- 1–2% of pregnant women; do blood and urine culture

> [!info] Verified against SA Health's "Urinary Tract Infections in Pregnancy" guideline — admission for a minimum of 48 hours of IV antibiotic therapy is consistent with Australian practice; IV-to-oral switch once afebrile for ≥24h, inflammatory markers settling, and clinically improving, is the correct transition principle. The specific IV agent should be guided by local antibiogram/sensitivities and current eTG rather than assumed fixed — cefuroxime is a reasonable option but check current local guidance for the preferred first-line agent given this varies more by local resistance patterns than by a single national standard.
> IV abx per local guidance (e.g. cefuroxime or similar) ± stat-dose gentamicin depending on severity. Minimum 48h IV therapy, then switch to PO once afebrile ≥24h and clinically improving; total course 2–3w. Repeat MSU to confirm eradication. If ≥2 confirmed UTIs in pregnancy: renal US, consider abx prophylaxis for remainder of pregnancy.

**AKI related to pregnancy**
- Causes: sepsis, haemolysis (e.g. HELLP), hypovolaemia, volume contraction (e.g. pre-eclampsia), drugs (esp. NSAIDs)
- Monitor urine output and fluid balance (catheterisation), aim >30 mL/h + renal bloods; refer to specialist if needed

### 0.12.8 Psychiatric conditions — drugs in pregnancy

- Risk vs benefit; consult specialists especially in severe depression, bipolar disorder, schizophrenia
- High risk of rapid postpartum relapse with significant risks to mother and infant, especially in bipolar disorder (up to 50%)

**Antidepressants**
- SSRIs, usually sertraline: small risk of persistent fetal pulmonary HTN and neonatal withdrawal
- Avoid paroxetine
- Breastfeeding OK unless detrimental to maternal wellbeing

> [!danger] Mood stabilisers (AEDs)
> **Valproate and carbamazepine are contraindicated in women of childbearing age (WoCA).**
> - Valproate: 10% malformation rate (NTDs, craniofacial abnormalities), 30–40% neurodevelopmental problems
> - Carbamazepine: 2.2% malformation rate
> - Lamotrigine: 2.1% malformation rate
> - Avoid all in breastfeeding also

**Lithium**: associated with teratogenicity, neonatal thyroid abnormality, floppy baby syndrome.
- Only prescribe if no other choice; offer specialist fetal echo
- Monitor drug levels q4w to 36w, then weekly; do not change brands
- Signs of toxicity: tremor, drowsiness, visual disturbances
- Stop during labour; check 12h post-dose level, restart based on result
- **No breastfeeding** — neonatal toxicity risk

**Antipsychotics**
- Rates of fetal abnormality ↑ in schizophrenia even in untreated women
- Women on antipsychotics with weight gain should have OGTT
- **Clozapine**: do not breastfeed (fetal agranulocytosis risk)
- Avoid depot medications in WoCA

**Benzodiazepines**: associated with cleft lip/palate — avoid. Avoid diazepam in delivery (neonatal withdrawal risk).

### 0.12.9 Epilepsy in pregnancy

**D**: ~0.5% of women of childbearing age. Other seizure causes to exclude: eclampsia, cerebral vein thrombosis, intracranial mass, stroke, metabolic abnormality, drugs/withdrawal.

**Before pregnancy**
- Optimise treatment to lowest effective dose
- Folic acid 5mg for >3 months prior to conception
- Counsel on ↑risk of epilepsy in children (4–5% if either parent affected, 15–20% if both)
- Drug-specific notes:
  - Lamotrigine: possibly lower malformation rates than other AEDs; may need dose ↑
  - Valproate: associated with neural tube defects
  - Carbamazepine: considered least teratogenic of older AEDs
  - Phenytoin: associated with cleft palate

**During pregnancy**
- Consultant-led clinic, aim for vaginal delivery
- Nuchal/anomaly scans (± fetal echo), serial growth scans in 3rd trimester (↑SGA risk)
- 5mg folic acid daily for 1st trimester
- Routine drug level testing not recommended
- Avoid stress and sleep deprivation (↑seizure risk)

**Intrapartum**: deliver in hospital, continue AEDs; epidural safe for pain relief; benzodiazepines if seizure not self-terminating (e.g. lorazepam 4mg IV). Seizures more common intra/postpartum due to ↓sleep.

**Postpartum**
- Vitamin K 1mg to baby
- Inpatient for 24h postpartum (↑risk)
- Advise strategies to avoid dropping baby if seizure occurs (e.g. changing baby on floor)
- Encourage breastfeeding; review AED dose within 10 days of delivery to avoid toxicity
- Discuss contraception — oestrogen-containing methods contraindicated; ↑seizure risk with lamotrigine interactions

### 0.12.10 Connective tissue disease in pregnancy

**Rheumatoid arthritis**
- 75% improve during pregnancy, but 90% flare in puerperium
- Medications: MTX unsafe (stop ≥6 months before conception); leflunomide unsafe; sulfasalazine/HCQ safe; TNF-α blockers possibly safe; low-dose corticosteroids may control symptoms; paracetamol for pain; NSAIDs OK until 32w (risk of early ductus arteriosus closure)
- Refer to obstetric anaesthetist (risk of atlanto-axial subluxation)

**SLE**
- Flares more common in pregnancy and puerperium — can ↑risk to baby
- Planned pregnancies in remission have better outcomes
- Flares managed with corticosteroids + HCQ
- Aspirin 75–150mg from +ve pregnancy test until delivery
- If mother Ro+ve: 2% risk of congenital heart block, 5% transient neonatal cutaneous lupus

**Antiphospholipid syndrome**
- Untreated: <20% pregnancies proceed to live birth (1st trimester loss or placental thrombosis)
- ↑risk of pre-eclampsia and IUGR
- Mx: uterine artery Dopplers at 20–24w; if high risk, 4-weekly growth scans + umbilical artery Dopplers from 28w; aspirin 75–150mg OD if no previous adverse outcome (70–80% live birth rate); if previous fetal loss, add LMWH (enoxaparin 40mg SC) from fetal heart identification (~6w) throughout pregnancy; postpartum: heparin or warfarin

### 0.12.11 HIV in pregnancy

- Without intervention, 15% of babies acquire HIV from an HIV+ve mother
- 2/3 of vertical transmission occurs during pregnancy; breastfeeding doubles the rate
- ↑risk with: longer membrane rupture, viral load >400, seroconversion during pregnancy, advanced disease, pre-term labour, HCV co-infection
- ≤1% risk with maternal HAART, elective C-section, bottle feeding
  - Vaginal delivery recommended if viral load <50 at 36w, otherwise C-section
  - Start zidovudine infusion 4h before C-section
- Screening at booking and 28w; if status unknown at labour, perform rapid test and manage accordingly

### 0.12.12 Rubella in pregnancy

**Maternal s/smx** — absent in 50%: fever prodrome, maculopapular rash starting on face spreading to body, lymphadenopathy.

- Fetus most at risk in first 16w; 80% of fetuses affected if maternal primary infection in first 12w
- Miscarriage or stillbirth may occur

**Congenital rubella syndrome**: sensorineural deafness, congenital cataracts (infection at 8–9w), congenital heart disease e.g. PDA (infection at 5–10w), growth retardation, hepatosplenomegaly, purpuric skin lesions, 'salt and pepper' chorioretinitis, microphthalmia, cerebral palsy.

**Ix**: antibodies 10 days apart; look for IgM 4–5w from incubation period or contact date. If confirmed in 1st trimester, termination offered without invasive prenatal diagnosis.

### 0.12.13 Measles in pregnancy

**Maternal s/smx**: prodromal irritability, cough, conjunctivitis, fever; Koplik spots (before rash); rash starts behind ears, spreads to whole body (discrete maculopapular → blotchy/confluent); diarrhoea in 10%.

**Ix**: IgM antibodies detected >4 days and <1 month of rash onset; viral RNA in saliva.

**Implications in pregnancy**: associated with fetal loss and preterm delivery; no congenital infection/abnormality syndrome. If maternal rash appears 6 days pre- or post-delivery, give human normal immunoglobulin immediately after birth/exposure to prevent neonatal subacute sclerosing panencephalitis.

**Complications**: encephalitis (typically 1–2w post-illness); subacute sclerosing panencephalitis (very rare, may present 5–10y after illness — behavioural change, myoclonus, choreoathetosis, dystonia, dementia, coma, death); febrile convulsions.


<!-- ===== SOURCE: 16_06-07_Ante-Perinatal_Infections.md ===== -->


> [!info] Localisation status — every antibiotic/antiviral regimen in this file has now been individually verified against Australian sources (SA Health, Safer Care Victoria, Melbourne Sexual Health Centre, and current international paediatric dosing literature) — see the [!info] verification note under each condition below for the specific correction or confirmation made. Several genuine dosing/drug corrections were found (anti-D was resolved separately in [[16_01-05_Antenatal_Care]]; syphilis maternal treatment uses a different drug and schedule entirely; GBS and gonococcal/chlamydial neonatal conjunctivitis regimens also needed real corrections, not just terminology swaps).

## CMV (Cytomegalovirus) in pregnancy

🐞 CMV — subtype of Herpes group.

**E**: 5:1000 live births infected. 20% develop late, usually minor problems (1:1000). 10% symptomatic — 1/3 die, 2/3 have long-term problems (congenital CMV).

**Maternal s/smx**: mononucleosis-like — flu-like symptoms, ↑temperature, lymphadenopathy, rash, sore throat.

**Fetal s/smx (congenital CMV)**: growth retardation, pinpoint petechial "blueberry muffin" skin lesions, microcephaly, sensorineural deafness, encephalitis (seizures), hepatosplenomegaly → jaundice.

**Ix**: generally refer to lab; amniocentesis at >20w + shell viral culture can detect fetal transmission; throat swab, urine culture, baby's serum after birth.

**Prevention**: avoid exposure to toddlers' urine.

---

## Toxoplasmosis in pregnancy

🐞 *Toxoplasma gondii* — 🔀 oocytes in cats/rats → GIT, lungs, broken skin.

**E**: affects 2–7:1000 pregnancies; 40% of fetuses affected if mother has illness. Earlier in pregnancy = more damage but less transmissible.

**Maternal s/smx**: similar to glandular fever ± fever, rash, eosinophilia.

**Fetal s/smx**: >90% asymptomatic, picked up by serology. If severe: intracranial calcification, hydrocephalus, chorioretinitis. Others: encephalitis, epilepsy, developmental delay, jaundice, hepatosplenomegaly, thrombocytopenia, skin rashes.

**Ix**: diagnosis by reference lab IgG/IgM.

> [!info] Verified against SA Health's Toxoplasmosis in Pregnancy Perinatal Practice Guideline, Aug 2026 — **the drug choices and general dosing structure below are genuinely consistent with Australian practice**, not UK-specific figures needing wholesale replacement. The single most important Australia-specific point: **spiramycin, pyrimethamine, and sulfadiazine are not TGA-registered in Australia**, though all remain available (typically via the Special Access Scheme or as named-patient supply through a tertiary/reference centre) — a genuinely practical access point an Australian clinician needs to know before assuming these can simply be prescribed and dispensed through a normal community pharmacy pathway. Management of confirmed/suspected toxoplasmosis in pregnancy should involve specialist input (maternal-fetal medicine/infectious diseases) given both the diagnostic complexity and this medication-access issue. "Avoid sheep during lambing" in the prevention advice below reflects a genuine additional transmission route (infected ewes shedding oocysts around lambing) relevant to rural/farming exposure, not a UK-specific quirk.
> **Maternal Mx**
> - If <18w gestation: start spiramycin 1g PO q8h ASAP
> - In symptomatic non-immune women: test every 10w through pregnancy
> - If infected, consider amniocentesis to check fetal infection
> - If ≥18w and fetal infection confirmed: pyrimethamine 50mg BD day 1, then 1mg/kg/day thereafter; **and** sulfadiazine 50mg/kg/12h; **and** calcium folinate 15mg twice weekly until delivery

**Prevention**: avoid raw meat; wash hands after touching raw meat; wear gloves gardening/handling cat litter; avoid sheep during lambing.

---

## Intrauterine syphilis

🐞 *Treponema pallidum*

**Fetal/neonatal s/smx**: rhinitis & snuffles, rash, hepatosplenomegaly, lymphadenopathy, anaemia, jaundice, ascites, hydrops, nephrosis, meningitis, keratitis, sensorineural hearing loss.

**Ix**: XR (perichondritis), nasal discharge exam (spirochetes), CSF (↑monocytes and protein with +ve serology).

> [!info] Verified against Melbourne Sexual Health Centre's syphilis-in-pregnancy treatment guidelines and current O&G Magazine RANZCOG-context guidance, Aug 2026 — **this is a genuine, substantial correction, not a units/naming adjustment**: Australian maternal treatment uses **benzathine penicillin (a long-acting depot formulation) 2.4 million units IM as a single dose** for early/symptomatic syphilis, or **three doses of 2.4 million units IM at weekly intervals** for late latent or unknown-duration syphilis — a completely different drug formulation, dose, and schedule from the original "benzylpenicillin 600mg/24h IM daily for 10 days" (benzylpenicillin and benzathine penicillin are pharmacologically different products; the daily-dosing regimen in the original note doesn't reflect Australian practice for this indication at all). **Maternal treatment is only considered adequate if completed at least 30 days before delivery** — a genuinely important practical threshold, since infants born within 30 days of maternal treatment require careful individual neonatal evaluation regardless of the treatment given. For the neonate: a single dose of benzathine penicillin 50,000 U/kg is used specifically for lower-risk infants (mother treated ≥30 days before delivery, with neonatal RPR titre <4× the maternal titre) — higher-risk or inadequately-treated scenarios need a more intensive IV benzylpenicillin course and specialist paediatric input, not the single-dose regimen. Given the complexity of syphilis serology interpretation and treatment adequacy assessment, specialist involvement (infectious diseases/sexual health) is strongly recommended for all cases, not just an optional escalation.
> **Mx**
> - Maternal: benzathine penicillin 2.4 million units IM — single dose for early/symptomatic syphilis; 3 doses at weekly intervals for late latent/unknown duration
> - Neonate, lower risk (mother treated ≥30 days before delivery, neonatal RPR <4× maternal): benzathine penicillin 50,000 U/kg IM single dose
> - Neonate, higher risk or inadequately/not treated: more intensive IV benzylpenicillin course per current local/eTG neonatal protocol and specialist input — not the single-dose regimen above

---

## Parvovirus B19

🐞 DNA virus — 🔀 respiratory droplets. **E**: 50% of women in UK are immune.

**Maternal s/smx**: usually asymptomatic; if symptomatic — "slapped cheek" rash, maculopapular rash, fever, arthralgia. Usually no maternal complications.

> [!danger] If mother is immunocompromised or susceptible (e.g. sickle cell disease), sudden haemolysis may occur requiring transfusion.

**Fetal s/smx**: affects 30% of fetuses — suppression of erythropoiesis and cardiac toxicity → heart failure and fetal hydrops. 10% of fetuses affected at <20w will die. No teratogenicity.

**Ix**: paired samples in acute and convalescent phases (>10 days apart) — IgM antibodies appear, IgG titres rise.

**Mx**: serial US for signs of fetal anaemia (fetal hydrops, abnormal MCA Dopplers). If fetal anaemia develops, manage in tertiary fetal medicine unit; consider in-utero RBC transfusion.

---

## Listeria

🐞 Gram +ve rod, *Listeria monocytogenes* — 🔀 soil, food contamination (soft cheeses, unpasteurised milk, deli meats/salads, meat spreads, pâté). Rarely spreads vertically. Affects 6–15:100,000 pregnancies.

**Maternal s/smx**: fever, shivering, myalgia, headache, sore throat, cough, vomiting, diarrhoea, vaginitis. Rarely: miscarriage (± recurrent), premature labour, stillbirth.

> [!info] Maternal Ix/Dx — blood cultures in any pregnant patient with unexplained fever ≥48h. Serology, vaginal and rectal swabs do NOT help. This is a universal diagnostic principle for Listeria, not jurisdiction-specific guidance.

**Fetal s/smx**: 20% affected are stillborn; fetal distress in labour common. Early postnatal: respiratory distress from pneumonia. Others: convulsions, hepatosplenomegaly, pustular/petechial rashes, conjunctivitis, fever, leukopenia, meningitis.

**Fetal Ix/Dx**: blood cultures ± CSF, meconium, placenta cultures.

> [!danger] Mx
> **Isolate baby.** Treat with ampicillin (dosing broadly similar internationally, commonly cited around 50–100mg/kg/dose 6-hourly, though this should be checked against current local protocol) and gentamicin until 1 week after fever subsides. Monitor abx levels.
>
> [!info] Verified, Aug 2026 — the ampicillin + gentamicin combination itself is genuinely standard first-line therapy for neonatal listeriosis internationally, including in Australia, so the drug choice doesn't need correcting. **The gentamicin dose specifically should not be taken as a single fixed number**: a 2025 Australian study (Flinders Medical Centre/SA Health) comparing neonatal gentamicin dosing guidelines found **5 different unique Australian guidelines in use**, with doses ranging 4.5–7mg/kg and intervals of 24–48 hours depending on gestational age and postnatal age at treatment — a deliberately individualised approach, not a single flat mg/kg/interval figure like the original "3mg/kg/12h". Always use the current local NICU/neonatal protocol for the specific gentamicin dose and interval, with therapeutic drug monitoring (peak/trough levels) given the narrow therapeutic index and real nephrotoxicity/ototoxicity risk — this monitoring principle from the original note remains correct and important.

---

## Hepatitis B in pregnancy

🐞 DNA virus — 🔀 blood, bodily fluids, vertically.

**Maternal infection**: can be asymptomatic. Acute infection in mid/3rd trimester → high risk of perinatal infection. Risk of maternal death 0.5–3%. All mothers screened for HBsAg (then full hepatitis panel) — carriers have HBsAg >6 months; HBeAg+ve indicates ↑infectivity.

**Fetal infection**: without immunisation, 95% of babies born to these mothers develop Hep B; 93% become chronic carriers at 6 months. Most infections occur at birth, some transplacental (apparent vaccination failure). Lifetime HCC risk in infected infants: 50% (M), 20% (F) — most develop cirrhosis.

**Mx**: immunoglobulin (200U IM) + vaccinate babies of carriers/infected mothers at birth. In uncomplicated Hep B, anti-HBc and anti-HBe antibodies develop, antigens disappear ~3 months. Serology of vaccinated baby at 12–15 months; if HBsAg-ve and anti-HBs+ve, child is protected.

- Not transmitted by breastfeeding
- Little evidence that C-section reduces vertical transmission

## Hepatitis E in pregnancy

**Maternal infection**: ↑maternal mortality risk — 25% in 3rd trimester. Can cause fulminant hepatic failure, coma, massive PPH → death.

**Fetal infection**: 33–50% of babies become infected.

---

## Herpes simplex (HSV) in pregnancy

🐞 HSV1, HSV2.

**Maternal infection**: prevalence of past infection 25%; recurrence in pregnancy rare.

If mother develops **primary genital herpes** in pregnancy:
- Refer to GUM to screen for other STIs + confirm primary infection (PCR)
- If 3rd trimester: PO aciclovir or valaciclovir
- ± elective C-section if primary infection within 6w of due date

If mother has primary infection lesions at time of delivery:
- LSCS recommended
- If vaginal delivery: give mother and newborn high-dose aciclovir
- Avoid fetal blood sampling, scalp electrodes, instrumental delivery

**Fetal infection**: if vaginal delivery, risk to baby 41% → high-dose aciclovir; PCR for baby at birth. Neonatal infection appears at 5–21 days: grouped vesicles/pustules on red base (tends to be on presenting part or trauma sites e.g. scalp electrode); ± periocular/conjunctival lesions; ± non-vesicular rash.

> [!danger] Complications: blindness, ↓IQ, epilepsy, jaundice, respiratory distress, DIC, death (in 30% even with treatment).

---

## Varicella zoster in pregnancy

🐞 DNA virus (Herpes group).

**Maternal infection**: symptoms as per normal VZV. 5x ↑risk of pneumonitis.

**Risks to baby**
- **Fetal varicella syndrome**: 1% risk if infection before 20w — skin scarring, eye defects (microphthalmia), limb hypoplasia, microcephaly, learning disabilities
- **Shingles in infancy**: 1–2% risk if maternal exposure in 2nd/3rd trimester
- **Severe neonatal varicella**: fatal in 20% of cases — risk if mother develops rash between 5 days before and 2 days after birth (↑↑transmission risk)

> [!info] Verified, Aug 2026 — genuinely different finding from most other flags in this file: **RANZCOG hosts and directly adopts the RCOG Green-top Guideline No. 13 (Chickenpox in Pregnancy) for Australian practice**, rather than having a substantially different Australian-specific protocol — so the overall approach below (VZIG for non-immune exposed women, effective up to 10 days post-exposure, not given once a rash has already developed, aciclovir for established infection) is genuinely consistent with Australian guidance, not a UK-specific approach needing wholesale replacement. The main Australian-specific detail: the product used in Australia is typically **VariZIG**, dosed by weight (125 IU/10kg, up to a maximum of 625 IU) rather than a flat UK-style dose — and, as in the UK, supply can be limited, so availability should be confirmed with the local immunisation/blood product service before offering it. Ideally given within 96 hours of exposure, though effective up to 10 days.
> - If in doubt, check maternal blood for varicella antibodies urgently
> - If ≤20w and not immune: give **VZIG (varicella zoster immunoglobulin)** ASAP — effective up to 10 days post-exposure, and the deadline exists because preformed antibody can only pre-empt an infection that has not yet established (see [[08_01-03_Infectious_Disease_-_Bacterial_Infections]] Passive Immunisation — Immunoglobulin After an Exposure)
> - If >20w and not immune: VZIG, or PO antivirals from day 7–14 post-exposure
>
> **Mx of chickenpox in pregnancy**: refer to specialist. RCOG suggests PO aciclovir if ≥20w and presenting within 24h of rash onset. If <20w, PO aciclovir considered with caution.

---

## Chlamydia trachomatis (perinatal)

🐞 *Chlamydia trachomatis*

**Fetal infection/complications**: associated with low birthweight, premature membrane rupture, fetal death; 30% of infected mothers have affected babies. Conjunctivitis develops 5–14 days after birth (late) — inflammation/pus. Complications: chlamydial pneumonitis, pharyngitis, otitis media.

> [!info] Verified, Aug 2026 — the erythromycin dose/duration below is broadly consistent with current international paediatric dosing (oral erythromycin ~50mg/kg/day divided into 4 doses, i.e. ~12.5mg/kg/6h, for 14 days is the most commonly cited regimen; some sources use up to 3 weeks given erythromycin's efficacy is only ~80%, sometimes requiring a second course) — check current eTG for the exact Australian-specified duration, but the drug choice and general dosing structure don't need wholesale replacement. Azithromycin is an accepted alternative with a shorter course where compliance with the 2–3 week erythromycin course is a concern.
> Local eye cleansing. Erythromycin ~12.5mg/kg/6h PO for 2–3 weeks eliminates lung organisms. Give parents erythromycin or azithromycin 1g PO single dose.

## Gonorrhoea (perinatal)

🐞 *Neisseria gonorrhoeae*

**Fetal infection/complications**: occurs within 4 days of birth (early), purulent discharge, lid swelling ± corneal hazing, corneal rupture, panophthalmitis. 50% have concurrent chlamydial infection.

> [!info] Verified, Aug 2026 — **genuinely important Australian-specific practice point, per Children's Health Queensland guidance**: Australia does **not** use routine ocular prophylaxis (silver nitrate or antibiotic ointment/drops instilled at birth) as a preventive measure, unlike historical US/Canadian practice — this means chemical conjunctivitis from prophylaxis (a recognised early differential in some other jurisdictions) is essentially not a consideration in Australian neonates. **For active infection, current international dosing consistently favours a single dose of ceftriaxone (25–50mg/kg IV/IM, max 125mg) as the primary systemic treatment**, with frequent saline eye irrigation as an adjunct rather than routine additional topical antibiotic (e.g. chloramphenicol) once systemic ceftriaxone has been given — a genuinely different emphasis from the original file's cefotaxime-plus-topical-chloramphenicol structure. Hospitalise given gonococcal conjunctivitis can rapidly penetrate an intact cornea (a genuine ocular emergency) — check current eTG for the exact Australian-specified dose/duration before use.
> - Infants of mothers with known untreated gonorrhoea, or infants with confirmed gonococcal conjunctivitis: single dose ceftriaxone (dose per current eTG/local paediatric protocol, typically in the 25–50mg/kg IV/IM range, max 125mg) + frequent saline eye irrigation; hospitalise and evaluate for disseminated infection.

## Ophthalmia neonatorum

**D**: purulent discharge from eye of a neonate <21 days old.

**A**: blocked lacrimal glands, infection (chlamydia, herpes virus, staph, strep, pneumococci, *E. coli*).

**Dx**: swab for bacterial/viral culture, microscopy (look for intracellular gonococci), chlamydia testing.

**Mx**: treat underlying infection; bathe eyes with cooled boiled water or expressed breast milk.

## Clostridium perfringens (puerperal)

**D**: Gram +ve bacilli.

**R**: illegal termination of pregnancy (improperly cleaned equipment).

**S/smx**: endometritis → septicaemia, gangrene → myoglobinuria → renal failure → death. In-utero fetal death. Local infection of anaerobic sites (e.g. haematomas).

**Ix/Dx**: swab genital site — intracellular encapsulated Gram +ve rods.

**Mx**: surgical debridement of all devitalised tissue; hyperbaric O2; high-dose IV benzylpenicillin (erythromycin if severe penicillin allergy).

## TB in pregnancy

- BCG vaccination after birth for: all babies born into households with TB; mothers from areas with high TB prevalence; all babies who will travel to such areas
- Give all other vaccinations per normal schedule; avoid BCG arm for 3 months
- Separate babies from mothers with active/open TB until 2 weeks of treatment and sputum-negative
- Vaccinate baby with BCG and treat with isoniazid until +ve skin reaction (Mantoux)
- Consider CXR in pregnant women with cough, fever, or weight loss
- Encourage breastfeeding

## Group B Streptococcus (GBS)

🔀 Mother to child — 20–40% of mothers carry GBS in bowel flora.

**Risk factors for transmission**: premature birth, prolonged rupture of membranes, previous sibling GBS infection, maternal pyrexia.

**Which screening strategy triggers prophylaxis is service-dependent** — Australian guidance permits either universal culture-based screening at 35–37 weeks or a risk-factor-only approach, and there is no single national standard. Set out in [[08_01-03_Infectious_Disease_-_Bacterial_Infections]] Group B Strep (*Streptococcus agalactiae*), not repeated here; the intrapartum dosing below is owned by this file.

**Fetal infection/complications**: severe, early onset — 20% mortality. Pneumonia, meningitis, septicaemia.

> [!info] Verified against Safer Care Victoria's Group B Streptococcus (GBS) — screening and management guideline, Aug 2026 — **the risk-factor list below is broadly consistent with Australian practice** (GBS-positive swab, GBS bacteriuria, preterm labour, prolonged rupture of membranes, intrapartum fever are all genuine Australian indications for intrapartum antibiotic prophylaxis, IAP). The **maintenance dose needed a genuine correction**: Australian dosing is benzylpenicillin **3g IV loading, then 1.8g every 4 hours** (not 1.5g) until birth — a real, if modest, difference from the UK figure, not just a units/terminology issue. **Adequate prophylaxis is specifically defined as the first dose given at least 4 hours before birth** — a practical threshold worth stating explicitly, given a shorter interval is considered inadequate regardless of how many doses were given. For penicillin allergy, **Australian first-line alternative is cephazolin** (2g IV loading, then 1g every 8 hours) rather than clindamycin as the primary alternative — clindamycin remains an option in some state guidelines specifically for those with a history of anaphylaxis to penicillin (given cephazolin carries some cross-reactivity risk in that specific group), so the choice of alternative agent should follow the allergy severity, not be a blanket clindamycin default.
>
> **Timing is part of the dose.** **IAP should be given ≥4 hours before birth** wherever possible — prophylaxis started later is substantially less effective at preventing early-onset neonatal disease, which is why the decision to start is made on suspicion rather than after confirmation. (Stated in [[08_01-03_Infectious_Disease_-_Bacterial_Infections]] Group B Strep (*Streptococcus agalactiae*) and absent from this entry — the one that owns the regimen — until the Phase 4 close GBS check, 2026-08-29.)
>
> **Dosing**: benzylpenicillin 3g IV loading dose, then 1.8g q4h throughout labour. If penicillin-allergic (no anaphylaxis history): cephazolin 2g IV loading, then 1g q8h. If history of anaphylaxis to penicillin: clindamycin (confirm susceptibility where possible) — check current local/eTG guidance for the specific alternative given practice varies somewhat by state.


<!-- ===== SOURCE: 16_08-09_Antenatal_and_Perinatal_Problems.md ===== -->


> [!info] Localised for Australia — pre-eclampsia diagnostic thresholds confirmed internationally standard (including in SOMANZ guidance); aspirin prophylaxis dose corrected to the SOMANZ-specific 150mg nightly figure, Aug 2026. See the Pre-eclampsia entry below for detail.

## Small for gestational age (SGA)

**D**: <10th centile for gestational age, or abdominal circumference <10th centile.

**R**: maternal age >40, smoker, cocaine use, previous SGA baby, previous stillbirth, maternal/paternal SGA, chronic HTN, DM, renal impairment, antiphospholipid syndrome, heavy antepartum bleeding, echogenic bowel, pre-eclampsia, low PAPP-A.

**A**: abnormal trophoblast invasion tends to cause **asymmetrical** growth restriction (head sparing, ↓abdominal circumference). Genetic abnormalities (e.g. trisomies), congenital abnormalities and infections usually cause **symmetrically** small fetus.

**Screening**: mothers with risk factors monitored more closely; if <10th centile or static growth → refer for fetal US.

**Mx**
- Once identified: serial fetal size measurements; umbilical artery Doppler from 26–28w + every 2–3w
- Normal Dopplers: aim induction at 37w
- Abnormal Dopplers: urgency of delivery determined by other factors; may require C-section
- Offer corticosteroids for fetal lung maturity up to 35+6w
- After birth: temperature regulation important; feed within 2h of birth + monitor for hypoglycaemia

**P**: adulthood risks — ↑HTN, coronary artery disease, T2DM, autoimmune thyroid disease.

---

## Large for gestational age (LGA)

**D**: >95th centile in weight for gestation.

**R**: FHx of LGA babies, maternal DM, obesity.

**Mx**: prepare for shoulder dystocia delivery. Babies prone to hypoglycaemia and hypocalcaemia — prompt feeding, monitor electrolytes. Left colon syndrome may develop (self-limiting condition clinically mimicking Hirschsprung disease).

---

## Abdominal pain in pregnancy — DDx

### Pregnancy-related causes

> [!danger] Red flags requiring urgent workup
> - **Pre-eclampsia** — especially in later pregnancy
> - **Cardiac causes** — chest, back, or epigastric pain, severe enough for opioids, requires full cardiac workup
> - **Placental abruption** — triad of abdominal pain + uterine rigidity/tenderness + vaginal bleeding (clinical diagnosis, don't rely on US); 1 in 80–200 pregnancies; high fetal loss if >50% placenta affected; prepare for DIC (33–50%) and PPH
> - **Uterine rupture**

- Uterine fibroids, uterine torsion, ovarian tumours

### Other causes

| Cause | Notes |
|---|---|
| Appendicitis | ~1:1000 pregnancies; ↑mortality, ↑perforation risk; presentation may differ due to shifted appendix position; fetal mortality 1.5% (simple) vs ~30% (perforated). **Don't delay surgery — laparoscopy.** |
| Cholecystitis | ~1–6:10,000 pregnancies; similar presentation to non-pregnant (subcostal pain, N&V); US confirms stones; usually self-resolving, lap chole safe if surgery required |
| Gastroenteritis | Common; manage at home if otherwise well (ORS + rest); beware sepsis |
| Rectus sheath haematoma | Rare; Dx via US; laparoscopy if Dx in doubt or patient in shock |
| Pancreatitis | Rare, ↑mortality; Dx via serum amylase |
| UTI / pyelonephritis | See antenatal care notes (16.04) |

---

## Pre-eclampsia

**D**: disorder of pregnancy characterised by:
- New-onset HTN (SBP ≥140 and/or DBP ≥90 mmHg) after 20w
- Proteinuria (≥300mg/24h urine, dipstick ≥2+, or protein:creatinine ratio ≥30mg/mmol)
- Where proteinuria is absent: thrombocytopenia (platelets <100), renal insufficiency (SrCr >90µmol/L), impaired liver function, pulmonary oedema, or new-onset headache not attributable to other conditions

> [!info] Risk factors for prophylactic aspirin
> **High risk factors**: HTN disease in previous pregnancy, chronic HTN, CKD, T1/T2DM, autoimmune disease (e.g. SLE)
>
> **Moderate risk factors**: nulliparity, ≥40yo, previous pregnancy >10y ago, BMI ≥35 at booking, FHx of pre-eclampsia, twins

**A/P**: failure of normal trophoblast invasion → maladaptation of maternal spiral arterioles → vasoconstriction and capillary leaking → HTN + complications.

**S/smx (if severe)**: HTN (typically >160/110), proteinuria, headache, visual disturbances/papilloedema, RUQ/epigastric pain, hyperreflexia, HELLP syndrome.

**Ix**: urinalysis + bloods (Dx and complication screening), fetal US, umbilical artery Doppler velocimetry, amniotic fluid assessment.

**Mx**
- **Prevention**: in women with ≥1 high-risk or ≥2 moderate-risk factors — aspirin 150mg PO nightly, commencing before 16 weeks' gestation (ideally from 11–12 weeks, given earlier initiation hasn't shown additional benefit) and ceasing after 34 weeks — verified against the SOMANZ (Society of Obstetric Medicine of Australia and New Zealand) Hypertension in Pregnancy Guideline, the Australian-specific reference for this dose, Aug 2026. This is a genuinely more specific figure than a "75–150mg" range — SOMANZ specifically recommends 150mg based on RCT evidence in this population, though international guidance is not fully settled (WHO favours 75mg, citing postpartum haemorrhage risk at higher doses) — 150mg nightly is the correct Australian-specific standard to use here.
- New onset suspected: refer for emergency secondary care assessment; BP ≥160/110 → likely admission and observation; if BP remains high after antihypertensives, may need **prophylactic magnesium sulfate — given to prevent progression to eclampsia** (about **1%** of pre-eclampsia does progress).

> [!warning] **Magnesium sulfate is named here with no dose and no monitoring, and this is the entry where prophylaxis is actually started** — before any seizure has happened. The regimen and, more importantly, the **stop criteria** are owned by [[16_14-15_Obstetric_Emergencies]] Eclampsia, verified there against Queensland Clinical Guidelines: **4g IV over 20 minutes** via a controlled infusion device, then **1g/h for 24 hours**, continued for 24 hours after the last seizure or after delivery. **Stop for RR <12/min, loss of tendon reflexes, or urine output <20 mL/h** — magnesium toxicity is the reason this drug is monitored rather than simply given, and none of that was reachable from here. Linked by the G34 round, 2026-08-29.
- For HTN Mx (>150/100): PO labetalol; nifedipine (e.g. if asthmatic) or hydralazine
- Plan for baby's birth

---

## HELLP syndrome

**= H**aemolysis, **E**levated **L**iver enzymes, **L**ow **P**latelets.

**R**: white ethnicity, mother >35yo, obesity, chronic HTN, DM, autoimmune disorders, migraine, abnormal placentation (e.g. molar pregnancy), previous pregnancy with pre-eclampsia.

**A/P**: thought to be a severe form of pre-eclampsia (~10–20% of severe pre-eclampsia patients develop HELLP).

> [!info] Gap-filled — HELLP's three components were named without explaining why this specific triad occurs together, or how it connects to the pre-eclampsia mechanism established above. **All three components stem from the same underlying process**: pre-eclampsia's endothelial damage (see the trophoblast invasion/spiral arteriole mechanism above, not repeated here) becomes severe enough to cause **widespread microthrombi and fibrin deposition within small blood vessels** — a microangiopathic process. As red cells are forced through these partially obstructed, fibrin-laden vessels, they're mechanically sheared and fragmented, producing **haemolysis** (the schistocytes/fragmented red cells seen on blood film below are the direct visual evidence of this mechanical damage). The same fibrin deposition and microthrombi occurring specifically within the **hepatic sinusoids** cause localised ischaemia and periportal/focal hepatocyte necrosis, releasing **elevated liver enzymes**. And the same widespread endothelial damage and microthrombi formation **consumes platelets** faster than they can be replaced, producing **thrombocytopenia**. This is precisely why HELLP is understood as an extension of pre-eclampsia's endothelial dysfunction rather than three coincidentally co-occurring abnormalities — one underlying process (microangiopathy) manifesting in three different ways depending on which tissue bears the brunt of the vascular damage.

**S/smx**: N&V, RUQ pain, lethargy.

**Ix**: FBC with differential incl. platelets (↓Hb, thrombocytopenia); peripheral blood smear (schistocytes = haemolysis); liver enzymes (AST/ALT >70, or >2x ULN); urinalysis; clotting screen.

**Mx**
- If suspected: start IV magnesium sulfate to prevent seizures
- Start dexamethasone
- Definitive Mx: deliver the baby

**Complications**: eclampsia, fetal IUGR/prematurity, liver involvement, heart failure, haemorrhage (placental abruption, intra-abdominal haemorrhage, ICH).

---

## Oligohydramnios

**D**: reduced amniotic fluid — amniotic fluid index ≤5cm (or <5th centile).

**Causes**
- Idiopathic (cause may be found after baby is born)
- Maternal: uteroplacental insufficiency (e.g. pre-eclampsia), medications (e.g. NSAIDs)
- Placental: abruption, thrombosis/infarction
- Fetal: chromosomal abnormalities (e.g. Potter sequence — bilateral renal agenesis + pulmonary hypoplasia → ↓urine production → ↓amniotic fluid); growth restriction or death; post-term gestation; ruptured fetal membranes

## Polyhydramnios

**D**: excessive amniotic fluid — suspect if uterine size large for gestational age; amniotic fluid index ≥24cm.

**Causes**
- Idiopathic
- Fetal structural anomaly impeding swallowing (e.g. oesophageal atresia, cleft lip)
- Fetal neuromuscular disorder impeding swallowing
- Genetic syndromes (e.g. trisomies, Prader-Willi)
- High fetal cardiac output state (e.g. severe anaemia)
- Maternal diabetes mellitus
- Macrosomia
- Hydrops fetalis

---

## Prematurity

**D**: infant born before 37w gestation. Extreme prematurity: <28w. Term infants: 37–41w.

**R**: intrauterine infection, preterm premature rupture of membranes, pre-eclampsia/PIH, placental abruption, antepartum haemorrhage.

**Risks/complications in premature infants**

| Complication | Notes |
|---|---|
| Respiratory distress syndrome | 2° immature lungs/surfactant deficiency; Mx: antenatal corticosteroids + postnatal surfactant; ↑risk of bronchopulmonary dysplasia |
| Sepsis | ↑risk with ↓gestational age and invasive procedures |
| Anaemia of prematurity | Treat with adequate nutrition + iron supplementation |
| Nutritional deficiencies | Early breast milk or premature formula |
| Jaundice | 2° inadequate GI bilirubin losses + poor enteral nutrition; Mx: phototherapy |
| Intraventricular haemorrhage / white matter injury | 2° immature cerebral blood vessels; ↑mortality and neurodevelopmental dysfunction |
| Necrotising enterocolitis | Mx: antenatal corticosteroids + breast milk ± probiotics |
| Patent ductus arteriosus | — |
| Retinopathy of prematurity | Associated with excessive O2 exposure; maintain sats 85–93% until 32w post-menstrual age; regular ophthalmology screening |
| Cerebral palsy / neurodevelopmental impairment | — |
| ADHD / behavioural sequelae | — |
| ↑risk of abuse/neglect | — |

> [!note] Extreme prematurity carries the highest mortality risk of all prematurity categories.

---

## Postmaturity

**D**: infant born after 42w pregnancy.

**R**: previous prolonged pregnancy.

**Risks to baby & mother**: intrapartum deaths (4x risk), early neonatal deaths (3x risk), ↑induction/operative delivery rates, ↑placental insufficiency risk, macrosomia (>4kg)/shoulder dystocia/fetal injury, more ossified/less mouldable fetal skull, ↑meconium passage in labour (↑meconium aspiration risk), ↑fetal distress in labour, ↑C-section rates.

**Mx**
- At 38w visit: discuss plan, arrange 41w visit if not delivered
- Membrane sweep: finger inserted through cervix, membrane palpated — thought to induce natural prostaglandins; offered at 40w and 41w visits
- Induction at 41w reduces fetal death: vaginal prostaglandin + oxytocin
- If mother declines induction: twice-weekly CTG + amniotic fluid depth estimation (detects fetuses becoming hypoxic)

---

## Birth injuries

| Injury | Description |
|---|---|
| Moulding (not an injury) | Overriding of skull bones |
| Cephalhaematoma | Subperiosteal swelling, limited by suture lines, fluctuant; may take weeks to resolve; may contribute to jaundice |
| Caput succedaneum | Oedematous scalp swelling superficial to periosteum; **not** limited by suture lines; from venous congestion + exuded serum during labour ("chignon" if ventouse-caused); resolves in first few days |
| Erb's palsy | Brachial trunk nerve damage — flaccid arm/hand in fixed posture; most resolve; exclude fractured clavicle, arrange physio; unlikely to improve further if not resolved by 6 months |
| Subaponeurotic haematoma | Blood between aponeurosis and periosteum; associated with vacuum extraction; may contribute to anaemia or jaundice |
| Skull fractures | Associated with difficult forceps delivery or impacted head at 2nd-stage C-section; parietal/frontal bones most affected; check CNS signs → neurosurgery consult |
| Intracranial injuries | ICH associated with difficult/fast labour, instrumental or breech delivery, especially in premature babies; anoxia may cause intraventricular haemorrhage; asphyxia may cause ICH → cerebral palsy (extradural, subdural, or subarachnoid); supportive Mx |
| Fetal laceration | 1–2% of C-section deliveries (more common in breech C-section and post-membrane-rupture C-section); most superficial, heal without scarring; warn parents when consenting for C-section |

*See 15.23 for minor neonatal problems.*


<!-- ===== SOURCE: 16_10-13_Labour_and_Delivery.md ===== -->


> [!info] Localised for Australia — the LMWH/regional anaesthesia timing and caesarean urgency classification verified against Queensland Clinical Guidelines and Safer Care Victoria, Aug 2026; blood product preparation confirmed as genuinely risk-stratified in Australian practice rather than following a single fixed figure. See individual entries below for detail.

## Normal labour

**D**: onset of regular and painful contractions associated with cervical dilation and descent of the presenting part.
- Occurs after 37w gestation
- Results in spontaneous delivery within 24h of onset of spontaneous contractions
- Divided into 3 stages

**Pre-labour signs**
- "Show": shedding of cervical mucus plug and a little blood (membranes stripping from the os)
- ± Rupture of membranes

### Stage 1
From onset of true labour to full cervical dilation (~8–18h primip; 5–12h multip).
- **Latent phase**: painful, irregular contractions; cervix shortens + dilates to 3cm*
- **Established phase**: regular contractions with dilation from 3cm,* normally 1cm/h

> [!note] *Passmed cites 3cm as the established-phase threshold; OHCS cites 4cm. Source disagreement — verify against local protocol.

### Stage 2
From full dilation to delivery of fetus (~3h primip, 2h multip).
- **Passive phase**: complete cervical dilation but no pushing. 1–2h passive stage recommended in women with epidural anaesthesia to ↓instrumental delivery rate
- **Active phase**: pushing with abdominal muscles + Valsalva. Encourage comfortable maternal position. If contractions wane, oxytocin augmentation may be needed. Active phase ~1h; if >1h, consider ventouse extraction, forceps delivery, or C-section

### Stage 3
Delivery of the placenta.
- Uterus contracts to <24w size after birth
- Placenta separates from uterus, buckles; small retro-placental haemorrhage aids removal

### Monitoring in labour

> [!info] Monitoring frequency
> - Fetal heart rate: every 15 min, or continuous CTG
> - Contractions: assessed every 30 min
> - Maternal HR: every 1h
> - Maternal BP and temperature: every 4h
> - Vaginal exam: offered every 4h to check labour progression
> - Maternal urine: checked for ketones/protein every 4h

---

## Induction of labour

**Indications**
- Post-maturity pregnancy (1–2w after estimated delivery date)
- Prelabour premature rupture of membranes where labour doesn't start spontaneously
- Maternal medical problems: diabetic mother >38w, pre-eclampsia, obstetric cholestasis
- Rhesus disease
- Intrauterine fetal death

**Contraindications** (consider C-section instead)
