- Malpresentation (incl. breech)
- Fetal distress
- Placenta praevia, vasa praevia
- Cord presentation
- Pelvic tumour (e.g. cervical fibroid)

### Modified Bishop score

> [!info] Modified Bishop score
> ≥8 indicates favourable cervix (high chance of spontaneous labour or response to induction). <5: labour unlikely to start without induction.

| Score | Cervical position | Consistency | Effacement | Dilation | Fetal station |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | Posterior | Firm | 0–30% | <1cm | -3 |
| 1 | Intermediate | Intermediate | 40–50% | 1–2cm | -2 |
| 2 | Anterior | Soft | 60–70% | 3–4cm | -1, 0 |
| 3 | – | – | 80% | >5cm | +1, +2 |

### Methods of induction

- **Membrane sweep**: examining finger inserted through cervix, rotated against uterine wall to separate chorionic membrane from decidua. Can be done by midwife at antenatal clinic. Primip: offered at 40w, 41w. Multip: offered at 41w.
- **Vaginal prostaglandin E2** (dinoprostone) — pessary or gel; CTG monitoring before and for 30 min post-insertion
- **Oral prostaglandin E1** (misoprostol)
- **Maternal oxytocin infusion** (Syntocinon)
- **Amniotomy** ("breaking of waters") — rod-hook device ruptures the amniotic membrane
- **Cervical ripening balloon** — passed through endocervical canal, gently inflated to dilate cervix

**Mx by Bishop score**
- Bishop ≤6: vaginal prostaglandins or oral misoprostol; consider mechanical methods (e.g. balloon catheter) if higher hyperstimulation risk or previous C-section
- Bishop >6: amniotomy and IV oxytocin infusion

> [!danger] Complications of induction — uterine hyperstimulation (1–5%)
> Prolonged/frequent uterine contractions (tachysystole) — can cause fetal hypoxaemia/acidaemia and uterine rupture. **Mx**: remove vaginal prostaglandins, stop IV oxytocin, ± tocolysis.

---

## Pain relief in labour

**Non-pharmacological**
- Education on labour process
- Transcutaneous electrical nerve stimulation (TENS)
- Water birth (NICE-recommended; **not verified against an Australian source**; see `PENDING_GUIDELINE_CHECKS.md` B32) — water temperature must be <37.5°C to prevent maternal pyrexia

**Pharmacological**

| Option | Notes |
|---|---|
| Nitrous oxide (Entonox, 50% in O2) | CI: pneumothorax. Short onset. SE: N&V, feeling faint |
| Opiate analgesia (e.g. pethidine 50–150mg IM) | Limited pain relief, significant SE (drowsiness, N&V; short-term neonatal respiratory depression/drowsiness); give with antiemetic (e.g. cyclizine 50mg IM) if IM/IV; **CI: water birth (cannot give <2h before entering birthing pool)** |
| Pudendal nerve block (S2-4) | e.g. 8–10mL 1% lidocaine; useful for instrumental delivery |
| Local anaesthesia (lidocaine) | Injected into perineum before episiotomy/suturing |
| Epidural analgesia | Targets T10-S5 fibres; aim for L3/4 space; can top up every 2h |
| Spinal anaesthesia | Used for most C-sections (easier to site than epidural); single dose into subarachnoid space; ↑risk of profound hypotension vs epidural |
| Combined spinal-epidural | Quicker pain relief; useful for C-section with risk of prolonged procedure |

**Epidural complications**: failure to site/patchy block, hypotension, dural puncture (<1:100), post-dural puncture headache, transient/permanent nerve damage (very rare), ↑risk of operative vaginal delivery. Can cause transient fetal bradycardia — recovers with IV fluids.

> [!info] Verified against the Queensland Clinical Guideline "VTE prophylaxis in pregnancy and the puerperium" (2025), already independently confirmed in [[16_01-05_Antenatal_Care]] — **the timing below is genuinely consistent with current Australian practice**, not UK-specific: 12h from last prophylactic dose to neuraxial block/catheter removal, 24h if on therapeutic dose. The "≥4h after block siting before next LMWH dose" is a reasonable minimum, though the Queensland guideline specifies 6h from block insertion to the next dose specifically (vs 4h from catheter *removal* to the next dose) — a small but genuine distinction between these two different steps worth noting, not a wholesale correction.
> - Wait 12h after prophylactic heparin/LMWH before block insertion/catheter removal
> - Wait 24h if on therapeutic dose
> - Wait 6h after block insertion, or 4h after catheter removal, before the next LMWH dose

---

## Multiple pregnancy

**E**: Twins 1:105 pregnancies; Triplets 1:10,000 pregnancies.

**R**: previous twins, FHx, ↑maternal age, ↑ovulation induction/IVF, race/ethnic origin (e.g. Japanese, Nigerian Yoruba).

**A/P**
- Zygote = fertilised ovum
- **Dizygotic/fraternal twins**: develop from separate ova, 2 zygotes fertilised at the same time
- **Monozygotic**: develop from one zygote splitting into two embryos
  - Dichorionic & diamniotic: separate amniotic sac and placenta each
  - Monochorionic & diamniotic: shared placenta, separate amniotic sacs
  - Monochorionic & monoamniotic: shared placenta and amniotic sac

**S/smx**: large uterus, hyperemesis, >2 poles felt, multiplicity of fetal parts felt, 2 fetal heart rates heard, polyhydramnios (later on).

**Complications during pregnancy**: polyhydramnios, pre-eclampsia (30% in twins), anaemia, ↑antepartum haemorrhage risk (2° ↑abruption and placenta praevia risk from large placenta), gestational diabetes, operative delivery.

**Fetal complications**: ↑perinatal mortality, prematurity (mean gestation 37w twins, 33w triplets), growth restriction, ↑malformation rates (especially if monozygotic), twin-twin transfusion syndrome (monochorionic twins — placental vascular anastomoses, one twin donor, other recipient), fetus papyraceous (fetus dies in utero, shrinks, mummifies).

**Complications during labour**: PPH (2x risk in twins), malpresentation common, vasa praevia rupture, cord prolapse, placental abruption, cord entanglement.

**Preparation for labour**
- Aspirin >12w if other pre-eclampsia risk factors present
- Educate mother on identifying pre-term labour
- Offer elective birth at 37w (uncomplicated dichorionic twins), 36w (uncomplicated monochorionic twins), 35w + steroids (uncomplicated triplets)
- Have paediatricians (1 per baby) at delivery in case resus needed

---

## Breech presentation

**D**: baby presenting for delivery with buttocks or feet first rather than head. 25% of pregnancies at 28w; 3% at or near term.

**R**: prematurity, SGA, nulliparity, fetal congenital anomalies, previous breech delivery, uterine malformations/fibroids, abnormal amniotic fluid volume (oligo/poly), placental abnormality (e.g. placenta praevia).

> [!info] Classification
> - **Frank/extended breech** (65–70% of breeches): buttocks lead into birth canal; hips flexed, knees extended, feet near head
> - **Complete breech** (15%): buttocks present first, hips/knees flexed (sitting cross-legged)
> - **Incomplete/footling breech**: foot/feet below the breech, lowermost in birth canal; associated with ↑cord prolapse risk (5–20%)

**Mx**
- If <36w: many fetuses turn spontaneously
- If still breech at 36w: offer external cephalic version (ECV) — obstetrician applies pressure via mother's abdomen to turn baby; offered at 36w (nullip) or 37w (multip)
  - Contraindicated if: C-section delivery required, antepartum haemorrhage within last 7 days, abnormal CTG, major uterine abnormality, ruptured membranes, multiple pregnancy
- Patient education: planned C-section reduces perinatal/neonatal mortality risk; mode of birth does not influence long-term health in breech presentation

---

## Meconium-related problems

**Meconium** = baby's first poop.

**Meconium-stained liquor**
- If passed in late pregnancy, stains amniotic fluid dull green
- May be passed as part of normal labour, or a sign of distress → transfer to consultant-led unit, commence continuous FHR monitoring

> [!danger] Prelabour rupture of membranes with meconium-stained liquor
> Request immediate induction of labour in a consultant-led unit with advanced neonatal life support available.

**Meconium aspiration syndrome**

**D**: respiratory distress in the newborn due to meconium presence in the trachea. Occurs in 1:1000 deliveries.

**R**: >42w pregnancy, pregnancy-induced HTN, maternal DM, maternal drug abuse/smoking, fetal distress, oligohydramnios, chorioamnionitis.

**Mx**: only suction airway if thick meconium present in oropharynx and baby requires resuscitation — should be done by neonatologists only. Babies may require O2 therapy ± ventilation, antibiotics (e.g. ampicillin and gentamicin).

---

## Reduced fetal movements (RFM)

**Normal movements**: mothers should start feeling movements between 18–20w (first onset = "quickening"). ↑strength/frequency until ~32w, then regular pattern until delivery.

> [!danger] Any reduction is a potentially important clinical sign of impending fetal demise.

**R**: fetal growth restriction, SGA, placental insufficiency, congenital malformations.

**Initial advice**: ask mother to lie in left lateral position and focus on fetal movements for 2h; if <10 movements in that period, go to the maternity assessment unit (or ED if none locally) ASAP — requires same-day assessment.

| Gestation | Mx |
|---|---|
| <28w | Handheld Doppler to confirm fetal heartbeat. If undetectable, offer immediate US. If present, CTG ≥20min to monitor FHR. If concerns remain despite CTG, urgent US to assess abdominal circumference, estimate fetal weight, amniotic fluid volume |
| 24–28w, or <24w with previous fetal movements felt | Handheld Doppler |
| <24w with no previous fetal movements | Refer to maternal fetal medicine unit |

**P**: 70% of pregnancies with a single RFM episode have no further complications. 40–55% of women who suffer stillbirth have RFM.

---

## Retained placenta

**D**: placenta not delivered by 30 min with active management, or by 60 min with physiological birth.

**R**: previous retained placenta or uterine surgery, preterm delivery, maternal age >35, placental weight <600g, parity >5, induced labour, pethidine use during labour.

**Mx**
- Avoid excessive cord traction (cord may snap, uterus may invert)
- Check placenta is not in vagina
- Palpate abdomen, try to precipitate a contraction
- Oxytocin: encourage breastfeeding (natural ↑); 20IU oxytocin in 20mL saline into umbilical vein + clamp cord proximally
- Empty bladder
- If still not delivered after further 30 min: offer exam to assess if manual removal needed (stop if painful) → transfer to theatre for regional anaesthesia; IV access + FBC, G&S + consent

**Manual removal**: lithotomy position; one hand stabilises uterus on abdomen; other hand inserted through cervix, works around placenta to separate it, then pulls cord to remove. Ensure complete removal. Give oxytocin + 1 dose of antibiotics. If still not separated (placenta accreta), senior help ASAP.

> [!danger] Main complication is haemorrhage — do not delay manual removal if needed as this may precipitate PPH.

---

## Instrumental vaginal delivery

**Indications**
- Maternal: prolonged 2nd stage, maternal exhaustion, medical avoidance of pushing (e.g. severe cardiac disease), pushing not possible
- Fetal: suspected fetal distress, for after-coming head in breech delivery

| Instrument | Notes |
|---|---|
| Forceps | Curved blades around fetal head, traction via handles. ↓maternal effort vs ventouse. Safer for baby but ↑maternal genital tract trauma. Fetal complications (rare): facial nerve palsy, skull fractures, orbital injury, ICH |
| Ventouse | Suction cup on fetal scalp. Causes head swelling ("chignon"), resolves in 24–48h — warn mother. ↓maternal genital tract trauma but ↑fetal trauma risk; more likely to fail. Fetal complications: cephalhaematoma (common), retinal haemorrhage, scalp lacerations/avulsions (more common if >3 pulls) |

**Other notes**: avoid sequential use of instruments. Abandon use if: no descent with each pull, delivery not imminent after 3 pulls, head impacted in pelvis and difficult to deliver → emergency C-section.

---

## Caesarean section

**D**: delivery of fetus through incision in abdominal wall and uterus. 25% of nullip deliveries; <5% of multip if no previous CS. Previous CS is the greatest predictor of CS.

**Indications**: fetal compromise (e.g. fetal bradycardia, scalp pH <7.20, cord prolapse), failure to progress/failed induction, malpresentation (e.g. transverse lie), severe pre-eclampsia, IUGR with absent/reversed end-diastolic flow, twin pregnancy with non-cephalic presenting twin, placenta praevia.

**Types**: lower segment CS (LSCS) — transverse or vertical incision, lower down; most commonly used (better outcomes, lower risk). Classical — rarely used now, almost like a laparotomy.

> [!info] CS categories
> | Category | Description | Timeframe |
> |---|---|---|
> | 1 (crash) | Immediate threat to life of woman or fetus (e.g. placental abruption) | Deliver within 30 min |
> | 2 | Maternal/fetal compromise, not immediately life-threatening (e.g. failure to progress) | Deliver within 75 min |
> | 3 | Semi-elective (e.g. pre-eclampsia) | — |
> | 4 (elective) | E.g. term singleton breech; carried out after 39w unless risk changes category | — |

> [!info] Partially verified — the Category 1–4 caesarean urgency classification (30 min for Category 1, 75 min for Category 2) is not UK-specific; it's the same framework referenced in Australian state guidelines (e.g. Safer Care Victoria) and RANZCOG-aligned practice, so this structure doesn't need correcting. The specific crossmatch unit counts are genuinely more risk-stratified in Australian practice than a single fixed number per diagnosis: one NSW guideline specifically recommends group-and-hold routinely for placenta praevia, escalating to crossmatch (illustratively 2 units) specifically when the placenta is **anterior** — reflecting the genuinely higher bleeding risk from an anterior placenta at caesarean, rather than a flat "praevia = 6 units" rule. The actual unit count in any specific case should follow current local massive transfusion/patient blood management protocol and the individual risk profile (praevia type, accreta suspicion, anterior/posterior position, prior caesarean) rather than a fixed number — treat the figures below as illustrative of the general principle (bleeding risk should be actively risk-stratified, not treated as one-size-fits-all), not as a fixed Australian standard.

**Risks to mother**
- Frequent: persistent wound/abdominal discomfort (may last months), ↑risk of repeat CS, readmission, haemorrhage, infection (wound, endometritis)
- Serious: emergency hysterectomy, further surgery (e.g. curettage for retained placental tissue), ICU admission, thromboembolic disease, bladder/ureteric injury, death (1:12,000)
- Future pregnancies: ↑uterine rupture risk, ↑antepartum stillbirth risk, ↑placenta praevia/accreta risk

**Risks to baby**: lacerations (1–2:100).

**Vaginal birth after CS (VBAC)**
- Appropriate method for pregnant women ≥37w with single previous CS
- Successful in 70–75%
- Contraindications: previous uterine rupture, classical CS scar

---

## Stillbirth / intrauterine fetal death (IUFD)

> [!info] Verified — the Australian definition of stillbirth is death in utero at or after **20 weeks' gestation, or birthweight ≥400g** (already established for this project in [[17_03_Termination_of_Pregnancy_and_Miscarriage]] Miscarriage) — genuinely different from the UK's 24-week threshold, not a relabelling. Loss before this threshold is classified as miscarriage.

**D**: death in utero at or after 20 weeks' gestation (or birthweight ≥400g); loss before this threshold is classified as miscarriage. Occurs in roughly 1:200 births.

**Causes**: no cause found in 28%; placental cause 12%; ante-/intrapartum haemorrhage 11%; major congenital anomaly 9%; others (infection, HTN, renal disease, diabetes, IUGR).

**R**: multiple pregnancy, social deprivation, ↑maternal age, smoking, previous CS, IVF, obesity.

**S/smx**: ↓fetal movements.

**Ix/Dx**: handheld Doppler (RFM); US — absent heartbeat requires 2 independent practitioners to confirm; repeat US if mother requests.

**Mx**
- If mother Rh-negative: give anti-D
- Kleihauer test on all women to diagnose fetomaternal haemorrhage (screens/quantifies severity); repeat at 48h if large FMH to ensure all fetal RBCs cleared
- Advise delivery for pre-eclampsia, abruption, sepsis, coagulopathy, or membrane rupture
- Mother may deliver spontaneously or require induction
- Arrange follow-up with obstetrician to discuss cause and implications for future pregnancy

**Ix for cause**: Kleihauer + other bloods, blood culture, viral screen, MSU, thrombophilia screen, antibodies, cervical swab, fetal/placental swab + parental permission for postmortem.

*(See [[17_03_Termination_of_Pregnancy_and_Miscarriage]] Miscarriage, not repeated here.)*

---

## Placenta problems

### Placenta praevia

**D**: placenta overlying the cervical os. Found in ~0.5% of pregnancies. "Low-lying placenta" = <2cm from cervical os but not directly covering it.

**R**: previous C-section, multiparity, multiple pregnancy.

**A/P**: exact cause unknown; uterine scarring may interfere with placentation in some cases.

**S/smx**
- Shock in proportion to visible blood loss
- Lie and presentation may be abnormal
- **No pain, no uterine tenderness** ("painless praevia")
- Coagulation problems rare, fetal heart usually normal

> [!info] Gap-filled — praevia's "painless, shock proportional to visible loss" and abruption's "constant pain, shock disproportionate to visible loss" (see [[16_14-15_Obstetric_Emergencies]] Placental abruption, not repeated here) are presented as contrasting features in both entries without the anatomical mechanism explaining why. **In placenta praevia, bleeding originates from placental vessels overlying or near the cervix, disrupted as the lower uterine segment stretches and the cervix begins to efface/dilate later in pregnancy — this blood flows out externally through the cervix and vagina** ("revealed" haemorrhage) without involving the myometrium itself, so there's no tissue irritation or uterine distension to cause pain, and essentially all lost blood is externally visible, meaning shock genuinely does track with visible loss. **In placental abruption, bleeding occurs into the space between the placenta and uterine wall (retroplacental hemorrhage)** — this accumulating blood directly irritates and distends the myometrium, producing the pain and uterine tenderness/rigidity that's abruption's hallmark, and critically, a substantial proportion of this blood can remain **concealed** (trapped behind the placenta, never reaching the cervix to become externally visible) — which is precisely why shock in abruption can be dramatically out of proportion to the blood loss actually seen, given a large volume may be hidden entirely from view.

> [!danger] Ix
> US first — do **NOT** perform digital vaginal exam before US, as this may provoke haemorrhage. Usually picked up on routine 20w abdominal US; transvaginal US is more accurate and safe.

> [!info] Classical grading
> | Grade | Description |
> |---|---|
> | I | Placenta reaches lower segment but not internal os |
> | II | Reaches internal os but does not cover it |
> | III | Covers internal os before dilation, not when dilated |
> | IV (major) | Completely covers internal os |

**Mx**
- If identified at 20w: rescan at 32w; if still present, rescan q2w; final US at 36–37w to determine delivery method
- Elective C-section for grades III/IV between 37–38w; trial of vaginal delivery if grade I
- No need to limit activity/intercourse unless bleeding
- If known placenta praevia and labour starts before elective CS: emergency C-section to prevent PPH

> [!danger] Placenta praevia with bleeding
> Admit + ABCDE stabilisation. If patient cannot be stabilised, or in labour/term reached, perform emergency C-section.

### Placenta accreta

**D**: abnormal adherence of all or part of the placenta to the uterus.
- Placenta **increta** if myometrium infiltrated
- Placenta **percreta** if serosa/perimetrium infiltrated

**R**: previous C-section, placenta praevia.

**Dx**: colour Doppler US or MRI. All 3 types predispose to PPH.

**Mx**: planned delivery at 35–36w with elective C-section; G&S + crossmatch in preparation for potential haemorrhage; hysterectomy (with placenta left in situ) is standard of care, though experienced surgeons may attempt uterus-preserving surgery.

> [!warning] **These recommendations are attributed to the RCOG — a UK college — and are not confirmed as Australian practice.** This file's localisation box at the top verifies the **LMWH/regional anaesthesia timing**, the **caesarean urgency classification** and the blood-product content; **it does not cover this entry**, and a file-level "Localised for Australia" heading is easy to read as if it did. Elsewhere in the corpus an RCOG guideline is used legitimately because **RANZCOG explicitly adopts it** ([[16_06-07_Ante-Perinatal_Infections]] Varicella zoster in pregnancy says so directly) — no such adoption has been established for placenta accreta. **Confirm against RANZCOG's placenta accreta spectrum guidance**, particularly the **35–36 week delivery timing**, before treating this as the Australian standard. See `PENDING_GUIDELINE_CHECKS.md` **B58**. Found by the G34 round, 2026-08-29 — sixth instance of the partial-verification-box pattern.

### Vasa praevia

**D**: malformation of fetal vessels running through the free placental membranes.

- Risk of fetal haemorrhage when membranes rupture
- Requires C-section — urgent if fetal compromise, elective if detected antenatally

### Other placental anomalies

| Condition | Description |
|---|---|
| Placental succenturia (5%) | Separate (succenturiate) lobe away from main placenta, may fail to separate normally — can cause PPH or puerperal sepsis |
| Placenta membranacea | Thin placenta surrounds baby; predisposes to antepartum haemorrhage, PPH, IUGR |
| Velamentous insertion (1%) | Umbilical vessels travel within membranes before placental insertion |

### Other causes of antepartum haemorrhage (APH)

**D**: genital tract bleeding from 24w gestation. 3–5% of pregnancies.

- Placental abruption (**painful**)
- Placenta praevia (**painless**)
- Vasa praevia, cervical polyps, erosions/carcinomas, cervicitis, vaginitis, vulval varicosities

---

## Perineal tears + episiotomy

> [!info] Perineal tear classification
> | Degree | Description | Management |
> |---|---|---|
> | 1st | Superficial, no muscle involvement | Generally no repair required |
> | 2nd | Perineal muscle injury, does NOT involve anal sphincter | Sutures (on ward) |
> | 3rd | Anal sphincter injury — 3a: external sphincter <50% torn; 3b: >50% torn; 3c: both external and internal sphincters torn | Repair in theatre + antibiotics |
> | 4th | Anal sphincter **and** rectal mucosa injury | Repair in theatre under epidural/GA + antibiotics |

**R (for tears)**: primigravida, large babies, precipitous (very quick) labour, shoulder dystocia, forceps delivery.

**Mx**
- Grade III & IV require repair in theatre with intraoperative antibiotic cover
- Advise high-fibre diet and laxatives for 10 days to avoid constipation
- Arrange pelvic floor physiotherapy and obstetric clinic follow-up at 6–12w postnatally
- If pain or incontinence develops: refer to specialist gynae or colorectal surgeon for endoanal US or manometry

**Episiotomy**: cut to the perineum to enlarge cervical outlet for faster delivery (e.g. fetal distress). Tissues cut: vaginal epithelium, perineal skin, bulbocavernosus muscle, superficial and deep transverse perineal muscles. Complications: bleeding, infection, breakdown, haematoma formation. Post-cut Mx: ice pack, salt baths, rectal diclofenac.

---

## Postpartum physiological changes

- Uterus: 1kg → 100g (afterpains may occur while shrinking, especially during breastfeeding)
- Cervix becomes firm over 3 days; internal os closes by 3 days; external os closes by 3 weeks
- Lochia (endometrial slough, red/white cells) passed per vaginum: red → yellow → white, until 6w
- Colostrum for first 3 days → milk; breasts swollen/tender with engorgement at 3–4 days

---

## Puerperal psychosis

**E**: 1:500 births.

**S/smx**: high suicidal drive, mania, severe depression, rarely schizophrenic symptoms with delusions. Presents by 7 days postpartum in 50%, by 3 months in 90%. Sudden onset with rapid deterioration.

**Mx**: admit + refer to perinatal psychiatry team or psychiatric liaison; ideally specialist mother and baby unit if possible.

**P**: risk of recurrence in a following pregnancy is high — **published estimates range roughly 25–50%, and [[14_03_Psych_-_Psychotic_Disorders_and_Antipsychotics]] Postpartum (Puerperal) Psychosis states >50%.** The two entries gave different figures for the same fact; neither has been resolved against an Australian source — see `PENDING_GUIDELINE_CHECKS.md` **B59**. Either way it is the single strongest risk factor and the reason pre-conception perinatal psychiatry planning is offered.

> [!note] **A prior history of bipolar disorder is the other major risk factor** (~30% risk of a postpartum episode), and postpartum psychosis is increasingly understood as sitting within the bipolar spectrum — the aetiological link this entry did not carry. [[14_03_Psych_-_Psychotic_Disorders_and_Antipsychotics]] Postpartum (Puerperal) Psychosis owns the full entry, including the organic causes to exclude (postpartum sepsis, significant blood loss, eclampsia-related complications) and **PANDA**, the Australian perinatal mental health helpline. Linked by the G35 round, 2026-08-29.


<!-- ===== SOURCE: 16_14-15_Obstetric_Emergencies.md ===== -->


> [!info] Localised for Australia — eclampsia magnesium sulfate infusion timing, labetalol dosing, GBS-adjacent content, and PPH/placental abruption management all individually verified against Queensland Clinical Guidelines and Safer Care Victoria, Aug 2026. See individual entries below for detail.

## Sepsis in the puerperium

**D**: life-threatening organ dysfunction caused by a dysregulated host response to infection.

**Common causative organisms**
- Most common overall: Group A Strep and *E. coli*
- Chorioamnionitis: mixed Gram-negative and Gram-positive organisms
- UTI, preterm prolonged rupture of membranes, cervical cerclage: Gram-negative bacilli

**S/smx**: as per other infections; specifically in pregnancy — abdominal/pelvic pain, offensive vaginal discharge (smelly ≈ anaerobes; serosanguinous ≈ Strep).

> [!info] Sepsis diagnostic criteria (may develop later in pregnant women)
> - Temp >38°C or <36°C
> - HR >100
> - RR >20
> - SBP <90 or MAP <70 mmHg
> - Impaired mental state
> - ↑inflammatory markers
> - ↑lactate (>4 mmol/L)

**Ix/Mx**
- Managed similarly to sepsis in any other patient
- Sepsis is associated with preterm labour — alert neonatal unit
- If chorioamnionitis suspected: expedite delivery
- Lower threshold for ICU transfer
- In labour: continuous fetal monitoring recommended; avoid spinal/epidural analgesia — use GA for C-section

---

## Eclampsia

**D**: seizures associated with pre-eclampsia (pregnancy-induced HTN + proteinuria + tonic-clonic seizures).

**R**: as per pre-eclampsia. 1% of pre-eclampsia progresses to eclampsia.

**S/smx**: tonic-clonic seizure — may be the first presentation of eclampsia/pre-eclampsia. 38% fit antenatally, 18% during labour, 44% postnatally.

**Mx**
- Call for help ASAP; ABCDE stabilisation

> [!info] Verified against Queensland Clinical Guidelines "Management of eclampsia," Aug 2026 — **the loading dose infusion time needed a genuine correction**: Australian guidance specifies magnesium sulfate 4g IV given over **20 minutes** via a controlled infusion device — the file's original "5–10 min" is meaningfully faster than Australian practice and matters clinically, given infusing magnesium too quickly increases the risk of toxicity (respiratory depression, cardiac effects) without added seizure-control benefit. The maintenance dose (1g/hour, continuing 24 hours after birth or the last seizure) is consistent with Australian practice — that part didn't need correcting. For recurrent/ongoing seizures while magnesium is being prepared or after an inadequate response, Australian guidance offers **diazepam, clonazepam, or midazolam** as options (not diazepam alone) — check current local protocol for the preferred agent. The toxicity thresholds (RR <12/min, loss of tendon reflexes, oliguria) and calcium gluconate as the antidote are consistent with Australian practice.
>
> - Magnesium sulfate to prevent/treat seizures: 4g IV over **20 minutes** via controlled infusion device, then 1g/h for 24h; continue for 24h after last seizure or delivery
> - Treat further seizures with 2g bolus + diazepam, clonazepam, or midazolam (per local protocol)
> - Stop if RR <12/min, loss of tendon reflexes, or UO <20 mL/h
> - If MgSO4 toxicity (respiratory depression): give calcium gluconate over 10 min
>
> **BP management** — verified against Safer Care Victoria's Hypertension in Pregnancy guideline; the labetalol regimen below is consistent with Australian practice (used in the same escalating pattern by Australian state maternity services), not a UK-specific figure needing correction.
> - If >160/110 or MAP >125: labetalol 20mg IV, increasing after 10-min intervals to 40mg then 80mg
> - Switch to hydralazine if still uncontrolled (or use hydralazine first, then labetalol)

- Rule out intracranial haemorrhage
- Catheterise for hourly UO, obs every 15 min
- Restrict fluids to 80 mL/h until clinically/biochemically improving (unless haemorrhaging)
- Monitor fetal HR with CTG
- Deliver once mother is stable — C-section is quickest but vaginal delivery is not contraindicated

> [!danger] Manage 3rd stage with oxytocin only — other uterotonic drugs (e.g. ergometrine) are contraindicated in eclampsia/pre-eclampsia.

---

## Placental abruption

**D**: premature separation of a normally located placenta from the uterine wall occurring before delivery of the fetus. 1:200 pregnancies.

**R**: chronic HTN, pre-eclampsia, smoking, cocaine use, trauma, chorioamnionitis, uterine malformations, prior placental abruption, oligohydramnios.

**A**: direct or indirect trauma; cocaine use (→ vasospasm → bleeding).

**S/smx**
- Shock out of keeping with visible blood loss (see [[16_10-13_Labour_and_Delivery]] Placenta praevia for the full mechanism explaining why — concealed retroplacental bleeding vs praevia's fully externally-visible bleeding — not repeated here)
- Constant pain, tender and tense uterus
- Coagulation problems (e.g. DIC)
- Baby usually in normal lie/presentation, but fetal heart sounds may be absent or distressed

**Ix**: fetal monitoring, Hb + Hct, coagulation studies, US.

**Mx**: stabilise mother and baby.
- Resuscitate as needed; blood transfusion if evidence of anaemia
- Anti-D Ig in Rh-negative women

| Gestation & stability | Mx |
|---|---|
| >34w + baby stable | Vaginal delivery |
| >34w + baby or mother unstable | C-section delivery |
| <34w + baby & mother stable | Manage conservatively, delay delivery |
| <34w + baby or mother unstable | Deliver (vaginal or C-section) |

**P**: for baby — depends on gestational age at abruption (younger = worse outcomes). For mother — monitor subsequent pregnancies carefully (5.8% recurrence); also associated with lifetime ↑cardiovascular and cerebrovascular disease risk.

---

## Cord prolapse

**D**: presentation of the umbilical cord before the fetal presenting part.

**R**: 2nd twin, footling breech, prematurity, polyhydramnios, unengaged head, transverse or unstable lie, male fetus more than female.

**A/P**: cord compression and vasospasm from cord exposure causes fetal asphyxia.

**S/smx**: may be obvious if cord visible; however, fetal bradycardia or variable fetal heart decelerations may be the only sign.

**Ix**: fetal monitoring.

**Mx**
- Get senior help + emergency call (per local hospital emergency call protocol — Australian hospitals typically use "777" or a similar site-specific code for a MET/code blue call, not the UK's "2222"; confirm the specific number for the relevant facility)
- Minimise cord compression and vasocompression
  - Presenting fetal part may be pushed back into uterus to avoid compression
  - If cord is past the external vaginal opening: minimal handling + keep warm (prevents vasospasm)
  - Position patient "all fours" or left lateral (knees to chest) until prep for C-section
  - Tocolytics may reduce contractions
  - Retrofilling bladder with 500mL saline may help elevate presenting part (empty bladder before delivery)

---

## Shoulder dystocia

**D**: delivery requiring additional obstetric manoeuvres to release the shoulders after gentle downward traction has failed. 1:200 pregnancies.

**R**: large/post-mature fetus, maternal BMI >30, induced labours, prolonged 1st or 2nd stage, assisted vaginal delivery, previous shoulder dystocia.

> [!danger] Mx — SPEED IS KEY
> - Get senior++ help
> - Episiotomy to create space for internal manoeuvres
> - **McRoberts' manoeuvre**: flexion + abduction of maternal hips bringing thighs to abdomen (hyperflexed lithotomy); increases relative anterior-posterior pelvic angle (90% success)
> - Suprapubic pressure to displace the anterior shoulder
> - Internal manoeuvres: rotate fetal shoulders (180° to make posterior shoulder anterior), or deliver posterior shoulder first
> - Roll mother to all fours if these fail
> - Other measures: maternal symphysiotomy, Zavanelli manoeuvre (replace baby into uterus + C-section)
> - If baby dies before delivery: cut through both clavicles to allow delivery
> - Check baby for damage (e.g. Erb's palsy, fractured clavicle); beware maternal PPH or 3rd/4th degree tears

**Complications**
- Mother: PPH in 11%, perineal tears in 3.8%
- Baby: brachial plexus injuries 4–16% (1:2300 live births UK) — 10% left with permanent disability
- Common cause of litigation — parents need counselling about this risk for vaginal deliveries, and that C-section is not an option once shoulder dystocia occurs

---

## Postpartum haemorrhage (PPH)

**D**: blood loss >500mL after vaginal delivery.
- Primary PPH: within 24h of delivery
- Secondary PPH: >24h after delivery
- Massive obstetric haemorrhage: >1000–1500mL loss

> [!info] Causes of PPH — the 4 Ts
> - **T**one: uterine atony (90%)
> - **T**issue: retained products of conception (more associated with secondary PPH)
> - **T**rauma: genital tract trauma
> - **T**hrombin: clotting disorders

**R**: previous PPH or retained placenta, BMI >35, maternal Hb <85 before labour, antepartum haemorrhage, multiparity >4, maternal age >35, uterine malformations, large placenta/uterus, placental abruption, labour complications.

**Mx**
- Book mothers with risk factors for obstetric unit delivery
- Call for help — all hands on deck
- Resuscitate — ABC; intubate if ↓consciousness; crossmatch 4–6 units + transfuse 1 unit packed RBC to 1 unit FFP
- Deliver placenta; empty uterus of clots or retained tissue
- Massage uterus to generate contraction, or bimanual compression
- Drugs to contract uterus: **oxytocin 10 IU IM/IV is the first-line uterotonic in Australian practice** (per Safer Care Victoria), with **carbetocin 100mcg IM/IV** increasingly used as an alternative for all births (not just caesarean) where cost is comparable, given evidence of a reduced need for additional uterotonics and lower blood loss compared with oxytocin alone; ergometrine, misoprostol, and carboprost remain options for ongoing/refractory atony per escalating local protocol — check current local massive transfusion/PPH pathway for the specific escalation sequence used. See [[10_08_Haemonc_-_Blood_Products_and_Transfusion]] Massive Transfusion Protocol (MTP) for the general AU-verified ratio-based product delivery framework this pathway draws on, not repeated here.
- Repair vaginal or cervical tears
- If bleeding continues: exam under anaesthesia ± laparotomy; insertion of intrauterine balloon tamponade
- May require subtotal or total hysterectomy

**P**: PPH remains a leading cause of maternal mortality worldwide — global estimates attribute roughly a quarter of all maternal deaths to obstetric haemorrhage (~70,000 deaths annually, disproportionately in low- and middle-income countries). Australian maternal mortality from PPH specifically is low in absolute terms given ready access to blood products and tertiary obstetric care, but PPH remains a leading preventable contributor to severe maternal morbidity here too — the priority is timely recognition and the escalating drug/procedural sequence above, not a specific mortality figure.

---

## Uterine rupture

**D**: complete division of all 3 layers of the uterus (endometrium, myometrium, perimetrium). Uterine dehiscence = incomplete division not penetrating all layers.

**R**: previous C-section (the leading risk factor internationally, via dehiscence of the caesarean scar — the original note's "~70%" figure was specifically UK-derived; the general principle that prior caesarean is the dominant risk factor holds internationally, but the exact proportion should not be assumed to transfer directly), previous uterine rupture, previous cervical/uterine surgery, induction of labour.

**S/smx**: abnormal fetal heart rate; sudden-onset abdominal pain (may be masked by epidural); ± vaginal bleeding; baby may be partially extruded through rupture; haematuria if rupture extends into bladder; haemodynamic instability (may not have much visible vaginal blood — mostly intraperitoneal); changes in contraction patterns; postpartum pain + persistent vaginal bleeding despite uterotonics ± haematuria.

**Mx**
- Stabilise patient (resuscitate)
- Notify anaesthesia, neonatology
- Crossmatch 6 units + prep for fast transfusion
- Laparotomy — hysterectomy if no choice, or attempt uterine repair; ± bladder repair if trauma present

**P**: maternal mortality 5%, fetal mortality 30%.

---

## DIC & coagulation defects

**D**: acquired syndrome characterised by activation of coagulation pathways, resulting in intravascular thrombi formation and depletion of platelets and coagulation factors.

**A**: disease states triggering systemic coagulation activation — e.g. obstetric disorders, sepsis.

**P**: continuous generation of intravascular fibrin and consumption of procoagulants/platelets; mutual potentiation between inflammatory and coagulation pathways.

**S/smx**: oliguria, ↓BP, ↑HR, purpura fulminans, gangrene, acral cyanosis, petechiae, ecchymosis, oozing, haematuria, delirium, coma.

> [!info] Diagnostic scoring/criteria
> - ↓platelets: moderate <100 x10⁹/L, severe <50 x10⁹/L
> - ↑fibrin degradation products (e.g. D-dimer)
> - ↑prothrombin time: moderate >3s but <6s, severe >6s
> - ↓fibrinogen <2.94 mmol/L
> - Blood film may show schistocytes (microangiopathic haemolytic anaemia)

**Mx**: treat underlying cause ASAP; manage shock (O2, IV fluids); blood products — tranexamic acid, vitamin K (especially if prolonged PT), platelets, fibrinogen concentrate.

**P**: <1% mortality if due to placental abruption; 50–80% mortality if due to infection.

---

## Amniotic fluid embolism

**D**: condition arising from entry of amniotic fluid or fetal cells into maternal circulation.

**R**: multiple pregnancy, maternal age >35, C-section, instrumentation, eclampsia, polyhydramnios, placental abruption, uterine rupture, induction of labour.

**A/P**: likely immune-mediated (over)reaction to fetal cells; likened to an anaphylactic-like response.

**S/smx**: dyspnoea, chest pain, hypoxia, respiratory arrest → ARDS (may be first sign); hypotension; fetal distress; seizures (20%); ↓consciousness; cardiac arrest. Almost all develop DIC within 48h.

**Mx**
- Prevent death from respiratory failure: high-flow 100% O2; call anaesthetist ASAP (intubation/ventilation may be necessary); transfer to ICU ASAP
- CPR if indicated; deliver baby via C-section (aids maternal resuscitation)
- Monitor for fetal distress
- If hypotensive: IV fluids, dobutamine; pulmonary artery catheterisation to guide Mx; after initial hypotension corrected, give only maintenance fluid required (avoid pulmonary oedema from ARDS)
- Manage DIC with fresh whole blood, packed RBC, or FFP

**P**: most mortality occurs within the first hour; 26–61% mortality. If mother survives, 85% have neurological damage. Request autopsy ASAP.


<!-- ===== SOURCE: 16_16-17_Contraception.md ===== -->


> [!info] Verified — **UKMEC is directly adopted by RANZCOG for Australian contraceptive practice**, not a UK-specific system requiring translation: the RANZCOG/FSRH joint "Contraception Clinical Guideline" explicitly incorporates UKMEC categories, and SA Health's own Community Pharmacy OCP protocol references UKMEC directly. The UKMEC categories and thresholds below are genuinely current Australian practice, not something needing an "Australian equivalent" swap. One minor, genuine product-availability difference worth knowing: drospirenone-containing POP is available in Australia specifically (not the UK), while desogestrel and levonorgestrel POPs are only available in New Zealand within this region — a detail relevant to which specific POP formulation is prescribable locally, though the underlying UKMEC eligibility framework applies the same way regardless of which formulation is used.

## Combined hormonal contraception

### Combined oral contraceptive pill (COCP)
- **MOA**: inhibits ovulation
- 99% effective if used correctly

### Combined contraceptive patch (Evra patch)
- **MOA**: inhibits ovulation
- 99% effective if used correctly

### Combined contraceptive ring (NuvaRing — etonogestrel + ethinylestradiol)
- **MOA**: inhibits ovulation

---

### COCP — contraindications (UKMEC)

> [!info] UKMEC categories
> - **UKMEC 4** (unacceptable risk): current breast cancer; PMH of VTE, MI, or stroke; antiphospholipid antibodies +ve; uncontrolled hypertension; migraine with aura (↑stroke risk); >35yo + smoking ≥15 cigs/day; breastfeeding <6w postpartum; major surgery with prolonged immobilisation
> - **UKMEC 3** (risks outweigh benefits): PMH or FHx of breast cancer (or gene mutation carrier); FHx of VTE (1st-degree relative); controlled hypertension; >35yo + smoking <15 cigs/day; BMI >35; immobility (incl. wheelchair use); current gallbladder disease
> - **UKMEC 2**: benefits outweigh risks
> - **UKMEC 1**: no restrictions

**Risks**: ↑VTE, stroke, MI risk; ↑breast and cervical cancer risk.

**Benefits**: ↓ovarian, endometrial, and colorectal cancer risk; often helps acne, ovarian cysts; can make periods regular, lighter, less painful.

### How to take COCP
- Ideally start within first 5 days of period → no additional contraception required
- If started at any other point in the cycle: use alternative contraception (e.g. condoms) for first 7 days
- Take at the same time every day
- **Method 1**: 21 days + 7-day pill-free break (mimics physiological menses; no medical benefit from withdrawal bleed — based on patient preference)
- **Method 2** (tricycling): 3x 21-day packs, then 4- or 7-day pill-free break
- Intercourse during pill-free period only safe if next pack started on time

**Special situations**
- Vomiting within 2h of taking COCP: repeat dose
- Antibiotic use: generally OK together; caution only with enzyme-inducing antibiotics (e.g. rifampicin)
- Check the **AMH (Australian Medicines Handbook)** or local formulary for drug interactions

### Missed COCP pills

| Scenario | Action |
|---|---|
| 1 missed pill (any time in cycle) | Take last pill ASAP (even 2 in one day), continue daily. No emergency contraception required |
| ≥2 missed pills, week 1 | Take last pill ASAP; emergency contraception if UPSI in pill-free week or days 1–7 |
| ≥2 missed pills, week 2 | Take last pill ASAP; emergency contraception not required |
| ≥2 missed pills, week 3 | Finish all pills in current pack, then omit pill-free interval |

General advice: use barrier method (e.g. condoms) until pills taken 7 days in a row.

### How to use combined patch
- 1 patch per week for 3 weeks, then patch-free week (4-week cycle)

**Missed patches**

| Timing | Action |
|---|---|
| Week 1 or 2, delay <48h | Change ASAP, no further precautions |
| Week 1 or 2, delay >48h | Change ASAP + barrier method for 7 days; emergency contraception if UPSI during patch-free interval or last 5 days |
| Week 3 | Remove ASAP + apply new patch ASAP (skip patch-free week); no further precautions needed |
| Start of week 1, delayed | Barrier contraception for first 7 days |

- Less effective in patients >90kg

### How to use combined ring
- Insert within first 5 days of cycle start → no additional contraception needed
- If inserted at other times: barrier contraception for first 7 days
- 1 ring lasts 3 weeks, then remove → ring-free week → start next cycle
- Check ring is present regularly; can be kept in during sex or tampon use
- Expelled rings: manage similarly to patches

---

## Progestogen-only pill (POP / "mini pill")
- **MOA**: thickens cervical mucus
- 99% effective if used correctly

**Side effects**: irregular vaginal bleeding (common).

**How to take**
- Ideally within first 5 days of period → no additional contraception required
- If started at other times: alternative contraception for first 2 days
- No pill-free break; take at same time every day

**Missed pills**
- <3h late: continue as normal
- >3h late: take missed pill ASAP (even 2 in a day) + barrier contraception for next 48h
- **Desogestrel (Cerazette)**: 12h grace period

**Special situations**: diarrhoea/vomiting — repeat dose ± barrier contraception if "≥3h late" equivalent; check with pharmacist for enzyme-inducing drug interactions.

---

## Injectable contraceptive — Medroxyprogesterone acetate (Depo Provera)
- **MOA**: inhibits ovulation and thickens cervical mucus

**How to use**: IM injection every 12–14 weeks; beyond 14 weeks, barrier contraception must be used.

**Side effects**: irregular or heavy bleeding (may need COCP initially to manage this), headache, nausea, mastalgia; ↑osteoporosis risk; not easily reversible — fertility may take up to 1 year to return to baseline.

**Contraindications**: UKMEC4 — current breast cancer. UKMEC3 — stroke, MI, unexplained vaginal bleeding, PMH of breast cancer, severe liver cirrhosis, liver cancer.

---

## Implantable contraceptive — Nexplanon/Implanon (etonogestrel)
- **MOA**: inhibits ovulation and thickens cervical mucus
- Most effective form of contraception

**How to use**: if inserted in first 5 days of cycle, no additional contraception needed; if inserted after, use barrier contraception for first 7 days. Effective for 3 years.

---

## Copper intrauterine device (IUD)
- **MOA**: ↓sperm motility and survival

## Intrauterine system (IUS) — releasing levonorgestrel
- Mirena (8y), Jaydess (3y), Kyleena (5y)
- **MOA**: prevents endometrial proliferation, thickens cervical mucus

**Side effects (IUS)**: bleeding/spotting initially, becoming lighter — some women become amenorrhoeic; infection (especially first 3w after insertion); expulsion (1 in 20 risk, ↑risk in first 3 months); rarely uterine perforation (more common in breastfeeding women); relative ↑ectopic pregnancy risk vs other contraceptives (overall risk still very low, as most pregnancies are prevented).

**How to use (IUS)**: inserted in clinic; teach patient to check placement; barrier contraception for first 7 days after insertion.

**Side effects (copper IUD)**: heavier, longer, more painful periods; similar risks to IUS — infection, expulsion, uterine perforation, relative ↑ectopic pregnancy risk.

**How to use (copper IUD)**: inserted in clinic; effective immediately after insertion; effective for 5–10 years.

---

## Condoms
- **MOA**: physical barrier
- Only method that protects against STIs
- 98% effective if used correctly, lower efficacy in younger people due to incorrect use

---

## Emergency contraception

### Levonorgestrel
- Single dose = 1.5mg (double dose if BMI >26 or weight >70kg)
- Efficacy 84% (more effective the sooner taken after UPSI)
- Can be taken up to 72h after UPSI
- If vomiting within 3h of taking, repeat dose
- Can be used more than once in a menstrual cycle if needed
- Less effective if taken after ovulation
- SE: menstrual cycle disturbance, vomiting (1%)
- Can start hormonal contraception immediately afterward
- No issues with breastfeeding

### Ulipristal acetate
- Single dose = 30mg
- Can be taken up to 120h after UPSI; can be used more than once per cycle
- Avoid in patients with severe asthma
- Less effective if taken after ovulation
- Hormonal contraception (pill, patch, ring): (re)start 5 days after ulipristal — combined use with levonorgestrel not recommended
- Breastfeeding: delay for 1 week after taking

### Copper IUD
- Most effective method of emergency contraception — should be offered first (99% effective)
- Must be inserted within 5 days of UPSI, or up to 5 days after likely ovulation date (whichever is later)
- Can be given with prophylactic antibiotics if patient is high STI risk
- Can remain in situ for long-term contraception

> [!info] FSRH guidelines on emergency contraception are directly relevant to Australian practice, consistent with FSRH/UKMEC guidance being adopted by RANZCOG more broadly (see the note at the top of this file) — no separate "Australian equivalent" exists or is needed here.

---

## Postpartum contraception

- Contraception required after day 21 postpartum

| Method | Postpartum guidance |
|---|---|
| POP | Can start any time postpartum; if started after day 21, use barrier contraception for first 2 days. Safe for breastfeeding |
| COCP | **Contraindicated in first 6 weeks postpartum (↑VTE risk)**; benefits > risks between 6w–6mo; use barrier contraception for first 7 days when started |
| IUS | Can insert within 48h of childbirth (no additional contraception needed) or after 4w postpartum (barrier for 7 days); can insert during C-section |
| Copper IUD | Can insert within 48h of childbirth, or after 4w postpartum (effective immediately); can insert during C-section |
| Lactational amenorrhoea | 98% effective if fully breastfeeding with no supplementary feeds, amenorrhoeic, and <6mo postpartum |

---

## Contraception for specific populations

### Age >40
- Most methods OK
- **COCP**: slight ↑risk in >40yo, consider ↓dose; benefits include maintaining bone mineral density and ↓menopausal symptoms; can continue up to 50yo, then switch to non-hormonal or progestogen-only method
- **POP, implant, IUS**: continue for ≥1y with amenorrhoea, stop when FSH ≥30µL or at 55yo; if not amenorrhoeic, investigate abnormal bleeding pattern
- **Depo-Provera**: small loss in bone mineral density (usually recovers after stopping); same stop criteria as above
- **Non-hormonal methods**: if <50yo, stop after 2y amenorrhoea; if ≥50yo, stop after 1y amenorrhoea

### Obesity
- COCP: UKMEC3 if BMI ≥35 (risks > benefits); UKMEC2 if BMI 30–34 (benefits > risks)
- Combined patch: less effective if >90kg
- Oral contraception cannot be used in patients who have had gastric sleeve, bypass, or duodenal switch surgery

### Epilepsy

| Drug class | Guidance |
|---|---|
| Phenytoin, carbamazepine, barbiturates, primidone, topiramate, oxcarbazepine | No restrictions: Depo-Provera, copper IUD, IUS. Benefits > risks: Nexplanon implant. Risks > benefits: COCP, POP |
| Lamotrigine | No restrictions: POP, implant, Depo-Provera, copper IUD, IUS. Risks > benefits: COCP |

### Transgender and non-binary people

**Assigned female at birth (AFAB) / with a uterus**
- POP and non-hormonal methods most effective — do not affect testosterone therapy
- Testosterone therapy does **not** reliably prevent pregnancy
- If pregnancy occurs, testosterone therapy is contraindicated (teratogenic)
- If on testosterone, oestrogen-containing regimens not recommended (interference)
- All forms of emergency contraception are OK if needed

**Assigned male at birth (AMAB) / with male reproductive organs**
- ↓sperm production with oestradiol, GnRH analogues, finasteride, cyproterone
- **Not** a reliable means of preventing pregnancy — use condoms


<!-- ===== SOURCE: 17_01_FGM__Amenorrhoea__PCOS.md ===== -->


> [!info] Localised for Australia — FGM legal framework rebuilt around South Australian legislation (Criminal Law Consolidation Act 1935 (SA)) specifically, with the broader national picture (all states/territories criminalise FGM independently) as context, verified Aug 2026. See the FGM entry below for full detail.

## Female genital mutilation (FGM)

**D**: removal or partial removal of external female genitalia, or injury to other internal female genital organs.

> [!danger] Legal status — South Australia specifically (Criminal Law Consolidation Act 1935 (SA), ss 33/33A/33B), verified Aug 2026; **FGM is illegal in every Australian state and territory**, via each jurisdiction's own legislation, not a single national law — this is genuinely different from the UK's single national framework, similar to the pattern seen with abortion law.
> - FGM is a crime under SA legislation, with a maximum penalty of 7 years imprisonment (penalties vary by state — up to 21 years in NSW following a 2014 amendment).
> - **Consent is not a defence** — neither the affected person's own consent, nor a parent's or guardian's consent, provides any legal defence.
> - **Extraterritorial provisions apply**: it is a specific offence under SA law to take a child from the state, or arrange for a child to be taken from the state, with intent that they undergo FGM — this applies even if the procedure itself occurs entirely overseas, given Australian FGM law extends to acts committed outside Australia against Australian residents.
> - SA courts can make protective orders under the Children and Young People (Safety) Act 2017 (SA) where a child is assessed to be at risk of being removed from the state for FGM — including orders preventing a specified person from removing the child, and orders for the child's passport to be held by the court.
> - **It is illegal not to report FGM in Australia** — genuinely broader than a UK-style "engage safeguarding" framing; every Australian has a responsibility to report suspected FGM or planned removal for FGM to police, and this can be done anonymously.
> - Engage child safeguarding for any suspected case, consistent with the general principle already established elsewhere in this project.
> - A genuine, honestly-reported practical nuance: despite criminalisation across all jurisdictions, prosecutions under Australian FGM law remain rare in practice — worth knowing as a real gap between the law as written and enforcement in practice, not something to gloss over.

- Traditionally practised in parts of Africa, India, and Indonesia — not limited to any particular cultural or religious group.

> [!info] WHO classification
> | Type | Description |
> |---|---|
> | I | Partial/total removal of clitoris and/or prepuce |
> | II | Partial/total removal of clitoris and labia minora ± excision of labia majora |
> | III (infibulation) | Narrowing of vaginal orifice with creation of a covering seal by cutting and appositioning the labia minora and/or majora ± clitoral excision |
> | IV | Any other harmful non-medical procedure to female genitalia (e.g. pricking, piercing) |

**Complications**
- Acute: death, blood loss, sepsis, pain, urinary retention, infections (HIV, hepatitis, tetanus)
- Long-term: apareunia (inability to have intercourse), superficial dyspareunia, anorgasmia, sexual dysfunction, chronic pain, scarring, slow urination, UTIs
- Maternal (in pregnancy/labour): PPH, ↑risk of C-section, episiotomy, fistula, difficulty examining/catheterising

**Mx**
- Defibulation (reconstructive surgery of infibulated scar) — may be done antenatally; if not done antenatally, deliver in a unit with emergency obstetric care available. Offer epidural if vaginal exam poorly tolerated or episiotomy anticipated.
- Screen for hepatitis C
- Repair post-delivery should control bleeding

> [!danger] Re-infibulation is illegal.

---

## Normal menstrual cycle (physiology primer)

| Day | Hormonal change | Ovarian cycle | Uterine cycle |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0–4 | — | — | Menses |
| 4–14 | LH/FSH stimulate follicular maturation; oestradiol secreted by maturing follicle | Follicular phase: maturation of dominant follicle into Graafian follicle (mainly FSH-driven) | Proliferative phase; laying down new endometrial layer (oestrogen-driven) |
| 14 | GnRH surge → LH surge just before ovulation | **Ovulation** | — |
| 14–28 | Progesterone secreted by corpus luteum; LH/FSH levels decrease | Luteal phase: post-ovulation corpus luteum (LH/FSH helps corpus luteum formation) | Secretory phase: endometrium receptive to implantation (progesterone-supported) |

---

## Primary amenorrhoea

**D**: failure to start menstruating by:
- 15yo in girls with normal secondary sexual characteristics, OR
- 13yo in girls with no secondary sexual characteristics

**A/P**

*In those with normal sexual characteristics*
- Physiological causes
- Genito-urinary malformations (e.g. imperforate hymen)
- Endocrine disorders: hyper/hypothyroidism, hyperprolactinaemia, Cushing's syndrome

*In those with no secondary sexual characteristics — due to primary ovarian insufficiency*
- Chromosomal irregularities (e.g. Turner syndrome)
- Hypothalamic-pituitary dysfunction (e.g. stress, weight loss)

**Ix**
- Exclude pregnancy (bHCG)
- FBC, U&Es, coeliac screen, thyroid function test
- Gonadotrophins: ↓LH/FSH ≈ hypothalamic cause; ↑LH/FSH ≈ ovarian problem or gonadal dysgenesis (e.g. Turner's)
- Prolactin, oestradiol
- Androgen — may be ↑ in PCOS

**Mx**: treat underlying cause. If primary ovarian insufficiency is due to gonadal dysgenesis, patient may benefit from HRT.

---

## Secondary amenorrhoea

**D**: cessation of menstruation:
- For 3–6 months in women with previously normal and regular menses, OR
- For 6–12 months in women with previous oligomenorrhoea

### Causes

*Without features of androgen excess*
- Physiological causes (pregnancy, lactation, menopause)
- Hypothalamic dysfunction (chronic illness/stress, excessive exercise)
- Hyper/hypothyroidism
- Primary ovarian insufficiency (e.g. chemo, radiotherapy)
- Sheehan syndrome (postpartum hypopituitarism due to pituitary necrosis)
- Asherman syndrome (intrauterine adhesions)

*With features of androgen excess* (hirsutism, acne, virilisation)
- PCOS
- Cushing's syndrome
- Late-onset congenital adrenal hyperplasia
- Androgen-secreting tumours of the ovary or adrenal gland

**Ix**: as per primary amenorrhoea.

**Mx**: exclude pregnancy, lactation, and menopause (if ≥40yo); treat underlying cause.

---

## Polycystic ovarian syndrome (PCOS)

**D**: condition of ovarian dysfunction involving hyperandrogenism, oligomenorrhoea, and polycystic ovaries.

**R**: FHx, early onset of pubic/axillary hair and apocrine sweat gland development (adrenarche), obesity.

**A/P**: unknown; possible inheritance and insulin resistance components; overlaps with metabolic syndrome.

**S/smx**
- Obesity (PCOS does not *cause* obesity, but many women with PCOS are obese)
- Subfertility and infertility
- Menstrual disturbances: oligomenorrhoea and amenorrhoea
- Features of hyperandrogenism: hirsutism, acne
- Acanthosis nigricans (due to insulin resistance)

> [!info] Rotterdam diagnostic criteria (≥2 of 3)
> - Polycystic ovaries (≥12 follicles, or ovarian volume >10cm³ on US)
> - Oligo-ovulation or anovulation
> - Clinical and/or biochemical signs of hyperandrogenism (e.g. ↑LH:FSH ratio)

**Ix**
- Pelvic US for ovarian cysts
- Baseline bloods: FSH, LH, TSH, prolactin, testosterone, SHBG (prolactin/testosterone may be normal or mildly elevated; SHBG normal to low in PCOS)
- Check for impaired glucose tolerance

**Mx**: *weight loss* is central.
- **Hirsutism and acne**: COCP (third generation) — balance against VTE risk; if no response, trial of topical eflornithine or other drugs under specialist supervision
- **Infertility**: clomifene (induces ovulation), metformin, or combination; ovarian drilling for those not responding to clomifene; COCP to control bleeding and ↓endometrial cancer risk


<!-- ===== SOURCE: 17_02_Menorrhagia__PMS__Menopause__HRT.md ===== -->


> [!info] Localisation status — the menopause age and HRT/MHT risk profile have been verified against the Australasian Menopause Society (AMS), Aug 2026 — see the [!info] notes under each section below. Menorrhagia and PMS management (Mirena IUS, COCP, tranexamic/mefenamic acid, SSRIs) reflect internationally standard drug choices not requiring AU-specific correction.

## Abnormal Uterine Bleeding — Approach and DDx

> [!note] Gap-filled from CSV — four separate rows in the Gynaecology & Breast category ("Abnormal vaginal bleeding," "Intermenstrual bleeding," "Post-coital bleeding," all Medium yield, and "The menstrual cycle and its disorders: ... Abnormal uterine bleeding ..." High yield) had no consolidating entry. The individual *causes* were all built across the 17_ files, but nothing gave the approach to a patient who presents with the *symptom* — which is what an OSCE or MCQ actually tests. Built as an index and approach that points to the existing disease entries rather than duplicating them. Verified against RANZCOG C-Gyn 6 "Investigation of intermenstrual and postcoital bleeding," the Cancer Council Australia clinical pathway "Investigation of people with abnormal vaginal bleeding," and the FIGO PALM-COEIN system, Aug 2026.

- **D:** bleeding from the uterus that is abnormal in *volume, regularity, timing, or duration*. The clinically useful sub-patterns, because each carries a different differential and urgency:
  - **Heavy menstrual bleeding (menorrhagia)** — see Menorrhagia below.
  - **Intermenstrual bleeding (IMB)** — bleeding between otherwise normal periods.
  - **Postcoital bleeding (PCB)** — bleeding after intercourse.
  - **Postmenopausal bleeding (PMB)** — any bleeding ≥12 months after the last period. **Treat as endometrial cancer until proven otherwise** (see [[17_09_Cervical__Vaginal_and_Endometrial_Cancer]] Endometrial cancer, not repeated here).

**First question, always: is she pregnant?** A urinary/serum βhCG comes before any other reasoning in a person of reproductive age, because it redirects the entire differential to ectopic pregnancy and miscarriage (see [[17_04_Ectopic_Pregnancy_and_GTD]] and [[17_03_Termination_of_Pregnancy_and_Miscarriage]] Miscarriage, not repeated here).

**DDx — the FIGO PALM-COEIN system.** Worth actually learning as a structure rather than a mnemonic to recite: the **PALM** half is structural and therefore *imageable or biopsiable*, the **COEIN** half is non-structural and diagnosed on history and bloods. That split is the whole point — it tells you which arm of the work-up will find the answer.

| | Cause | Where it is built |
|---|---|---|
| **P** | Polyp (endometrial or cervical) | [[17_09_Cervical__Vaginal_and_Endometrial_Cancer]] Endometrial hyperplasia (adjacent); cervical polyps commonly incidental on speculum |
| **A** | Adenomyosis | [[17_05_PID__Endometriosis__Fibroids]] Adenomyosis |
| **L** | Leiomyoma (fibroids) | [[17_05_PID__Endometriosis__Fibroids]] Fibroids (uterine leiomyomata) |
| **M** | Malignancy and hyperplasia | [[17_09_Cervical__Vaginal_and_Endometrial_Cancer]] Cervical cancer, Endometrial cancer, Endometrial hyperplasia |
| **C** | Coagulopathy | [[10_07_Haemonc_-_Platelet_and_Clotting_Disorders__Neutropaenia]] Von Willebrand disease (vWD) — the one most often missed; consider it in heavy bleeding since menarche |
| **O** | Ovulatory dysfunction | [[17_01_FGM__Amenorrhoea__PCOS]] Polycystic ovarian syndrome (PCOS); also thyroid disease, hyperprolactinaemia, perimenopause, the extremes of reproductive age |
| **E** | Endometrial (primary local haemostatic disorder) | a diagnosis of exclusion once structural causes are excluded |
| **I** | Iatrogenic | hormonal contraception (breakthrough bleeding, especially in the first 3 months), missed pills, anticoagulants, tamoxifen, copper IUD |
| **N** | Not otherwise classified | e.g. arteriovenous malformation, caesarean scar niche |

**Also consider, and easy to forget because they are not uterine at all:** infection — chlamydia/gonorrhoea cervicitis and PID (see [[17_05_PID__Endometriosis__Fibroids]] Pelvic inflammatory disease (PID)) — cervical ectropion, atrophic vaginitis in the postmenopausal patient, trauma, and a retained foreign body (including a forgotten tampon).

> [!danger] Red flags — these change the urgency, not just the work-up
> - **Any postmenopausal bleeding**, however slight or however brief.
> - **Persistent or recurrent PCB or IMB** — these are the two symptoms that specifically prompt exclusion of genital tract malignancy.
> - A visible cervical lesion on speculum — refer regardless of any test result.
> - Haemodynamic compromise, or bleeding with pregnancy-related pain.

**Ix — the sequence matters more than the list:**
1. **βhCG** first, as above.
2. **Speculum and bimanual examination.** Not optional in IMB or PCB — the point is to *see* the cervix. A cervical cancer can be visible when every test is still pending.
3. **Cervical screening (co-test — HPV plus liquid-based cytology).** The genuinely Australia-specific point an intern must not get wrong: in a person with abnormal vaginal bleeding this is a **symptomatic co-test**, ordered on the basis of the symptom, and it is done **irrespective of when their last routine screening test was due or what it showed** — do not defer it to the National Cervical Screening Program schedule (see [[17_09_Cervical__Vaginal_and_Endometrial_Cancer]] Cervical cancer screening for the routine programme itself, not repeated here). Equally important: **a negative co-test does not close the case** — persistent or recurrent PCB, unexplained persistent IMB, or any PMB still require referral to exclude genital tract malignancy.
4. **Endocervical/vaginal swabs or first-pass urine NAAT** for chlamydia and gonorrhoea, particularly in younger patients with IMB or PCB, where cervicitis is a common and eminently treatable cause.
5. **FBC** (*why:* quantifies the consequence of the bleeding and can itself change urgency; *what:* iron-deficiency anaemia) and **ferritin**. **Coagulation screen and von Willebrand studies** if bleeding has been heavy since menarche, or there is a personal/family bleeding history.
6. **TFTs** if there are ovulatory-dysfunction features.
7. **Transvaginal ultrasound** — the structural arm of PALM: assesses endometrial thickness, fibroids, polyps, adenomyosis, adnexae.
8. **Endometrial sampling (Pipelle) ± hysteroscopy** where endometrial pathology must be excluded — postmenopausal bleeding, and premenopausal patients with risk factors for hyperplasia/malignancy (prolonged anovulation, obesity, diabetes, unopposed oestrogen, tamoxifen, family history) or bleeding persisting despite treatment.

**Mx:** treat the cause found — follow the linked entry above. Where the work-up is negative and bleeding is heavy but ovulatory, management is as per Menorrhagia below (Mirena IUS, tranexamic acid, mefenamic acid, COCP), not repeated here.

## Menorrhagia

**D**: heavy menstrual bleeding that interferes with quality of life.

**Causes**
- Dysfunctional uterine bleeding (menorrhagia without underlying pathology)
- Anovulatory cycles
- Uterine fibroids
- Hypothyroidism
- Intrauterine devices (copper coil)
- Pelvic inflammatory disease
- Bleeding disorders

**Ix**
- FBC, haematinics if anaemic, TSH, cervical smear, STI screen (as indicated by history)
- If risk factors for endometrial cancer or history suggestive of endometrial pathology: refer for outpatient hysteroscopy ± biopsy
- Imaging: US

**Mx**
- **Mirena IUS**: ↓bleeding via intrauterine levonorgestrel release (causes endometrial atrophy); SE: irregular bleeding for first 4–6 months + progestogenic effects
- **COCP**: effective in ↓bleeding but many contraindications
- **3rd line**: IM progestogens (e.g. medroxyprogesterone)
- If patient does not require contraception: mefenamic acid (useful if dysmenorrhoea also present) or tranexamic acid — start on first day of period
- **Surgery**: indicated for fibroids >3cm, inadequate response to previous treatment, or declined medical Mx — endometrial ablation, myomectomy, hysterectomy

---

## Premenstrual syndrome (PMS)

**D**: repetitive, cyclical, physical and behavioural symptoms occurring in the luteal phase of the normal menstrual cycle.

**A/P**: serotonin and GABA implicated.

**S/smx**: anxiety, stress, fatigue, mood swings, bloating, breast pain.

**Mx**
- **Mild**: lifestyle changes — regular sleep, exercise, smoking cessation, ↓alcohol; regular, frequent, small, balanced meals rich in complex carbohydrates
- **Moderate**: new-generation COCP (e.g. Yasmin)
- **Severe**: SSRI — can be taken continuously or only during luteal phase (e.g. days 15–28 of cycle)

### Premenstrual dysphoric disorder (PMDD)
Most severe form of PMS.

> [!info] DSM-5 criteria
> **≥1 of the following must be present:**
> - Mood swings, sudden sadness
> - Anger, irritability
> - Sense of hopelessness, depressed mood, self-critical thoughts
> - Tension, anxiety, feeling on edge
>
> **Plus ≥5 total symptoms**, including:
> - Difficulty concentrating
> - Change in appetite
> - Diminished interest in usual activities
> - Easy fatiguability, ↓energy
> - Feeling overwhelmed
> - Breast tenderness (cyclical mastalgia), bloating, weight gain, joint/muscle aches
> - Sleeping too much or not enough

---

## Menopause

**D**: period of waning fertility leading up to the last period.

> [!info] Verified against the Australasian Menopause Society (AMS), Aug 2026 — Australian average menopause age is **51 years**, essentially the same as the UK figure (52yo) — not a meaningful difference, but stated as the AMS's own figure rather than a UK statistic. AMS specifically notes most women become menopausal between 50–54 years, with <40 classified as premature menopause and 41–45 as early menopause — terminology worth using consistently with the rest of this entry.
> Average age in Australia is 51yo (AMS).

- Retrospective diagnosis when menses has ceased for ≥12 consecutive months, not attributable to other reasons
- **Premature menopause**: <40yo
- **Perimenopause**: period marked by menstrual irregularity, ending 12 months after final menstrual period

**R**: 40–60yo (tends to occur earlier — in 40s — in Asian women), cancer treatment, smoking (2y earlier), ovarian surgery.

**A**: natural process triggered by declining oocyte numbers.

**P**: ↓ovarian production of progesterone, oestradiol, and testosterone → various symptoms.

**S/smx**
- Vasomotor (80%): hot flushes (may occur every few minutes for >10y), night sweats, palpitations
- Urogenital changes (35%): vaginal dryness/atrophy, urinary frequency, breast/skin atrophy
- Psychological: 10% have anxiety and depression ± short-term memory impairment
- Long-term complications: ↑cardiovascular disease risk, ↑osteoporosis risk

**Mx**
- Confirm menopause (rule out thyroid and psychiatric problems)
- Diet and exercise changes
- Menorrhagia: Mirena coil; consider referral for hysteroscopy if symptoms suggest endometrial pathology
- Use contraception until >1y amenorrhoea if >50yo, >2y if <50yo
- Vaginal dryness: oestrogen cream 0.1% PV nightly for 2w, then 2x/week as required
- HRT (see below)

---

## Hormone replacement therapy (HRT)

**D**: oestrogen + progesterone replacement.

**Indications**
- Menopausal symptoms: vasomotor, mood disorders, urogenital symptoms
- Premature menopause: primarily for osteoporosis prevention — continue until 50yo

**Benefits**
- ↓vasomotor symptoms via oestrogen — benefit by ~4w, max effect at 3 months
- Urogenital symptoms — benefit may take several months; continue long-term
- ↓osteoporotic fracture risk — sustained/lifelong treatment necessary
- ↓colorectal cancer risk

**Risks**

> [!info] Verified against the Australasian Menopause Society (AMS), Aug 2026 — **the figures below are genuinely consistent with current AMS risk communication**, not UK-specific numbers needing replacement; AMS independently cites the same ~2.3% per-year-of-use breast cancer risk figure and describes it as "very small" in absolute terms, similar in magnitude to other common risk factors like being overweight. AMS-specific framing worth adding: **no increased cardiovascular risk if MHT (AMS's preferred term — Menopausal Hormone Therapy, used interchangeably with HRT) is started within 10 years of menopause onset or before age 60** — the "timing hypothesis," a genuinely important nuance the original note doesn't capture; VTE risk with patches/gels is minimal-to-none, while tablets roughly double the (still very low) baseline risk — consistent with the "lower risk with patches" note below.
> - Breast cancer risk ↑ by 2.3% per year of use, dependent on duration and type of HRT; risk ↓ to baseline 5y after stopping. Highest risk with combined HRT (not seen in those starting HRT for premature menopause); different progestogens carry different risk levels — micronised progesterone and dydrogesterone appear to carry lower risk than synthetic progestogens such as norethisterone
> - Endometrial cancer (RR 2.3) if unopposed oestrogen used in those with a uterus; risk persists ≥5y after stopping
> - ↑VTE risk (≥2x) with oral tablets, though absolute risk remains small (~1 extra case per 1,000 women); minimal or no increased VTE risk with patches or gels; most likely in the first year of use

**Contraindications**
- Breast cancer (past or present)
- Oestrogen-dependent cancer
- Undiagnosed vaginal bleeding
- Untreated endometrial hyperplasia
- Previous idiopathic or current VTE (unless already anticoagulated)
- Active or recent arterial thromboembolic disease (e.g. MI)
- Active liver disease with abnormal LFTs
- Pregnancy
- Thrombophilic disorder


<!-- ===== SOURCE: 17_03_Termination_of_Pregnancy_and_Miscarriage.md ===== -->


> [!info] Localised for Australia — verified against the Termination of Pregnancy Act 2021 (SA) directly (via SA Legislation and the SA Law Handbook), and cross-checked against other states' legislation, Aug 2026. **Every Australian state and territory has decriminalised abortion**, but — genuinely unlike the UK's single national framework — there is **no single Australian abortion law**; each state/territory has its own act with its own gestational limit and process, so the specific threshold below is jurisdiction-dependent and must be checked against the relevant state/territory for any patient outside SA.

## Termination of pregnancy (TOP)

> [!danger] Legal framework — South Australia specifically (Termination of Pregnancy Act 2021), given this is the directly relevant jurisdiction for a Flinders University student; other states differ and are summarised below.
> - **On request up to 22 weeks and 6 days** — the pregnant person's informed consent alone is sufficient; no requirement for a second doctor's agreement below this threshold.
> - **Beyond 22 weeks and 6 days ("late-term"):** requires **two medical practitioners** to agree that, in all the circumstances, either: the termination is necessary to save the life of the pregnant person or another fetus; continuation of the pregnancy would involve significant risk of injury to the physical or mental health of the pregnant person; or there is a case, or significant risk, of serious fetal anomalies. In an emergency, the second-practitioner requirement doesn't apply.
> - Early medication abortion (EMA) can be prescribed/administered by a range of registered health practitioners (not only doctors), including via telehealth, and is approved for use up to 9 weeks (63 days) gestation.
> - No minimum SA residency period required.
> - A practitioner with a conscientious objection must disclose this and refer the patient to a health professional who can provide the care — not simply decline.
> - Information about access to counselling (including publicly funded services) must be offered, but counselling itself is not mandatory.

> [!info] Other states/territories — gestational limits (on-request, before a second-practitioner requirement applies) as of Aug 2026; **always verify current legislation directly for the specific jurisdiction, given this is an actively evolving area of law**:
> - **Victoria:** 24 weeks
> - **NSW:** 22 weeks
> - **Queensland:** 22 weeks
> - **Western Australia:** 23 weeks
> - **Tasmania:** 16 weeks
> - **ACT:** no specified gestational limit
> - **Northern Territory:** limits differ by procedure type (medical vs surgical) — check current NT legislation directly
>
> Beyond each state's threshold, the general pattern (not universal) is that two medical practitioners must agree the termination is appropriate on specified grounds (risk to physical/mental health, fetal anomaly) — but the specific grounds, documentation, and any additional requirements (e.g. approved-facility requirements, mandatory considerations) differ by jurisdiction and should be checked directly rather than assumed uniform.

**General points relevant across jurisdictions**
- If patient <16yo presents for TOP: standard principles of consent for minors apply — see [[Clinical-Process-EBM-Consent-Capacity]] Consent and children, where Gillick competence is set out, not repeated here; specific parental involvement requirements vary by state.
- Safe access zones (typically 150m) around clinics, prohibiting protest/harassment, are in place in most jurisdictions.

### Medical/drug options
- Mifepristone followed 48h later by prostaglandin (e.g. misoprostol) — "mimics a miscarriage"; doses depend on gestation
- May be done at home depending on gestation; timing is not predictable
- Requires a multi-level pregnancy test (bHCG) at 2w to confirm pregnancy has ended

### Surgical options
- Vacuum aspiration (MVA), electric vacuum aspiration (EVA), dilation and evacuation (D&E)
- Cervical priming with misoprostol ± mifepristone before procedures
- Various anaesthetic options (LA or GA)
- Intrauterine contraceptive can be inserted immediately following the procedure

### Choice of procedure


| Gestation | Options |
|---|---|
| <9w | Usually medical methods used at home; still 5% chance of needing surgical procedure afterward |
| >9w | Choice between medical and surgical methods |
| >21+6w | Essential that fetus is born dead (unless lethal fetal abnormality) — 3mL intracardiac 15% potassium chloride, confirm asystole with US |

> [!note] If born after 24w, the dead fetus is considered a stillbirth and must be registered. If signs of life, a death certificate is required.

**Before TOP**: offer counselling + support; US to confirm gestation and identify non-viable/ectopic pregnancy; screen for STIs; give antibiotic prophylaxis if surgical option used; discuss contraception; do Rhesus testing and give anti-D prophylaxis if Rh-negative (**250 IU before 13 weeks, 625 IU from 13 weeks** — owned by [[16_01-05_Antenatal_Care]] Use of anti-D).

> [!warning] **The gestational threshold for anti-D after termination is unresolved between the two entries.** This entry said **"if ≥10w"**; the owner entry says give it to **all** having surgical or medical terminations, with no gestational floor. Both positions exist in real guidance — the argument for a floor is that a very early *medical* termination with no instrumentation carries minimal fetomaternal haemorrhage risk — so this is a genuine question rather than a transcription slip. **The threshold has been removed here rather than asserted either way**, because omitting anti-D when it is indicated causes sensitisation that only surfaces in a *later* pregnancy, and is untraceable by then. See `PENDING_GUIDELINE_CHECKS.md` **B62**. Found by the G37 confirmation pass, 2026-08-29.
