## 0.1 The Sexual History and STI Assessment

> [!warning] FLAG 2026-09-01 — the sexual history exists in three files
> Here (the "five Ps" and the normalising script) · `Infectious Disease_merged 08_08 ## The STI Check —
> Sexual History, What to Test, and When` + `### Taking a sexual history` ·
> `MSK_merged NEW_Inv_Ortho §0.18 STI Screening (Asymptomatic Sexual Health Check)`.
> **`O6` has 23 inbound references and only 2 internal** — GI ×6, ID ×5, Neuro ×4, Paediatrics — so this
> is already the de facto owner. **A history-taking framework, flagged for `History-Taking.md`;
> not moved.**

> [!tip] Structure it, normalise it, and do not assume
> **Normalise before asking:** "I ask everyone these questions because they're important for your health — is that alright?" This single sentence changes the encounter.
> **Do not assume the gender of partners, the type of sex, or that a person is or is not sexually active.** Ask.
> **The "five Ps" structure it:**
> **· Partners** — number, gender, and whether they are regular or casual, and any overseas or travel-related contact.
> **· Practices** — **vaginal, anal, oral — receptive and insertive.** This is not prurience: **it determines which sites you swab**, and getting it wrong means missing the infection.
> **· Protection** — condom use, consistently or occasionally, and with whom.
> **· Past history of STIs.**
> **· Prevention of pregnancy** — contraception, and whether pregnancy is desired.
> **Also: symptoms, last test, vaccination status (HPV, hepatitis A and B), injecting drug use, sex work, and — always — whether all sexual contact has been wanted and consensual.**

> [!danger] Site-specific testing — a genital swab misses most extragenital infection
> **Most rectal and pharyngeal chlamydia and gonorrhoea are ASYMPTOMATIC**, and **testing only the urine or the cervix misses them entirely.**
> **Test the sites of exposure**, which is why the "practices" question matters.
> **Self-collected specimens are as sensitive as clinician-collected for chlamydia and gonorrhoea NAAT** — a **self-collected vaginal swab** and a **first-void urine** in men — and acceptability is far higher, which increases uptake.
> **First-void means the first part of the stream, and the patient should not have passed urine for a period beforehand** — instructions matter or the test underperforms.

> [!warning] Confidentiality — and being accurate about its limits
> **Say what confidentiality means, including its limits**, rather than promising absolute secrecy you cannot deliver.
> **Notifiable diseases are notified. Contact tracing occurs. Child protection obligations apply.**
> **For adolescents, confidentiality is central to whether they attend at all** — and capacity to consent is assessed by maturity and understanding rather than age alone. **South Australia also has statutory provisions on the age of consent to medical treatment.** Cross-refer [[A10]] 0.1. **Be clear about what you would need to share and why**, and involve them in that decision where possible.

> [!danger] Contact tracing and notification are a core competency, not someone else's job
> **Chlamydia, gonorrhoea, syphilis, HIV and hepatitis are notifiable.**
> **Partner notification prevents reinfection and onward transmission**, and it can be done by **patient referral** (the person tells their contacts), **provider referral** (the clinician or public health unit does), or **contract referral**.
> **Australian tools exist to make this easier** — anonymous SMS and email notification services — and knowing they exist and offering them is practical and effective. `UNVERIFIED — current services and their names.`
> **Look back periods differ by infection**, and the sexual health service or public health unit will advise.

> [!warning] The Australian epidemiology that changes practice
> **Rates of chlamydia, gonorrhoea and infectious syphilis are substantially higher in some remote Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander communities**, and there has been a **sustained infectious syphilis outbreak across northern and central Australia, with cases of CONGENITAL SYPHILIS** — a condition that should not occur where antenatal screening and treatment are accessible.
> **The practical consequences: a lower testing threshold, opportunistic screening, repeat syphilis screening in pregnancy in high-prevalence settings rather than a single test, and prompt treatment.** Cross-refer [[AU1]] and [[O2]]. `UNVERIFIED — the current outbreak status and the recommended antenatal retesting schedule.`

**Ix:** **Chlamydia and gonorrhoea NAAT from all exposed sites** (*why:* extragenital infection is common and asymptomatic; *what:* positive NAAT). **Gonorrhoea CULTURE where positive or symptomatic, before treatment** (*why:* **NAAT does not give antimicrobial sensitivities, and gonorrhoea resistance is a growing and serious problem — culture is what allows resistance to be tracked and treatment adjusted**; *what:* organism and sensitivities). **Syphilis serology** (*why:* rising incidence, protean presentations, and it is easily treated; *what:* treponemal and non-treponemal results). **HIV testing** (*why:* offer broadly rather than selectively; *what:* fourth-generation assay — cross-refer [[K3]] 0.4). **Hepatitis B serology, and hepatitis C where risk factors** (*why:* vaccination status, chronic infection, and hepatitis C is now curable; *what:* serology). Pregnancy test where relevant (*why:* changes management and drug choice; *what:* positive). **Repeat testing after the window period** (*why:* early infection may not yet be detectable, and a negative test taken too soon gives false reassurance; *what:* seroconversion — `UNVERIFIED — window periods for each infection`).

### 0.1.1 Mx – Immediate
Empirical treatment where the presentation is symptomatic and follow-up is uncertain — **treating presumptively is often correct in sexual health**, since the cost of overtreatment is low and loss to follow-up is common.

### 0.1.2 Mx – Definitive
Pathogen-specific treatment, partner notification, and notification.

### 0.1.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Retesting for reinfection**, which is common. Vaccination (HPV, hepatitis A and B). **Discuss PrEP where there is ongoing HIV risk** — cross-refer [[K3]] 0.2.

---

## 0.2 The Major Sexually Transmissible Infections

> [!tip] Chlamydia
> **The commonest notifiable condition in Australia, and largely asymptomatic** — which is why screening rather than symptom-based testing is the strategy.
> **Presentations: urethritis, cervicitis, pelvic inflammatory disease, epididymo-orchitis, proctitis, conjunctivitis, and reactive arthritis.** **Fitz-Hugh-Curtis syndrome** — perihepatitis causing right upper quadrant pain in a woman with PID — cross-refer [[C1]] 0.3.
> **Consequences of untreated infection in women: PID, tubal factor infertility, chronic pelvic pain and ectopic pregnancy.** That is the reason for the low treatment threshold.
> **Retest for reinfection at around three months** — not test of cure, but because reinfection from an untreated partner is common. `UNVERIFIED — regimens and retesting interval; note that doxycycline has replaced azithromycin as first line in most situations, including rectal infection.`

> [!danger] Gonorrhoea — and antimicrobial resistance
> **Purulent urethral discharge and dysuria in men (usually symptomatic); cervicitis in women (frequently NOT symptomatic); and pharyngeal and rectal infection that is usually asymptomatic.**
> **Disseminated gonococcal infection** — a migratory polyarthralgia, tenosynovitis and pustular skin lesions ("arthritis-dermatitis syndrome"), or a frank septic arthritis. **Consider gonorrhoea in a sexually active young person with a septic-looking joint.** Cross-refer [[L1]].
> **Ophthalmia neonatorum** — hyperacute purulent conjunctivitis in a newborn, which threatens sight.
> **Neisseria gonorrhoeae has developed resistance to successive antibiotic classes and remains a serious global concern.** **This is why culture matters, why the recommended regimen changes, and why the current guideline must be checked rather than recalled.** `UNVERIFIED — regimens, and the indications for test of cure, which are broader than for chlamydia.`

> [!danger] Syphilis — "the great imitator"
> **· Primary — a PAINLESS chancre with an indurated base at the site of inoculation**, healing spontaneously. **Because it is painless and may be internal (cervical, rectal, pharyngeal), it is frequently never noticed.**
> **· Secondary** — weeks to months later: **a widespread maculopapular rash CHARACTERISTICALLY INVOLVING THE PALMS AND SOLES**, condylomata lata, mucous patches, patchy alopecia, generalised lymphadenopathy, fever and malaise. **Highly infectious.**
> **· Latent** — asymptomatic, divided into early and late, which determines treatment duration.
> **· Tertiary** — gummata, **cardiovascular syphilis (aortitis, aortic regurgitation, aneurysm)**, and neurosyphilis.
> **NEUROSYPHILIS, OCULAR AND OTOSYPHILIS CAN OCCUR AT ANY STAGE, INCLUDING EARLY** — this is a common misconception. **Any patient with syphilis and visual symptoms, hearing loss or neurological features needs urgent assessment**, because ocular syphilis causes irreversible blindness and is treated as neurosyphilis.
> **Serology: a treponemal test (which stays positive for life) and a non-treponemal test such as RPR (which reflects disease activity and is used to monitor treatment response).** Interpreting the pair together is what distinguishes active from treated past infection.
> **Treatment is benzathine penicillin**, and **the Jarisch-Herxheimer reaction — fever, chills, myalgia and worsening rash within hours of the first dose — must be warned about**, or the patient believes they are allergic. **In pregnancy it can precipitate contractions and fetal distress**, so treatment in late pregnancy is given with monitoring.
> **Penicillin allergy in a pregnant woman with syphilis is a specific problem — desensitisation is indicated, because no alternative reliably treats the fetus.** Cross-refer [[K4]] 0.3.

> [!tip] Genital herpes — the counselling is the treatment
> **HSV-1 now causes a large and increasing proportion of genital herpes**, transmitted by oral sex, which surprises patients.
> **Primary infection** is more severe — multiple painful ulcers, systemic symptoms, inguinal lymphadenopathy, dysuria and sometimes urinary retention. **Recurrences are milder and shorter.**
> **Diagnosis is by PCR of a swab from the base of a deroofed lesion.** Type-specific serology has limited use.
> **The psychological impact substantially exceeds the medical significance**, and the counselling is the most important part of the consultation:
> **· It is very common. · Most transmission occurs from ASYMPTOMATIC SHEDDING, not from visible lesions, which is why partners may have had it for years unknowingly. · Suppressive antiviral therapy reduces both recurrences and transmission. · It does not affect fertility. · Disclosure to partners is encouraged and can be rehearsed. · It is not a reflection of anything about them.**
> **In pregnancy: the risk of neonatal herpes is highest with a FIRST (primary) infection acquired near term**, because there is no time for maternal antibody transfer. **Established recurrent herpes carries a much lower risk.** Cross-refer [[O3]].

> [!tip] HPV, warts and the Australian program
> **Genital warts are caused by low-risk HPV types; oncogenic types cause cervical, anal, penile and oropharyngeal cancer.**
> **Australia has a school-based HPV vaccination program for all genders, using the nonavalent vaccine, and has moved to a SINGLE-DOSE schedule** — a significant change worth knowing. **Australia is on track to be among the first countries to effectively eliminate cervical cancer as a public health problem**, which is a direct result of the vaccination and screening programs.
> **Cervical screening in Australia is now HPV-based rather than cytology-based, at extended intervals, with SELF-COLLECTION available to all screening participants** — which has substantially improved access for people who decline speculum examination, including those who have experienced sexual violence, and for culturally diverse and Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander women. `UNVERIFIED — the current screening interval, ages and self-collection pathway from the National Cervical Screening Program.`

> [!warning] The others worth knowing
> **· *Mycoplasma genitalium*** — an increasingly recognised cause of non-gonococcal urethritis, cervicitis and PID, with **substantial macrolide resistance**, requiring resistance-guided therapy and **test of cure.** Do not treat empirically without checking current guidance.
> **· *Trichomonas vaginalis*** — frothy malodorous discharge with vulvovaginal irritation, and a "strawberry cervix" classically. **Higher prevalence in remote Australian communities**, and it increases HIV transmission risk.
> **· Pelvic inflammatory disease** — cross-refer [[O5]]. **Treat empirically on a low threshold**, because the diagnostic tests are imperfect and the consequences of missing it are infertility, chronic pain and ectopic pregnancy.
> **· Epididymo-orchitis** — in younger men usually sexually transmitted; in older men usually enteric organisms. **Testicular torsion must be excluded first** — cross-refer [[H4]].
> **· Hepatitis A and B** — vaccine-preventable, with **hepatitis A vaccination recommended for men who have sex with men.**
> **· Lymphogranuloma venereum** — an invasive chlamydia serovar causing severe proctitis in men who have sex with men, requiring longer treatment.

**Ix:** As per 0.1. **PCR of a deroofed ulcer base for HSV, and syphilis PCR or dark-field where available** (*why:* an ulcer needs both tested — herpes and syphilis are the two main causes and they coexist; *what:* HSV type, *T. pallidum*). **Syphilis serology in anyone with any genital ulcer, rash, or unexplained neurological, ocular or auditory symptoms** (*why:* the great imitator; *what:* positive serology). *M. genitalium* NAAT with macrolide resistance testing where available (*why:* directs therapy and resistance is common; *what:* organism, resistance markers).

### 0.2.1 Mx – Immediate
Empirical treatment where symptomatic. **Urgent ophthalmology or neurology assessment for ocular or neurosyphilis.**

### 0.2.2 Mx – Definitive
Pathogen-specific treatment per current guidelines, **partner notification and treatment**, and notification.

### 0.2.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Retesting, vaccination, PrEP discussion, and **serological follow-up after syphilis treatment to confirm response.**

---

## 0.3 Contraception

> [!danger] Lead with the most effective methods, not the most familiar
> **Effectiveness is dominated by whether the method depends on the user:**
> **· Tier 1 — LARC: the etonogestrel implant and intrauterine devices.** **Typical-use failure is essentially the same as perfect-use failure, because there is nothing to remember.** They are **more effective than sterilisation** in the case of the implant.
> **· Tier 2 — injectable, pill, ring, patch.** **The gap between perfect use and typical use is large** — this is where most unintended pregnancies arise.
> **· Tier 3 — condoms, withdrawal, fertility awareness.**
> **LARC should be offered and discussed FIRST**, rather than reached for after the pill has failed. Many people have never had it explained to them.
> **Quote TYPICAL-use failure rates, not perfect-use** — the perfect-use figure describes a person who has never once been late, ill or on holiday.
> `UNVERIFIED — all failure rates and durations of action.`

> [!warning] Combined hormonal contraception — the contraindications that matter
> **Absolute or strong contraindications include:**
> **· MIGRAINE WITH AURA — at any age.** Increased ischaemic stroke risk. **This is the most examined and most clinically important one.** Cross-refer [[D1]] 0.3.
> **· Previous venous thromboembolism, known thrombophilia, or major surgery with prolonged immobilisation.**
> **· Smoking at age 35 or over.**
> **· Uncontrolled hypertension, established ischaemic heart disease, or previous stroke.**
> **· Current breast cancer.**
> **· Severe liver disease.**
> **· Antiphospholipid antibodies, including in SLE.**
> **· Less than 6 weeks postpartum and breastfeeding**, and the early postpartum period generally because of VTE risk.
> `UNVERIFIED — the full UKMEC/WHO categories, which are graded rather than binary and should be consulted directly.`
> **The non-contraceptive benefits are real and worth stating: cycle predictability, reduced dysmenorrhoea and menstrual blood loss, improvement in acne, management of endometriosis and PCOS, and a durable REDUCTION in ovarian and endometrial cancer risk.**

> [!tip] The progestogen-only options
> **· Progestogen-only pill** — **safe in most situations where combined contraception is contraindicated**, including breastfeeding, migraine with aura, VTE history and smoking over 35. **Traditional formulations have a narrow missed-pill window; drospirenone and desogestrel-containing pills have a longer window** — a practically important distinction. Some are now available without prescription in Australia. `UNVERIFIED — the windows and current availability.`
> **· Etonogestrel implant** — highly effective, and **the commonest reason for early removal is unpredictable bleeding.** **Counselling about the bleeding pattern BEFORE insertion substantially improves continuation** — patients who are told to expect it tolerate it; patients who are not, do not.
> **· Depot medroxyprogesterone acetate** — effective and private, but **causes a reduction in bone mineral density (largely recoverable) and a DELAYED return to fertility of up to a year after the last injection**, which must be discussed with anyone who may want to conceive soon.
> **· Levonorgestrel intrauterine system** — contraception plus **treatment of heavy menstrual bleeding, and endometrial protection** (useful in PCOS and with oestrogen therapy). Cross-refer [[O4]] and [[I4]] 0.6.
> **· Copper IUD** — **non-hormonal**, long-acting, and also the most effective emergency contraception. **It typically makes periods heavier and more painful**, which is the main reason for removal.

> [!warning] IUD insertion — take the pain seriously
> **Pain during IUD insertion has been systematically under-acknowledged**, and dismissing it is both a clinical and an ethical failure that deters people from an excellent method.
> **Discuss analgesic options in advance, offer them, and stop if the person asks you to.**
> **Risks to counsel: perforation (rare), expulsion (most common in the first months), and a small increased risk of PID confined to the first few weeks after insertion — not ongoing.** **The old belief that IUDs are unsuitable for nulliparous women or those at risk of STIs is outdated.**

> [!danger] Enzyme inducers reduce contraceptive efficacy — with important exceptions
> **Rifampicin and rifabutin, some antiepileptics (carbamazepine, phenytoin, phenobarbitone, topiramate at higher doses), some antiretrovirals, and St John's wort** induce hepatic enzymes and **reduce the efficacy of combined hormonal contraception, the progestogen-only pill and the implant.**
> **The methods NOT affected: the copper IUD, the hormonal IUD, and depot medroxyprogesterone** — because they do not depend on hepatic first-pass metabolism.
> **This matters most in epilepsy**, where the drugs are often teratogenic and an unplanned pregnancy is exactly what must be avoided. **The counselling is repeatedly missed.** Cross-refer [[D6]] 0.3.

> [!tip] Specific situations
> **· Adolescents** — capacity assessed by maturity; confidentiality; LARC is appropriate and should not be withheld on grounds of age or parity. Cross-refer [[A10]] 0.1 and [[M7]].
> **· Postpartum** — timing depends on breastfeeding and VTE risk; **the implant and progestogen-only methods can be started immediately**, and an IUD can be inserted immediately post-placenta or after a delay.
> **· Perimenopause** — contraception is still needed; the hormonal IUD is particularly useful, providing contraception, bleeding control and endometrial protection alongside oestrogen therapy.
> **· Obesity** — affects the efficacy of some methods and the choice of emergency contraception.
> **· Sterilisation** — **vasectomy is simpler, safer, cheaper and more effective than tubal ligation**, and this comparison is frequently not offered to couples. It requires a post-procedure semen analysis to confirm success.
> **· Cost and access are genuine barriers in Australia**, and the cheapest method the person will actually use beats the theoretically best one they cannot afford.

**Ix:** **Blood pressure and BMI before starting combined hormonal contraception** (*why:* both affect eligibility; *what:* hypertension, BMI). **A structured medical eligibility assessment including migraine with aura, VTE history, smoking and medications** (*why:* these determine safety and are the questions that are skipped; *what:* contraindications). Pregnancy exclusion before starting or inserting (*why:* avoids inserting a device or starting hormones in an established pregnancy; *what:* reasonable certainty of non-pregnancy). STI testing where indicated before IUD insertion (*why:* insertion during untreated infection increases PID risk — **though testing need not delay insertion in asymptomatic low-risk people**; *what:* chlamydia and gonorrhoea).

### 0.3.1 Mx – Immediate
Emergency contraception where indicated — see 0.4.

### 0.3.2 Mx – Definitive
The method the person chooses after being properly informed, with LARC discussed first.

### 0.3.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Review of tolerability and continuation, **a plan for device replacement before expiry**, and reassessment of eligibility as circumstances and health change.

---

## 0.4 Emergency Contraception and Unintended Pregnancy

> [!tip] The copper IUD is the most effective emergency contraception — and it is under-offered
> **· Copper IUD** — **by far the most effective**, usable within a defined window after unprotected sex, **and it provides ongoing long-acting contraception thereafter.** It should be offered to everyone presenting for emergency contraception, and frequently is not.
> **· Ulipristal acetate** — a longer window and **greater efficacy than levonorgestrel, particularly later in the window and at higher body weight.** **It delays the resumption of hormonal contraception**, which must be explained.
> **· Levonorgestrel** — a shorter window with declining efficacy, and **a higher dose is used at higher body weight or with enzyme inducers.**
> `UNVERIFIED — the time windows, doses, weight thresholds and the interval before restarting hormonal contraception.`

> [!warning] Emergency contraceptive pills are not abortifacients
> **They act principally by delaying or preventing ovulation. They do not disrupt an established pregnancy and are not effective if implantation has occurred.**
> **This matters because it is a frequent source of moral distress and misinformation**, both for patients and occasionally for pharmacists and clinicians, and it can be stated plainly.
> **Follow-up: a pregnancy test if the next period is late or unusually light**, and **starting or restarting ongoing contraception**, which is the opportunity that is usually missed in the encounter.

> [!danger] Unintended pregnancy — non-directive options counselling, and it is time-critical
> **The three options are continuing the pregnancy and parenting, continuing and arranging adoption, and termination.** **Present them neutrally.**
> **Time matters** — options narrow with gestation, so **delay caused by referral difficulty or clinician discomfort has real consequences.**
> **In Australia, termination is regulated by state law. In SOUTH AUSTRALIA it was decriminalised by the Termination of Pregnancy Act 2021**, moving it into the health framework, with provisions around gestational limits and practitioner requirements.
> **Medical termination using mifepristone and misoprostol (the MS-2 Step) can be prescribed in general practice by trained prescribers up to a defined gestation; surgical termination is used beyond that or by preference.**
> `UNVERIFIED — the gestational limits, prescriber requirements, and the current SA legal provisions. Verify before advising a patient, and do not rely on knowledge of another state's law.`

> [!warning] Conscientious objection carries obligations
> **A practitioner who conscientiously objects to providing termination is generally required to disclose that objection and to transfer care or refer the person to someone who can help, without delay and without moralising.**
> **The right to object does not extend to obstructing access, withholding information, or delaying.** **And in an emergency, care must be provided regardless.** `UNVERIFIED — the specific obligations under SA law and Medical Board guidance.`
> This is examinable and it is a genuine professional obligation, not a matter of personal preference.

**Ix:** **Pregnancy test with dating** (*why:* determines the available options and the urgency; *what:* gestation). Ultrasound (*why:* confirms intrauterine location and gestation, and **excludes ectopic pregnancy**, which is essential before medical termination; *what:* viability, location, dates). Rhesus status (*why:* anti-D prophylaxis may be indicated; *what:* blood group — `UNVERIFIED — the gestational threshold for anti-D`). STI screening (*why:* an opportunity, and infection increases post-procedural risk; *what:* infection).

### 0.4.1 Mx – Immediate
Emergency contraception within the window, with the copper IUD offered.

### 0.4.2 Mx – Definitive
Support the person's decision, refer promptly, and arrange ongoing contraception.

### 0.4.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Contraception initiated at the time of the encounter, not deferred** — the immediate post-termination or post-emergency-contraception period is when a method is most likely to be started and continued.

---

## 0.5 Sexual Dysfunction

> [!danger] Erectile dysfunction is a cardiovascular marker
> **The penile arteries are small, so endothelial dysfunction and atherosclerosis manifest there BEFORE they cause coronary symptoms — often by several years.**
> **A man presenting with erectile dysfunction should have a cardiovascular risk assessment: blood pressure, lipids, glucose, smoking, weight, and a symptom enquiry.**
> **It is a sentinel event and an opportunity for primary prevention**, and treating it as purely a sexual complaint wastes that. Cross-refer [[B2]] 0.1 and [[GER3]].

> [!tip] Organic versus psychogenic
> **Preserved spontaneous nocturnal and early morning erections, sudden onset, situational variability, and a clear precipitant point to PSYCHOGENIC.**
> **Gradual onset, loss of nocturnal erections, and consistent difficulty in all situations point to ORGANIC.**
> **Most cases have elements of both** — an organic problem generates performance anxiety which compounds it.
> **Causes: vascular (the commonest) · neurogenic (diabetes, spinal, post-prostatectomy) · endocrine (hypogonadism, hyperprolactinaemia — cross-refer [[I4]] 0.2) · DRUGS — antihypertensives (thiazides, beta-blockers), SSRIs, antipsychotics, finasteride, opioids, and alcohol · and psychological.**
> **Review the drug chart** — it is a common and correctable contributor, and men frequently stop antihypertensives silently because of it rather than raising it.
> **PDE5 inhibitors are first-line — and are ABSOLUTELY CONTRAINDICATED WITH NITRATES**, in any form, because of profound hypotension. **Ask about them before giving GTN to a man with chest pain**, and vice versa. Cross-refer [[B1]] 0.1.

> [!tip] Female sexual dysfunction
> Categorised by **desire, arousal, orgasm and pain** — and they interact, so identifying which predominates guides management.
> **Dyspareunia should be characterised as SUPERFICIAL or DEEP:**
> **· Superficial (entry) — vulvovaginal: genitourinary syndrome of menopause and atrophy, vulvodynia, vaginismus, dermatoses (lichen sclerosus), infection, inadequate arousal or lubrication.**
> **· Deep — pelvic: endometriosis, pelvic inflammatory disease, adhesions, ovarian pathology, irritable bowel, interstitial cystitis.**
> **Genitourinary syndrome of menopause is very common, very treatable with topical vaginal oestrogen, and vastly under-treated** — largely because it is not asked about. Cross-refer [[O4]] and [[O5]].
> **Vaginismus** — involuntary pelvic floor contraction, often with a cycle of anticipatory pain and avoidance. Responds to pelvic floor physiotherapy and psychological therapy.

> [!danger] Always ask about coercion and sexual violence
> **Sexual pain, dysfunction and avoidance are common presentations of past or current sexual violence, coercive control or intimate partner violence.**
> **Ask, privately and directly, and always with the partner out of the room.** Cross-refer [[GER4]] and [[K3]] 0.2.
> **Never conduct an examination in this context without explicit, unhurried consent, and stop if asked.**

> [!warning] Antidepressant-associated sexual dysfunction drives non-adherence
> **SSRIs commonly cause reduced libido, delayed or absent orgasm, and erectile difficulty**, and **patients frequently stop the medication without telling anyone rather than raise it.**
> **Ask about it proactively when prescribing and at review.** Options include dose adjustment, switching agent, or adjunctive strategies. Cross-refer [[N4]].

**Ix:** **Cardiovascular risk assessment — blood pressure, lipids, HbA1c, smoking status** (*why:* erectile dysfunction is a marker of vascular disease and this is the main clinical value of the presentation; *what:* risk factors). **Morning total testosterone, with SHBG and LH/FSH if low** (*why:* hypogonadism, and **the sample must be a morning one and confirmed on repeat** — cross-refer [[I4]] 0.6; *what:* low testosterone). Prolactin and TFTs (*why:* hyperprolactinaemia and thyroid disease; *what:* abnormal results). **Medication review** (*why:* a common, reversible cause; *what:* culprit drug). Examination including genital examination (*why:* Peyronie disease, testicular atrophy, phimosis, and in women atrophy, dermatoses and pelvic floor tone; *what:* structural findings). Pelvic ultrasound where deep dyspareunia (*why:* endometriosis, adnexal pathology; *what:* structural cause).

### 0.5.1 Mx – Immediate
Not applicable.

### 0.5.2 Mx – Definitive
Treat the reversible causes and the underlying condition; PDE5 inhibitors; topical oestrogen; pelvic floor physiotherapy; psychological and relationship therapy.

### 0.5.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Cardiovascular risk management in erectile dysfunction — the intervention that matters most in the long run.** Sex therapy referral where psychological or relational factors predominate.

---

## 0.6 Gender Diversity and Puberty

> [!tip] The concepts are distinct, and conflating them causes avoidable offence
> **· Sex assigned at birth** — based on external anatomy at birth.
> **· Gender identity** — a person's internal sense of their own gender.
> **· Gender expression** — how that is outwardly presented.
> **· Sexual orientation** — who a person is attracted to. **Independent of all of the above.**
> **Trans, non-binary and gender diverse people may or may not seek any medical intervention**, and being trans is not in itself a medical condition.

> [!danger] The health disparity is driven by discrimination, not by gender identity
> **Trans and gender diverse people in Australia experience markedly elevated rates of psychological distress, self-harm and suicidality, and high rates of discrimination and avoidance of healthcare.**
> **The evidence indicates this reflects minority stress — discrimination, rejection, violence and barriers to care — rather than anything intrinsic**, and that **gender-affirming care and social acceptance substantially improve mental health outcomes.**
> **What this means practically is that how you conduct the consultation is itself a health intervention.**

> [!tip] The practical competencies an intern needs
> **· Use the person's stated name and pronouns, and record them.** **If you get it wrong, correct yourself briefly and move on** — an extended apology makes it about you.
> **· Ask rather than assume**: "what name and pronouns would you like me to use?"
> **· Take an ORGAN INVENTORY rather than assuming anatomy from gender.** **This is the practical crux of safe care.** **A trans man who has not had a hysterectomy still needs cervical screening. A trans woman on oestrogen therapy may need breast screening. A trans man can become pregnant.** **Screening and differential diagnosis should follow the organs present, not the gender recorded.**
> **· Ask only the questions relevant to the presentation.** A person attending with an ankle injury does not need a detailed gender history, and being asked for one repeatedly is a documented reason people avoid care.
> **· Examine with explicit consent and explanation**, recognising that examination may be distressing.
> **· Do not stop gender-affirming hormone therapy on admission** without a specific clinical reason and discussion — abrupt cessation causes physical and psychological harm.
> **· Fertility preservation should be discussed BEFORE starting hormone therapy or surgery**, because some effects on fertility are irreversible.
> `UNVERIFIED — gender-affirming hormone regimens, monitoring, and the relevant Australian standards of care, which are specialist-led and evolving.`

> [!tip] Puberty — the normal and the abnormal
> **Normal puberty follows a predictable sequence** — in girls, breast development, then pubic hair, then growth spurt, then menarche; in boys, testicular enlargement first, then pubic hair, then growth spurt and voice change. **Staged with the Tanner system.** `UNVERIFIED — the age ranges and staging descriptors.`
> **PRECOCIOUS puberty** — onset before the defined age threshold.
> **· Central (gonadotrophin-dependent)** — premature activation of the axis. **More often idiopathic in girls; more often pathological in BOYS, so a boy with precocious puberty requires imaging of the CNS.**
> **· Peripheral (gonadotrophin-independent)** — gonadal or adrenal source, including **congenital adrenal hyperplasia**, tumours, and McCune-Albright syndrome.
> **· Concerns: accelerated bone maturation causing PREMATURE EPIPHYSEAL FUSION and reduced final adult height, and psychosocial impact.**
> **DELAYED puberty** — absence of onset by the defined age.
> **· Constitutional delay of growth and puberty is the commonest cause, is benign, and typically has a family history** — but it is a diagnosis of exclusion.
> **· Exclude: chronic disease (coeliac disease, inflammatory bowel disease, chronic kidney disease), UNDERNUTRITION AND EATING DISORDERS, excessive exercise, hypothyroidism, hyperprolactinaemia, Turner syndrome in girls, Klinefelter syndrome in boys, and Kallmann syndrome (hypogonadotrophic hypogonadism WITH ANOSMIA — ask about the sense of smell, it is a free and specific clue).**
> Cross-refer [[M4]] and [[M7]].

> [!warning] Confidentiality and capacity in adolescents
> **Adolescents may consent to treatment where they have sufficient maturity and understanding**, and **confidentiality is often the determining factor in whether they seek care at all.**
> **Be explicit about what is confidential and what is not**, encourage involvement of a trusted adult without requiring it, and be alert to the safeguarding situations where confidentiality cannot be maintained. Cross-refer [[A10]] 0.1, [[M7]] and [[GER4]].

**Ix:** **Organ-based rather than gender-based screening** (*why:* the whole point of the organ inventory; *what:* cervical screening, breast screening, prostate consideration as anatomically appropriate). For precocious or delayed puberty: **bone age radiograph** (*why:* compares skeletal to chronological age, distinguishing constitutional delay from pathology and assessing height potential; *what:* advanced or delayed bone age). **LH, FSH and sex steroids** (*why:* **distinguishes central from peripheral, and hypogonadotrophic from hypergonadotrophic hypogonadism — the key branch point**; *what:* the gonadotrophin pattern). **Karyotype** where indicated (*why:* Turner and Klinefelter syndromes; *what:* chromosomal abnormality). **MRI brain and pituitary** (*why:* central precocious puberty in boys, and hypogonadotrophic hypogonadism, may reflect an intracranial lesion; *what:* lesion). Coeliac serology, FBC, ESR, UEC, TFTs, prolactin (*why:* chronic disease as a cause of delay; *what:* underlying condition). **Growth chart plotting over time** (*why:* the trajectory is more informative than any single measurement; *what:* growth velocity, centile crossing).

### 0.6.1 Mx – Immediate
Not applicable.

### 0.6.2 Mx – Definitive
Specialist paediatric endocrine referral for precocious or pathological delayed puberty. Gender-affirming care through appropriate specialist services.

### 0.6.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Psychological support and, where relevant, connection with peer and community support**, which has good evidence for improving outcomes in gender diverse young people. Ongoing screening based on the organ inventory.

---

> HIV, PrEP and post-exposure prophylaxis → [[K3]] 0.2, 0.4 · Sexual assault pathway → [[K3]] 0.2 and [[GER4]] · Pelvic inflammatory disease and vulval symptoms → [[O5]] · Menstrual disorders, menopause and endometrial protection → [[O4]] · Pregnancy, antenatal screening and congenital infection → [[O2]] · Postpartum and neonatal herpes → [[O3]] · Acute scrotum and epididymo-orchitis → [[H4]] · Migraine with aura and contraception → [[D1]] 0.3 · Enzyme-inducing antiepileptics → [[D6]] 0.3 · Erectile dysfunction, nitrates and cardiovascular risk → [[B1]] 0.1 and [[B2]] 0.1 · Hypogonadism, hyperprolactinaemia and PCOS → [[I4]] 0.2, 0.6 · Antidepressant sexual side effects → [[N4]] · Penicillin allergy and desensitisation → [[K4]] 0.3 · Septic arthritis → [[L1]] · Capacity and consent in minors → [[A10]] 0.1 · Adolescent health → [[M7]] · Puberty and growth → [[M4]] · Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander health → [[AU1]] · Preventive health, screening and immunisation → [[GER3]]

> [!warning] Sourcing
> Written from model knowledge, not retrieved from guidelines. Clinical reasoning, discriminators and management principles are reliable at intern level. **Every age threshold, screening interval, dose and regimen carries an `UNVERIFIED` marker naming what to check, or has been omitted with the omission stated in place.** Verify against **BreastScreen Australia**, **Cancer Australia**, **RANZCOG**, eTG, and — for lactation — the **Australian Breastfeeding Association** and specialist medicines-in-lactation services.

---

## 0.1 Triple Assessment

> [!danger] Triple assessment means all three components, and they must be CONCORDANT
> **1. CLINICAL assessment — history and examination.**
> **2. IMAGING — ultrasound and/or mammography, chosen by age.**
> **3. PATHOLOGY — core biopsy.**
> **The critical principle is CONCORDANCE. If any one component is suspicious and the others are not, the case is NOT closed.**
> **A benign biopsy result in a clinically or radiologically suspicious lump means the biopsy may have missed the lesion — it does not mean the lump is benign.** **Escalate: repeat sampling, excision biopsy, or multidisciplinary discussion.**
> **Discordance is the mechanism by which cancers are missed after a "normal" biopsy**, and recognising it is the single most important safety concept in breast assessment.

> [!tip] Imaging is chosen by age, because breast density changes
> **· Younger women (broadly under 35–40) — ULTRASOUND first.** Dense glandular tissue makes mammography insensitive, and the yield of malignancy is low.
> **· Older women — MAMMOGRAPHY plus ULTRASOUND.**
> **· MRI — for high-risk screening (BRCA carriers, chest irradiation), assessing disease extent particularly in LOBULAR carcinoma, and for implant assessment.**
> `UNVERIFIED — the age thresholds.`
> **CORE BIOPSY is preferred over fine needle aspiration**, because it provides tissue architecture (distinguishing invasive from in situ disease), grade, and **receptor status (ER, PR, HER2)** — all of which determine treatment. **FNA retains a role for cyst aspiration and for lymph node sampling.**

> [!warning] Take the family history properly — it changes the pathway
> **The features that suggest a hereditary predisposition:**
> **· Multiple affected relatives on the SAME side of the family.**
> **· YOUNG age at diagnosis (particularly under 50).**
> **· BILATERAL breast cancer.**
> **· MALE breast cancer — a strong indicator, particularly of BRCA2.**
> **· OVARIAN cancer in the family, and breast plus ovarian in the same person or family.**
> **· Ashkenazi Jewish ancestry.**
> **· Known familial mutation.**
> **These warrant referral to a familial cancer service**, because the implications extend to surveillance, risk-reducing surgery, treatment choice (PARP inhibitors) and cascade testing of relatives.

> [!tip] Risk factors — dominated by age and oestrogen exposure
> **· AGE — the dominant risk factor by a wide margin.**
> **· Family history and germline mutations (BRCA1, BRCA2, PALB2 and others).**
> **· Lifetime oestrogen exposure — early menarche, late menopause, nulliparity, later first pregnancy, and menopausal hormone therapy.**
> **· Previous breast biopsy showing ATYPIA or lobular carcinoma in situ.**
> **· Previous CHEST RADIOTHERAPY, particularly for lymphoma in adolescence — these women need early, intensive surveillance and it is frequently overlooked.**
> **· Dense breast tissue · ALCOHOL · obesity after menopause · physical inactivity.**
> **Protective: parity, BREASTFEEDING, and physical activity.**

---

## 0.2 Breast Lump

> [!danger] Every discrete new breast lump requires triple assessment
> **Age, a benign-feeling lump, and an absent family history are not sufficient grounds for reassurance without assessment.**
> **The one-line rule: if you can feel a discrete lump, it gets assessed** — the pathway differs by age, but the assessment happens.

> [!tip] The common causes and how they feel
> **· FIBROADENOMA — younger women. Smooth, firm, well-defined, highly MOBILE ("breast mouse").** Usually observed once triple assessment confirms it; excised if large, growing or symptomatic.
> **· CYST — often fluctuates with the cycle, may be tender, smooth and sometimes tense.** **Aspiration is both diagnostic and therapeutic where symptomatic.** **Two findings after aspiration mandate further assessment: BLOODSTAINED aspirate, and a RESIDUAL MASS after complete aspiration.**
> **· CARCINOMA — hard, irregular, poorly defined, fixed to skin or chest wall, with skin dimpling or tethering, nipple retraction, or palpable axillary nodes.** **But early cancers can feel unremarkable**, which is why the imaging and biopsy are not optional.
> **· FAT NECROSIS — after trauma, surgery or radiotherapy. It can mimic carcinoma clinically AND radiologically**, so it usually still requires biopsy.
> **· PHYLLODES TUMOUR — rapidly growing, often large, resembling a fibroadenoma but enlarging quickly. It can be borderline or malignant and requires wide excision.**
> **· Lipoma, galactocele, abscess, and duct ectasia.**

> [!danger] Male breast lump — gynaecomastia versus carcinoma
> **GYNAECOMASTIA is CONCENTRIC, rubbery, symmetrically arranged around and beneath the nipple, often bilateral and often tender.**
> **CARCINOMA is ECCENTRIC (off to one side of the nipple), hard, fixed, painless, and may have skin or nipple change and axillary nodes.**
> **Male breast cancer exists, presents late because neither the patient nor the clinician expects it, and is associated with BRCA2** — which has implications for the man's relatives.
> **An eccentric hard male breast lump gets triple assessment, not reassurance.**

> [!tip] Gynaecomastia — look for a cause
> **Physiological: neonatal, pubertal (common, usually resolves), and in older men.**
> **DRUGS — the commonest identifiable cause and the most easily reversed: SPIRONOLACTONE · digoxin · cimetidine · antipsychotics (via prolactin) · antiretrovirals · anti-androgens and androgen deprivation therapy · finasteride · anabolic steroids · CANNABIS · and alcohol.**
> **Systemic: chronic liver disease · hypogonadism (primary or secondary) · hyperthyroidism · chronic kidney disease · refeeding after starvation.**
> **Tumours: hCG-secreting germ cell tumours (TESTICULAR — examine the testes) and adrenal or Leydig cell tumours.**
> **Rapid onset, pain, a large size, or a young man should prompt a search for a hormone-secreting tumour** — check hCG, and examine the testes. Cross-refer [[I4]] 0.6 and [[H4]].

**Ix:** **Triple assessment** as per 0.1 (*why:* the standard, and concordance is what makes it safe; *what:* clinical, imaging and pathological findings). **Aspiration of a symptomatic cyst** (*why:* diagnostic and therapeutic, and the findings determine next steps; *what:* fluid character, residual mass). **In male gynaecomastia: LFT, UEC, TFTs, testosterone, LH, FSH, oestradiol, prolactin, and βhCG, with testicular examination and ultrasound if indicated** (*why:* identifies the hormonal or systemic cause and excludes a secreting tumour; *what:* the underlying cause). **Medication review** (*why:* drugs are the commonest reversible cause of gynaecomastia; *what:* culprit drug).

---

## 0.3 Breast Pain

> [!tip] Cyclical versus non-cyclical
> **CYCLICAL — bilateral, diffuse, heaviest in the upper outer quadrants, worst in the days before menstruation and relieved by its onset.** Common, hormonally driven, and benign.
> **NON-CYCLICAL — often unilateral, localised, constant, and unrelated to the cycle.** Causes include **chest wall pain (costochondritis, Tietze syndrome), large or unsupported breasts, previous surgery or trauma, medication (hormone therapy, hormonal contraception, some antidepressants), mastitis, and referred pain — including CARDIAC and pleuritic pain, which should be considered before the breast is blamed.**

> [!danger] Breast pain alone is rarely a symptom of cancer
> **This is a genuinely reassuring and useful fact, and it should be given** — most women presenting with breast pain are frightened of cancer even when they do not say so.
> **But: examine both breasts and the axillae, and arrange imaging if the pain is FOCAL and PERSISTENT, if there is any associated lump or skin change, or if the woman's age warrants it.**
> **Reassurance after a proper assessment is treatment. Reassurance instead of an assessment is not.**

> [!tip] Chest wall pain is frequently mislabelled as breast pain
> **Localised, reproducible tenderness over the costochondral junctions or chest wall musculature — and it is confirmed by rolling the patient onto her side so the breast tissue falls away from the chest wall: if the tenderness follows the chest wall rather than the breast, it is musculoskeletal.**
> **This simple manoeuvre distinguishes them and permits confident, specific reassurance.**

> [!warning] Management is mostly non-pharmacological
> **· A well-fitted, supportive bra — including a sports bra for exercise and sometimes a soft bra at night.** **Poor bra fit is common and is a genuinely under-recognised cause**, and referral for professional fitting helps a substantial proportion.
> **· Simple analgesia and topical NSAIDs.**
> **· Review hormonal contraception and hormone therapy**, which may be contributing.
> **· A pain and cycle diary** to establish whether it is cyclical.
> **· Evidence for evening primrose oil is weak**, though it is widely used and harmless.
> **· Specific pharmacotherapy (danazol, tamoxifen) is reserved for severe refractory pain** and carries significant adverse effects.
> `UNVERIFIED — the evidence and dosing for pharmacological options.`

**Ix:** **Examination of both breasts, axillae and the chest wall, including the side-lying manoeuvre** (*why:* distinguishes breast from chest wall pain, and identifies an associated mass; *what:* focal tenderness, mass). Imaging **where the pain is focal, persistent, or associated with other findings, and by age-appropriate criteria** (*why:* excludes underlying pathology and allows definitive reassurance; *what:* normal or abnormal imaging). Consider cardiac and pleuritic causes (*why:* referred pain presents as breast pain, particularly on the left; *what:* ECG, other findings as indicated).

---

## 0.4 Nipple Discharge and Nipple Changes

> [!danger] The features that make discharge concerning
> **· SPONTANEOUS (staining clothing or bedding) rather than only expressed.**
> **· UNILATERAL.**
> **· SINGLE DUCT.**
> **· BLOODSTAINED or serosanguinous.**
> **· Associated with a MASS.**
> **· Older age.**
> **Reassuring by contrast: bilateral, multiple ducts, expressed only on squeezing, milky, green, yellow or brown, in a younger woman.**
> **The concerning pattern warrants triple assessment.**

> [!tip] The causes
> **· DUCT ECTASIA — older women, green, brown or creamy discharge from MULTIPLE ducts, often bilateral, sometimes with nipple retraction. Benign.**
> **· INTRADUCTAL PAPILLOMA — the commonest cause of SINGLE-DUCT BLOODSTAINED discharge. Benign, but requires excision to exclude malignancy and because it may harbour atypia.**
> **· CARCINOMA and DCIS — can present with bloodstained single-duct discharge.**
> **· GALACTORRHOEA — bilateral milky discharge NOT related to lactation. This is an ENDOCRINE problem, not a breast problem: check a PREGNANCY TEST, PROLACTIN, TFTs, and the MEDICATION LIST (antipsychotics, metoclopramide, SSRIs, opioids).** Cross-refer [[I4]] 0.2.
> **· PERIDUCTAL MASTITIS — strongly associated with SMOKING**, with subareolar inflammation, discharge, and sometimes a mammary duct fistula.

> [!danger] Paget disease of the nipple — biopsy persistent nipple eczema
> **An eczematous, scaly, weeping or ulcerated change of the NIPPLE, usually spreading onto the areola, and it is associated with an underlying breast malignancy in the majority of cases.**
> **The discriminator from ordinary eczema: ECZEMA typically affects the AREOLA and SPARES the nipple, is usually BILATERAL, and RESPONDS to topical steroid. PAGET DISEASE starts at the NIPPLE, is UNILATERAL, and does NOT respond.**
> **Any persistent unilateral nipple change that has not resolved with a short course of appropriate topical treatment must be BIOPSIED.**
> **It is routinely treated as dermatitis for months, which is the mechanism of delayed diagnosis.**

> [!warning] The other nipple and skin changes
> **· NEW nipple INVERSION or retraction, particularly unilateral — concerning. Longstanding, symmetrical inversion is usually benign.**
> **· SKIN DIMPLING or TETHERING — caused by tumour involving Cooper ligaments. Look for it with the arms raised and with the hands pressed on the hips, which accentuates it.**
> **· PEAU D'ORANGE — dermal lymphatic obstruction producing skin oedema with accentuated pores.**
> **· INFLAMMATORY BREAST CANCER — a rapidly developing red, swollen, warm, tender breast with peau d'orange, WITHOUT the fever and systemic illness of infection, and NOT responding to antibiotics.** **Any "mastitis" that does not respond promptly to antibiotics, particularly in a woman who is not breastfeeding, must be assessed for inflammatory breast cancer.** See 0.6.

**Ix:** **Triple assessment for concerning discharge** (*why:* excludes malignancy; *what:* imaging and histology). **Testing discharge for blood** (*why:* frank or occult blood raises concern; *what:* blood). **Prolactin, pregnancy test, TFTs and medication review for bilateral milky discharge** (*why:* galactorrhoea is endocrine and needs a different workup entirely; *what:* hyperprolactinaemia, pregnancy, hypothyroidism, drug cause). **BIOPSY of persistent nipple skin change** (*why:* Paget disease; *what:* histology). Ductography or MRI in selected cases (*why:* localises an intraductal lesion; *what:* filling defect).

---

## 0.5 Breast Cancer

> [!tip] The types and what they mean practically
> **· DCIS (ductal carcinoma in situ) — pre-invasive, confined within the duct.** Frequently detected by screening as microcalcification. Treated to prevent progression to invasive disease.
> **· INVASIVE DUCTAL CARCINOMA (no special type) — the commonest.**
> **· INVASIVE LOBULAR CARCINOMA — grows in single-file strands rather than forming a discrete mass, so it is HARDER TO FEEL and HARDER TO SEE on mammography, is more often detected late, has a greater tendency to be bilateral and multifocal, and is the type for which MRI is most often used to assess extent.** **A vague area of thickening rather than a discrete lump is a characteristic presentation.**
> **· Receptor status — ER, PR and HER2 — is determined on the biopsy and drives treatment. "Triple negative" disease lacks all three, is more common in younger women and in BRCA1 carriers, and has fewer targeted options.**

> [!info] Treatment principles worth knowing at intern level
> **· SURGERY — breast-conserving surgery with radiotherapy has EQUIVALENT SURVIVAL to mastectomy for suitable tumours**, which is an important thing to be able to say to a frightened patient.
> **· SENTINEL LYMPH NODE BIOPSY has replaced routine axillary clearance** in clinically node-negative disease, substantially reducing lymphoedema.
> **· NEOADJUVANT (pre-operative) systemic therapy is increasingly used** — to downstage tumours, permit conservation, and assess response in vivo.
> **· ENDOCRINE THERAPY for hormone receptor-positive disease — TAMOXIFEN and AROMATASE INHIBITORS**, taken for years, and see the adverse effects below.
> **· ANTI-HER2 therapy** for HER2-positive disease.
> **· Radiotherapy and chemotherapy** per protocol and multidisciplinary decision.

> [!danger] The complications an intern actually encounters
> **· NEUTROPENIC SEPSIS during chemotherapy — a medical emergency requiring antibiotics within a short defined window.** Cross-refer [[K1]] 0.5.
> **· ANTHRACYCLINE AND TRASTUZUMAB CARDIOTOXICITY — left ventricular dysfunction, monitored with serial echocardiography.** **A breast cancer patient with new breathlessness needs the heart considered, not just the lungs.** Cross-refer [[CV-X]] 0.1.
> **· TAMOXIFEN — increased VTE risk, and increased ENDOMETRIAL HYPERPLASIA AND CARCINOMA risk. Any abnormal vaginal bleeding in a woman on tamoxifen requires endometrial assessment.** Cross-refer [[O4]] 0.3. It also causes hot flushes and is associated with cataract.
> **· AROMATASE INHIBITORS — accelerated BONE LOSS requiring bone density monitoring and often bisphosphonate therapy, plus prominent ARTHRALGIA which is a common reason for non-adherence** and should be asked about and managed rather than left to cause silent discontinuation. Cross-refer [[GER1]] 0.6.
> **· LYMPHOEDEMA after axillary surgery or radiotherapy** — cross-refer [[B6]] 0.3. **Conventional practice avoids venepuncture, cannulation and blood pressure measurement in the affected arm, although the evidence for this is now less certain than the strength of the convention suggests.** **In an emergency, do not withhold necessary vascular access from a patient because of this.**
> **· FERTILITY — discuss and refer for fertility preservation BEFORE starting gonadotoxic treatment.** This is time-critical and is a well-documented gap, particularly for younger women who are focused on survival at the point of diagnosis.
> **· Menopausal symptoms induced by treatment — and vaginal oestrogen can often be used, in discussion with the oncologist**, rather than leaving women with severe genitourinary symptoms untreated. Cross-refer [[O4]] 0.6.

> [!warning] BreastScreen Australia — and the screening/diagnostic distinction
> **BreastScreen Australia provides free screening mammography to asymptomatic women within a defined target age range, at a defined interval.** `UNVERIFIED — the target age range, the invited range, and the interval.`
> **THE CRITICAL POINT: SCREENING IS FOR ASYMPTOMATIC WOMEN.**
> **A woman with a lump, nipple discharge, skin change or any breast symptom must be referred for DIAGNOSTIC assessment — not sent to BreastScreen.**
> **Sending a symptomatic woman for screening delays her diagnosis**, and it happens.
> **Also: a normal recent screening mammogram does NOT exclude cancer in a woman with a new symptom** — interval cancers occur, and lobular cancers in particular are mammographically occult.
> **High-risk women (BRCA carriers, previous chest radiotherapy, strong family history) require a different, more intensive surveillance program including MRI**, arranged through a familial cancer or breast service rather than through population screening.

> [!tip] Metastatic disease — where it goes
> **BONE is the commonest site** — presenting with pain, pathological fracture, **HYPERCALCAEMIA** (cross-refer [[I3]] 0.2) and **METASTATIC SPINAL CORD COMPRESSION** (cross-refer [[L4]] 0.3).
> **Also liver, lung and pleura, and BRAIN.**
> **New back pain in a woman with a history of breast cancer is metastatic disease until proven otherwise**, and it precedes neurological signs by weeks — which is the window.

**Ix:** **Triple assessment** (*why:* the diagnostic standard; *what:* concordant findings). Receptor and HER2 status on the biopsy (*why:* determines systemic therapy; *what:* ER, PR, HER2). Staging investigations per protocol (*why:* determines treatment intent; *what:* nodal and distant disease). **Baseline echocardiography before anthracyclines or trastuzumab** (*why:* cardiotoxicity monitoring; *what:* ejection fraction). **Bone density before and during aromatase inhibitor therapy** (*why:* accelerated bone loss; *what:* reduced density). **Genetic assessment where family history, young age, triple negative or male breast cancer** (*why:* changes treatment and has family implications; *what:* pathogenic variant). **Fertility referral before gonadotoxic therapy** (*why:* time-critical and frequently missed; *what:* preservation options).

---

## 0.6 Breast Infection and Lactation

> [!danger] Continue breastfeeding through mastitis — it is part of the treatment
> **Lactational mastitis is usually caused by inadequate milk removal leading to inflammation, with or without infection (*Staphylococcus aureus* most commonly).**
> **EFFECTIVE MILK REMOVAL IS THE PRIMARY TREATMENT.** **Continuing to breastfeed or express from the affected breast is safe for the baby and essential for resolution — stopping makes it worse and risks abscess formation.**
> **Women are frequently and wrongly advised to stop feeding**, and this advice causes harm.
> **Management: continue feeding, correct attachment and positioning, analgesia and anti-inflammatories, rest and fluids, and ANTIBIOTICS where there are systemic features, a nipple fissure, or no improvement within about 12–24 hours of improved milk removal.** `UNVERIFIED — antibiotic choice and duration, and the current guidance on management, which has been revised.`

> [!warning] Breast abscess — aspirate rather than incise
> **Ultrasound-guided needle aspiration, repeated if necessary, is preferred over incision and drainage for most breast abscesses** — better cosmesis, less disruption, and **breastfeeding can usually continue.**
> Incision and drainage is reserved for large or non-resolving collections.

> [!danger] Inflammatory breast cancer masquerades as mastitis
> **A red, swollen, warm breast with peau d'orange — WITHOUT fever or systemic illness, and NOT responding to antibiotics.**
> **Any presumed mastitis that has not clearly improved on appropriate antibiotics, and any inflammatory breast presentation in a woman who is NOT breastfeeding, requires urgent imaging and biopsy.**
> **The delay caused by successive antibiotic courses is the classic mechanism of late diagnosis in this aggressive cancer.**

> [!tip] Non-lactational and periductal mastitis
> **Periductal mastitis is STRONGLY associated with SMOKING** — recurrent subareolar inflammation, abscess and sometimes a mammary duct fistula, often with anaerobic organisms.
> **Smoking cessation is a core part of management**, and recurrence is common without it.

> [!warning] Lactation problems — most nipple pain is an attachment problem
> **· NIPPLE PAIN AND TRAUMA — the commonest cause by far is poor ATTACHMENT and POSITIONING**, and the treatment is skilled observation of a feed by a lactation consultant or midwife, not creams. **Observing a feed is the assessment.**
> **· ENGORGEMENT — in the early days; frequent effective feeding, cold packs between feeds, and expressing enough for comfort.**
> **· BLOCKED DUCT — a tender lump; continued feeding, positioning changes and gentle measures.**
> **· LOW SUPPLY versus PERCEIVED low supply — the latter is far commoner. The objective markers are wet nappies and weight gain**, not the feel of the breasts or the volume expressed.
> **· NIPPLE CANDIDIASIS — burning, radiating nipple pain persisting after feeds, often after antibiotics; treat mother and baby simultaneously.**
> **· RAYNAUD PHENOMENON OF THE NIPPLE — blanching then colour change with severe pain after a feed, triggered by cold. Frequently misdiagnosed as thrush.**
> **· Tongue-tie — a contested area; assess for functional feeding impact rather than appearance alone.**

> [!tip] Two practical supports and one common error
> **· AUSTRALIAN BREASTFEEDING ASSOCIATION, lactation consultants (IBCLC), midwives and child health nurses** — refer early rather than after weeks of struggle.
> **· MEDICATION IN BREASTFEEDING — most medicines are compatible with breastfeeding, and the reflex advice to "stop breastfeeding" or to withhold necessary treatment is usually wrong and causes real harm.** **Consult a specialist medicines-in-pregnancy-and-lactation information service rather than deciding from a product information leaflet, which is written defensively.** `UNVERIFIED — the relevant Australian services.`
> **· Screen for postnatal depression at these contacts** — breastfeeding difficulty and postnatal mental health are closely linked in both directions. Cross-refer [[O3]].

**Ix:** **Observation of a breastfeed** (*why:* the diagnostic test for most lactation problems, and it cannot be done by history alone; *what:* attachment, positioning, transfer). **Ultrasound where an abscess is suspected** (*why:* confirms a drainable collection and guides aspiration; *what:* collection). Milk or swab culture where infection is not responding (*why:* directs antibiotics, including resistant organisms; *what:* organism). **Urgent imaging and BIOPSY for any inflammatory breast presentation not responding to antibiotics** (*why:* inflammatory breast cancer; *what:* malignancy). Infant weight and output assessment (*why:* the objective measure of milk transfer; *what:* weight gain, wet nappies).
